Asymptotic Analysis of Boundary Layer Solutions to Poisson–Boltzmann Type Equations in General Bounded Smooth Domains
Jhih-Hong Lyu, Tai-Chia Lin

Abstract
We study the boundary layer solution to singular perturbation problems involving Poisson–Boltzmann (PB) type equations with a small parameter ϵ \epsilon in general bounded smooth domains (including multiply connected domains) under the Robin boundary condition. The PB type equations include the classical PB, modified PB and charge-conserving PB (CCPB) equations, which are mathematical models for the electric potential and ion distributions. The CCPB equations present particular analytical challenges due to their nonlocal nonlinearity introduced through integral terms enforcing charge conservation. Using the principal coordinate system, exponential-type estimates and the moving plane agruments, we rigorously prove asymptotic expansions of boundary layer solutions throughout the whole domain. The solution domain is partitioned into three characteristic regions based on the distance from the boundary: • Region I, where the distance from the boundary is at most T ​ ϵ T\sqrt{\epsilon} , • Region II, where the distance ranges between T ​ ϵ T\sqrt{\epsilon} and ϵ β \epsilon^{\beta} , • Region III, where the distance is at least ϵ β \epsilon^{\beta} , for given parameters T > 0 T>0 and 0 < β < 1 / 2 0<\beta<1/2 . In Region I, we derive second-order asymptotic formulas explicitly incorporating the effects of boundary mean curvature, while exponential decay estimates are established for Regions II and III. Furthermore, we obtain asymptotic expansions for key physical quantities, including the electric potential, electric field, total ionic charge density and total ionic charge, revealing how domain geometry regulates electrostatic interactions. Key words. boundary layer solutions, PB type equations, Robin boundary condition, mean curvature effect AMS subject classifications. 35B25, 35C20, 35J60



1
Introduction

When a charged surface (e.g., electrode, membrane, colloid) is in contact with an electrolyte, a structured layer of charge known as the electric double layer (EDL) forms.
The EDL plays a crucial role in various physical, engineering, and biological systems (cf.
[
14
,
17
,
42
]
).
It typically consists of two distinct regions:

•

Stern Layer — The region closest to the surface, where ions are strongly adsorbed, governed by the surface charge density;

•

Diffuse Layer — A region extending into the electrolyte, where ion distributions are influenced by electrostatic interactions and thermal fluctuations.

The behavior of the diffuse layer can be described by different types of Poisson–Boltzmann (PB) equations, including:

•

The classical PB equation, which models charge screening in electrolytes (cf.
[
8
,
20
,
31
]
);

•

The modified PB equation, which incorporates ion size effects and steric interactions (cf.
[
7
,
9
,
28
,
35
,
36
,
38
,
39
]
);

•

The charge-conserving PB equation, which enforces global charge neutrality (cf.
[
21
,
33
,
34
,
44
,
47
]
).

To analyze the structure of the diffuse layer, we study boundary layer solutions to PB-type equations in general bounded smooth domains (including multiply connected domains). This analysis provides key insights into electrostatic interactions in complex geometries, with applications in electrochemistry, biophysics, and materials science (cf.
[
3
,
4
,
11
,
13
,
15
,
16
,
30
,
48
,
49
]
).

The classical PB and modified PB equations can be represented by

\displaystyle-\epsilon\Delta\phi_{\epsilon}=f(\phi_{\epsilon})\quad\text{in}~\Omega,

(1.1)

where
\epsilon>0
is the dielectric constant and
\phi_{\epsilon}
is the electric potential.
Here
f=f(\phi)
is a smooth function for the total ionic charge density which satisfies the following conditions.

(A1)

The function
f=f(\phi)
is smooth, strictly decreasing in
\phi
, and satisfies

m_{f}=m_{f}(\mathcal{K}):=\sqrt{-\max_{\phi\in\mathcal{K}}f^{\prime}(\phi)}>0\quad\text{for any compact interval $\mathcal{K}\subseteq\mathbb{R}$.}

(A2)

The function
f
has a unique zero point
\phi^{*}
, i.e.,
f(\phi^{*})=0
, where
\phi^{*}
is called as reference potential (cf.
[
2
,
26
]
).

For example, function
f(\phi)=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{I}z_{i}c_{i}^{\text{b}}\exp(-z_{i}\phi)
describes the total ionic charge density of the classical PB equation, and satisfies conditions (A1) and (A2), where
I
is the number of ion species,
z_{i}\neq 0
is the valence and
c_{i}^{\text{b}}
is the concentration of the
i
th species in the bulk (cf.
[
20
,
31
]
).

The charge-conserving PB (CCPB) equation was derived from the static limit of the Poisson–Nernst–Planck equations in order to guarantee charge neutrality within electrolyte domains (cf.
[
47
]
).
The CCPB equation can be denoted as

\displaystyle-\epsilon\Delta\phi_{\epsilon}=\sum_{i=1}^{I}\frac{m_{i}z_{i}\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{\epsilon})}{\int_{\Omega}\!\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{\epsilon}(y))\,\mathrm{d}y}\quad\text{in}~\Omega,

(1.2)

where the constant
m_{i}>0
is the total concentration of species
i
with valence
z_{i}\neq 0
for
i=1,\dots,I
.
For charge neutrality, we assume

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{I}m_{i}z_{i}=0.

(1.3)

Due to the integral term
\int_{\Omega}\!\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{\epsilon}(y))\,\mathrm{d}y
, (
1.2
) has nonlocal nonlinearity which makes (
1.2
) more difficult than (
1.1
).
Note that equation (
1.2
) can be denoted as
-\epsilon\Delta\phi_{\epsilon}=f_{\epsilon}(\phi_{\epsilon}):=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{I}z_{i}c_{i,\epsilon}^{\mathrm{b}}\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{\epsilon})
in
\Omega
, which has the same form as the classical PB equation.
Here
f_{\epsilon}(\phi)=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{I}z_{i}c_{i,\epsilon}^{\mathrm{b}}\exp(-z_{i}\phi)
presents the total ionic charge density (also see (
3.3
)) and
c_{i,\epsilon}^{\mathrm{b}}=m_{i}\big/\penalty\left(\int_{\Omega}\!\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{\epsilon}(y))\,\mathrm{d}y\right)
is the concentration of the
i
th ion species in the bulk.

For the general bounded smooth domain
\Omega\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{d}
(
d\geq 2
), we assume

(A3)

\Omega=\Omega_{0}-\bigcup_{k=1}^{K}\Omega_{k}
,
K\in\mathbb{N}\cup\{0\}
(the number of holes) and
\Omega_{k}
are bounded, smooth, simply connected domains with
\Omega_{k}\subset\subset\Omega_{0}
for
k\in\{1,\dots,K\}
and
\operatorname{dist}(\Omega_{i},\Omega_{j})>0
for
i,j\in\{1,\dots,K\}
and
i\neq j
(see
Figure

1
). Here
\operatorname{dist}
denotes the distance.

Figure 1
:
We sketch the schematic diagram of bounded smooth domain
\Omega=\Omega_{0}-\bigcup_{k=1}^{K}\Omega_{k}
and
K=1
, where
\Omega
has
(K+1)
boundaries
\partial\Omega_{k}
for
k=0,1,\dots,K
.

The boundary condition of (
1.1
) and (
1.2
) is the Robin boundary condition given by

\displaystyle\phi_{\epsilon}+\gamma_{k}\sqrt{\epsilon}\partial_{\nu}\phi_{\epsilon}=\phi_{bd,k}\quad\text{on}~\partial\Omega_{k}~\text{for}~k=0,1,\dots,K,

(1.4)

where
\phi_{bd,k}
is a constant for the given external electric potential, and
\gamma_{k}>0
is the ratio of Stern-layer width to the Debye screening length (cf.
[
6
,
12
,
41
,
43
]
).
As
\phi_{bd,k}=\phi^{*}
for
k=0,1,\dots,K
, equation (
1.1
) with condition (
1.4
) has only a trivial solution
\phi_{\epsilon}\equiv\phi^{*}
so we study equation (
1.1
) with condition (
1.4
) under the assumption that
\phi_{bd,k}\neq\phi^{*}
for
k=0,1,\dots,K
.
When constants
\phi_{bd,k}
are equal, the solution
\phi_{\epsilon}
to equation (
1.2
) with condition (
1.4
) is trivial. This leads us to assume that constants
\phi_{bd,k}
are not equal. When the domain
\Omega=\Omega_{0}
is simply connected (i.e., condition (A3) with
K=0
), the solution
\phi_{\epsilon}
to equation (
1.2
) with condition (
1.4
) is also trivial.
Thus, we study equation (
1.2
) with condition (
1.4
) under the assumptions that
\phi_{bd,k}
are not equal and the domain
\Omega
satisfies (A3) with
K\in\mathbb{N}
.

To find the boundary layer solutions to PB type equations in domain
\Omega
, we assume
\epsilon>0
(related to the Debye length) as a small parameter tending to zero (cf.
[
5
,
19
,
23
,
29
,
40
]
), and study the singular perturbation problems of (
1.1
) and (
1.2
) with the Robin boundary condition (
1.4
).
We characterize the asymptotic expansions of solution
\phi_{\epsilon}
to equation (
1.1
) with condition (
1.4
) across three distinct regions based on their distance from the boundary:

•

Region I (
\Omega_{k,T,\epsilon}
), where the distance from the boundary
\partial\Omega_{k}
is at most
T\sqrt{\epsilon}
,

•

Region II (
\Omega_{k,T,\epsilon,\beta}
), where the distance from the boundary
\partial\Omega_{k}
ranges between
T\sqrt{\epsilon}
and
\epsilon^{\beta}
,

•

Region III (
\Omega_{\epsilon,\beta}
), where the distance from the boundary
\partial\Omega
is at least
\epsilon^{\beta}
,

for given parameters
T>0
and
0<\beta<1/2
.
Regions I and II are called tubular neighborhoods around
\partial\Omega_{k}
(cf.
[
25
]
).
A schematic illustration of these regions is shown in
Figure

2
.

Figure 2
:
Let
0<\beta<1/2
,
T>0
and
K=1
. In (a), we present the schematic diagram of regions
\Omega_{k,T,\epsilon}=\{x\in\Omega\,:\,\operatorname{dist}(x,\partial\Omega_{k})<T\sqrt{\epsilon}\}
for
k=0,1
(blue regions),
\overline{\Omega}_{k,T,\epsilon,\beta}=\{x\in\Omega\,:\,T\sqrt{\epsilon}\leq\operatorname{dist}(x,\partial\Omega_{k})\leq\epsilon^{\beta}\}
for
k=0,1
(red regions), and
\Omega_{\epsilon,\beta}=\{x\in\Omega\,:\,\operatorname{dist}(x,\partial\Omega)>\epsilon^{\beta}\}
(white region) as
\epsilon>0
sufficiently small. The union of these regions constitutes
\Omega
. In (b), we sketch the point
p-t\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p}
near the boundary
\partial\Omega_{k}
for
k=0,1
, where
p\in\partial\Omega_{k}
and
0\leq t\leq\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2}
, as
\epsilon\to 0^{+}
. Here
\nu_{p}
is the unit outer normal at
p
with respect to
\Omega
.

The results for equation (
1.1
) with condition (
1.4
) are stated as follows.

Theorem 1
.

Assume that (A1)–(A3) hold true, and that
\phi_{bd,k}\neq\phi^{*}
for
k=0,1,\dots,K
.
Let
\phi_{\epsilon}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega})
be the unique solution to equation (
1.1
) with condition (
1.4
).
Let
k\in\{0,1,\dots,K\}
,
p\in\partial\Omega_{k}
, and
T>0
be arbitrary.
Then

(a)

\displaystyle\phi_{\epsilon}(p-t\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p})=u_{k}(t)+\sqrt{\epsilon}\Big((d-1)H(p)v_{k}(t)+o_{\epsilon}(1)\Big),

(1.5)

\displaystyle\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}(p-t\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p})=-\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}u_{k}^{\prime}(t)+(d-1)H(p)v_{k}^{\prime}(t)\right)\nu_{p}+o_{\epsilon}(1)

(1.6)

for
0\leq t\leq T
as
\epsilon\to 0^{+}
, where
p-t\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p}\in\overline{\Omega}_{k,T,\epsilon}=\{x\in\overline{\Omega}\,:\,0\leq\operatorname{dist}(x,\partial\Omega_{k})\leq T\sqrt{\epsilon}\}
(see
Figure

2
).
Here
H(p)
and
\nu_{p}
denote the mean curvature and unit outer normal at
p
with respect to
\Omega
, respectively.
The term
o_{\epsilon}(1)
, tending to zero as
\epsilon
goes to zero, depends on
T
but is independent of
p\in\partial\Omega_{k}
, which means

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}\sup_{p\in\partial\Omega_{k},\,t\in[0,T]}\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}(\phi_{\epsilon}(p-t\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p})-u_{k}(t))-(d-1)H(p)v_{k}(t)\right|=0,

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}\sup_{p\in\partial\Omega_{k},\,t\in[0,T]}\left|\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}(p-t\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p})+\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}u_{k}^{\prime}(t)+(d-1)H(p)v_{k}^{\prime}(t)\right)\nu_{p}\right|=0.

The functions
u_{k}(t)
and
v_{k}(t)
are the unique solutions to

\displaystyle u_{k}^{\prime\prime}+f(u_{k})=0\quad\text{in}~(0,\infty),

(1.7)

\displaystyle u_{k}(0)-\gamma_{k}u_{k}^{\prime}(0)=\phi_{bd,k},

(1.8)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}u_{k}(t)=\phi^{*},

(1.9)

and

\displaystyle v_{k}^{\prime\prime}+f^{\prime}(u_{k})v_{k}=u_{k}^{\prime}\quad\text{in}~(0,\infty),

(1.10)

\displaystyle v_{k}(0)-\gamma_{k}v_{k}^{\prime}(0)=0,

(1.11)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}v_{k}(t)=0.

(1.12)

(b)

There exists a positive constant
\epsilon^{*}>0
such that

(i)

\displaystyle|\phi_{\epsilon}(p-t\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p})-\phi^{*}|\leq M^{\prime}\exp(-Mt),\quad|\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}(p-t\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p})|\leq\frac{M^{\prime}}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\exp(-Mt)

(1.13)

for
0<\epsilon<\epsilon^{*}
,
0<\beta<1/2
and
T\leq t\leq\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2}
, i.e.,
p-t\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p}\in\overline{\Omega}_{k,T,\epsilon,\beta}=\{x\in\Omega\,:\,T\sqrt{\epsilon}\leq\operatorname{dist}(x,\partial\Omega_{k})\leq\epsilon^{\beta}\}
;

(ii)

\displaystyle|\phi_{\epsilon}(x)-\phi^{*}|\leq M^{\prime}\exp\left(-M\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2}\right),\qquad|\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}(x)|\leq\frac{M^{\prime}}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\exp\left(-M\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2}\right)

(1.14)

for
0<\epsilon<\epsilon^{*}
,
0<\beta<1/2
and
x\in\overline{\Omega}_{\epsilon,\beta}=\{x\in\Omega\,:\,\operatorname{dist}(x,\partial\Omega)\geq\epsilon^{\beta}\}
.

Here
M>0
and
M^{\prime}>0
are constants independent of
\epsilon
.

Remark 1
.

In electrostatics, the solution
\phi_{\epsilon}
represents the electric potential, which is of order
\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}(1)
(cf. (
1.5
)), while the associated electric field
-\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}
is of order
\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}(1/\sqrt{\epsilon})
(cf. (
1.6
)) in Region I, denoted
\Omega_{k,T,\epsilon}
.
Reflecting geometric enhancement or suppression, the boundary mean curvature
H(p)
contributes to the second-order corrections in the asymptotic expansions (
1.5
) and (
1.6
), where
u_{k}
and
v_{k}
are solutions to equations (
1.7
)–(
1.12
) and satisfy the following properties.
When the external electric potential
\phi_{bd,k}>\phi^{*}
and
k\in\{0,1,\dots,K\}
, the function
u_{k}(t)\in(\phi^{*},\phi_{bd,k})
is strictly decreasing for
t\in(0,\infty)
, and
v_{k}
is positive on
(0,\infty)
, strictly increasing on
(0,t_{k}^{*})
and strictly decreasing on
(t_{k}^{*},\infty)
, where
t_{k}^{*}0
is a constant.
Conversely, when
\phi_{bd,k}<\phi^{*}
,
u_{k}(t)\in(\phi_{bd,k},\phi^{*})
is strictly increasing for
t\in(0,\infty)
, and
v_{k}
is negative on
(0,\infty)
, strictly decreasing on
(0,t_{k}^{*})
and strictly increasing on
(t_{k}^{*},\infty)
(cf.
Propositions

B.1
and
C.1
).
The asymptotic formulas (
1.5
)–(
1.6
) thus provide approximations for the electric potential and electric field induced by an applied potential difference.
In addition, the exponential decay estimates are given by (
1.13
) with respect to the variable
t
in Region II (
\Omega_{k,T,\epsilon,\beta}
), and by (
1.14
) with respect to the parameter
\epsilon
in Region III (
\Omega_{\epsilon,\beta}
), highlighting the localized nature of boundary layer phenomena.

The total ionic charge density plays crucial roles in the behavior of biological and physical systems (cf.
[
45
]
).
Using formulas (
1.5
), (
1.13
) and (
1.14
) along with Taylor expansion of
f
, we derive the asymptotic expansion of the total ionic charge density
f(\phi_{\epsilon})
as follows.
Let
k\in\{0,1,\dots,K\}
,
p\in\partial\Omega_{k}
,
T>0
, and
0<\beta<1/2
be arbitrary.

•

In Region I (
\Omega_{k,T,\epsilon}
),

\displaystyle f(\phi_{\epsilon}(p-t\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p}))=f(u_{k}(t))+\sqrt{\epsilon}[(d-1)H(p)f^{\prime}(u_{k}(t))v_{k}(t)+o_{\epsilon}(1)]\quad\text{for}~0\leq t\leq T;

(1.15)

•

In Region II (
\Omega_{k,T,\epsilon,\beta}
),

|f(\phi_{\epsilon}(p-t\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p}))|\leq M^{\prime}\exp(-Mt)\quad\text{for}~T\leq t\leq\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2};

•

In Region III (
\Omega_{\epsilon,\beta}
),

|f(\phi_{\epsilon}(x))|\leq M^{\prime}\exp(-M\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2})\quad\text{for}~x\in\overline{\Omega}_{\epsilon,\beta},

as
0<\epsilon<\epsilon^{*}
, where
\epsilon^{*}
comes from
Theorem

1
(b),
M^{\prime}
and
M
are generic positive constants independent of
\epsilon
.
Here we have used the condition
f(\phi^{*})=0
, which follows from assumption (A2).

The total ionic charge within regions
\overline{\Omega}_{k,T,\epsilon}
,
\overline{\Omega}_{k,T,\epsilon,\beta}
and
\overline{\Omega}_{\epsilon,\beta}
can be expressed by the integral of the total ionic charge density
f(\phi_{\epsilon})
over
\overline{\Omega}_{k,T,\epsilon}
,
\overline{\Omega}_{k,T,\epsilon,\beta}
and
\overline{\Omega}_{\epsilon,\beta}
, respectively. By
Theorem

1
, we have the following results.

Corollary 1
.

Under the hypothesis of
Theorem

1
, if
\phi_{bd,k}>\phi^{*}
(or
\phi_{bd,k}<\phi^{*}
), then we have

(a)

\int_{\overline{\Omega}_{k,T,\epsilon}}\!f(\phi_{\epsilon}(x))\,\mathrm{d}x=\sqrt{\epsilon}|\partial\Omega_{k}|(u_{k}^{\prime}(0)-u_{k}^{\prime}(T))

{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\int_{\overline{\Omega}_{k,T,\epsilon}}\!f(\phi_{\epsilon}(x))\,\mathrm{d}x=}+\,\epsilon(d-1)\left(\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\!H(p)\,\mathrm{d}S_{p}\right)(Tu_{k}^{\prime}(T)+v_{k}^{\prime}(0)-v_{k}^{\prime}(T))+\epsilon o_{\epsilon}(1)<0
(or
>0
),

(b)

\int_{\overline{\Omega}_{k,T,\epsilon,\beta}}\!f(\phi_{\epsilon}(x))\,\mathrm{d}x=\sqrt{\epsilon}|\partial\Omega_{k}|u_{k}^{\prime}(T)+\epsilon(d-1)\left(\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\!H(p)\,\mathrm{d}S_{p}\right)(-Tu_{k}^{\prime}(T)+v_{k}^{\prime}(T))+\epsilon o_{\epsilon}(1)<0
(or
>0
),

(c)

\left|\int_{\overline{\Omega}_{\epsilon,\beta}}\!f(\phi_{\epsilon}(x))\,\mathrm{d}x\right|\leq\sqrt{\epsilon}M^{\prime}\exp\left(-M\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2}\right)

for
0<\epsilon<\epsilon^{*}
and
0<\beta<1/2
, where
|\partial\Omega_{k}|
is the surface area of
\partial\Omega_{k}
.

Corollary

1
(a) and (b) imply that the total ionic charge in the near-boundary regions
\Omega_{k,T,\epsilon}
and
\Omega_{k,T,\epsilon,\beta}
is negative (or positive) when
\phi_{bd,k}>\phi^{*}
(or
\phi_{bd,k}<\phi^{*}
), the external electric potential is greater (or less) than the reference potential.
This gives a way for the redistribution of ionic species by the applied electric potential difference.
Moreover,

\displaystyle 0<\frac{\int_{\overline{\Omega}_{k,T,\epsilon,\beta}}\!f(\phi_{\epsilon}(x))\,\mathrm{d}x}{\int_{\overline{\Omega}_{k,T,\epsilon}}\!f(\phi_{\epsilon}(x))\,\mathrm{d}x}\to\frac{u_{k}^{\prime}(T)}{u_{k}^{\prime}(0)-u_{k}^{\prime}(T)}\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{|\overline{\Omega}_{k,T,\epsilon}|}{|\overline{\Omega}_{k,T,\epsilon,\beta}|}\to 0\quad\text{as}~\epsilon\to 0^{+},

(1.16)

for
T>0
,
0<\epsilon<1/2
and
k=0,1,\dots,K
.
Since
u_{k}^{\prime}(T)
decays exponentially to zero as
T
goes to infinity (cf.
Proposition

B.2
(a)), then (
1.16
) shows that for sufficiently large
T
, the majority of charged particles concentrate within the region
\Omega_{k,T,\epsilon}
, whose volume satisfies
|\Omega_{k,T,\epsilon}|\ll|\Omega_{k,T,\epsilon,\beta}|
as
\epsilon\to 0^{+}
.
In addition,
Corollary

1
(c) implies that the total ionic charge in the region
\Omega_{\epsilon,\beta}
decays exponentially as
\epsilon\to 0^{+}
, demonstrating the emergence of electroneutrality in this bulk region.
This result provides a rigorous justification for the commonly used electroneutrality assumption in bulk electrolyte solutions, while also quantifying the rate at which neutrality is approached in terms of the parameter
\beta
.

Analogously to
Theorem

1
, we characterize the asymptotic expansions of solution
\phi_{\epsilon}
to equation (
1.2
) with condition (
1.4
) across three distinct regions.
The results can be stated as below.

Theorem 2
.

Assume that the domain
\Omega
satisfies condition (A3) with
K\in\mathbb{N}
,
\phi_{bd,k}
are not equal, and the charge neutrality condition (
1.3
) holds true, where
m_{i}>0
and
z_{i}\neq 0
for
i=1,\dots,I
.
Let
\phi_{\epsilon}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega})
be the unique solution to equation (
1.2
) with condition (
1.4
).
Let
k\in\{0,1,\dots,K\}
,
p\in\partial\Omega_{k}
, and
T>0
be arbitrary.
Then

(a)

\displaystyle\phi_{\epsilon}(p-t\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p})=u_{k}(t)+\sqrt{\epsilon}[(d-1)H(p)v_{k}(t)+w_{k}(t)+o_{\epsilon}(1)],

(1.17)

\displaystyle\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}(p-t\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p})=-\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}u_{k}^{\prime}(t)+(d-1)H(p)v_{k}^{\prime}(t)+w_{k}^{\prime}(t)\right)\nu_{p}+o_{\epsilon}(1)

(1.18)

for
0\leq t\leq T
as
\epsilon\to 0^{+}
, where
p-t\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p}\in\overline{\Omega}_{k,T,\epsilon}
(see
Figure

2
).
Here
H(p)
and
\nu_{p}
denote the mean curvature and the unit outer normal at
p
with respect to
\Omega
, respectively.
The term
o_{\epsilon}(1)
, tending to zero as
\epsilon\to 0^{+}
, depends on
T
but is independent of
p\in\partial\Omega_{k}
, which means

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}\sup_{p\in\partial\Omega_{k},\,t\in[0,T]}\left|\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}(\phi_{\epsilon}(p-t\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p})-u_{k}(t))-(d-1)H(p)v_{k}(t)-w_{k}(t)\right|=0,

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}\sup_{p\in\partial\Omega_{k},\,t\in[0,T]}\left|\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}(p-t\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p})+\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}u_{k}^{\prime}(t)+(d-1)H(p)v_{k}^{\prime}(t)+w_{k}^{\prime}(t)\right)\nu_{p}\right|=0.

The functions
u_{k}(t)
,
v_{k}(t)
, and
w_{k}(t)
are the unique solutions to

\displaystyle u_{k}^{\prime\prime}+f_{0}(u_{k})=0\quad\text{in}~(0,\infty),

(1.19)

\displaystyle u_{k}(0)-\gamma_{k}u_{k}^{\prime}(0)=\phi_{bd,k},

(1.20)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}u_{k}(t)=\phi_{0}^{*},

(1.21)

\displaystyle v_{k}^{\prime\prime}+f_{0}^{\prime}(u_{k})v_{k}=u_{k}^{\prime}\quad\text{in}~(0,\infty)

(1.22)

\displaystyle v_{k}(0)-\gamma_{k}v_{k}^{\prime}(0)=0,

(1.23)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}v_{k}(t)=0,

(1.24)

and

\displaystyle w_{k}^{\prime\prime}+f_{0}^{\prime}(u_{k})w_{k}=-f_{1}(u_{k})\quad\text{in}~(0,\infty),

(1.25)

\displaystyle w_{k}(0)-\gamma_{k}w_{k}^{\prime}(0)=0,

(1.26)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}w_{k}(t)=Q.

(1.27)

The constants
\phi_{0}^{*}
and
Q
are determined uniquely by (
3.26
)–(
3.27
) and (
3.114
), respectively.
The functions
f_{0}
and
f_{1}
are given by

\displaystyle f_{0}(\phi)=\frac{1}{|\Omega|}\sum_{i=1}^{I}m_{i}z_{i}\exp(-z_{i}(\phi-\phi_{0}^{*}))\quad\text{for}~\phi\in\mathbb{R},

(1.28)

\displaystyle f_{1}(\phi)=-Qf_{0}^{\prime}(\phi)+\hat{f}_{1}(\phi)\quad\text{for}~\phi\in\mathbb{R},

(1.29)

where
|\Omega|
is the volume of
\Omega
, and

\displaystyle\hat{f}_{1}(\phi)=\frac{1}{|\Omega|}\sum_{i=1}^{I}\hat{m}_{i}z_{i}\exp(-z_{i}(\phi-\phi_{0}^{*}))\quad\text{for}~\phi\in\mathbb{R},

\displaystyle\hat{m}_{i}=\frac{m_{i}}{|\Omega|}\sum_{k=0}^{K}|\partial\Omega_{k}|\int_{0}^{\infty}\![1-\exp(-z_{i}(u_{k}(s)-\phi_{0}^{*}))]\,\mathrm{d}s\quad\text{for}~i=1,\dots,I.

Furthermore, the constant
Q
satisfies

\displaystyle Q=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}\frac{\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*}}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}=\frac{\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{K}\frac{|\partial\Omega_{k}|\hat{F}_{1}(u_{k}(0))+(d-1)\left(\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\!H(p)\,\mathrm{d}S_{p}\right)\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\!u_{k}^{\prime 2}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\right)}{u_{k}^{\prime}(0)+\gamma_{k}f_{0}(u_{k}(0))}}{\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{K}\frac{|\partial\Omega_{k}|f_{0}(u_{k}(0))}{u_{k}^{\prime}(0)+\gamma_{k}f_{0}(u_{k}(0))}},

(1.30)

where
\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}
is the unique zero of function
f_{\epsilon}
for
\epsilon>0
(cf.
Proposition

3.2
) and
\hat{F}_{1}(\phi)=\int_{\phi^{*}}^{\phi}\!\hat{f}_{1}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s=\frac{1}{|\Omega|}\sum_{i=1}^{I}\hat{m}_{i}[1-\exp(-z_{i}(\phi-\phi_{0}^{*}))]
for
\phi\in\mathbb{R}
.

(b)

There exists a positive constant
\epsilon^{*}>0
such that

(i)

\displaystyle|\phi_{\epsilon}(p-t\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p})-\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}|\leq M^{\prime}\exp(-Mt),\quad|\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}(p-t\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p})|\leq\frac{M^{\prime}}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\exp(-Mt)

(1.31)

for
0<\epsilon<\epsilon^{*}
,
0<\beta<1/2
and
T\leq t\leq\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2}
, i.e.,
p-t\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p}\in\overline{\Omega}_{k,T,\epsilon,\beta}
;

(ii)

\displaystyle|\phi_{\epsilon}(x)-\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}|\leq M^{\prime}\exp\left(-M\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2}\right),\quad|\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}(x)|\leq\frac{M^{\prime}}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\exp\left(-M\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2}\right)

(1.32)

for
0<\epsilon<\epsilon^{*}
and
0<\beta<1/2
.

Here
M>0
and
M^{\prime}>0
are constants independent of
\epsilon
.

The main difference of
Theorems

1
and
2
comes from the integral terms in (
1.2
) which give the solution
w_{k}
to (
1.25
)–(
1.27
) and (
1.30
). This reflects the effect of nonlocal nonlinearity in (
1.2
).

Remark 2
.

Recall that
f_{\epsilon}(\phi)=\sum\limits_{i=1}^{I}z_{i}c_{i,\epsilon}^{\mathrm{b}}\exp(-z_{i}\phi)
represents the total ionic charge density and
c_{i,\epsilon}^{\mathrm{b}}=\dfrac{m_{i}}{\int_{\Omega}\!\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{\epsilon}(y))\,\mathrm{d}y}
is the concentration of the
i
th ion species in the bulk.
From (
1.30
), we find

\displaystyle c_{i,\epsilon}^{\mathrm{b}}=c_{i}^{\mathrm{b}}+\sqrt{\epsilon}\left(\frac{m_{i}z_{i}Q\exp(z_{i}\phi_{0}^{*})+\hat{m}_{i}\exp(z_{i}\phi_{0}^{*})}{|\Omega|}+o_{\epsilon}(1)\right)\quad\text{for}~i=1,\dots,I,

(1.33)

\displaystyle f_{\epsilon}(\phi)=f_{0}(\phi)+\sqrt{\epsilon}(f_{1}(\phi)+o_{\epsilon}(1))\quad\text{for}~\phi\in\mathbb{R},

(1.34)

(cf.
Remark

7
and (
3.96
) in
Section

3.4
), where
c_{i}^{\mathrm{b}}:=m_{i}\exp(z_{i}\phi_{0}^{*})/|\Omega|
(is the concentration of the
i
th ion species in the bulk) may correspond to the bulk concentration in the classical PB equation
-\epsilon\Delta\phi_{\epsilon}=\sum_{i=1}^{I}z_{i}c_{i}^{\mathrm{b}}\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{\epsilon})=f_{0}(\phi_{\epsilon})
in
\Omega
.
Here
f_{0}
and
f_{1}
are given in (
1.28
)–(
1.29
).

As in
Remark

1
, we have the following observation.

Remark 3
.

In electrostatics, the solution
\phi_{\epsilon}
represents the electric potential, and its gradient
-\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}
represents the electric potential, with orders
\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}(1)
and
\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}(1/\sqrt{\epsilon})
, respectively, in the region
\Omega_{k,T,\epsilon}
.
Similar to
Remark

1
, the boundary curvature
H(p)
contributes to the second-order corrections in the asymptotic expansions (
1.17
)–(
1.18
), where
u_{k}
,
v_{k}
, and
w_{k}
are the solutions to (
1.19
)–(
1.27
) and satisfy the following properties.
When the external electric potential
\phi_{bd,k}>\phi_{0}^{*}
and
k\in\{0,1,\dots,K\}
, the function
u_{k}(t)\in(\phi_{0}^{*},\phi_{bd,k})
is strictly decreasing for
t\in(0,\infty)
, and
v_{k}
is positive on
(0,\infty)
, strictly increasing on
(0,t_{k}^{*})
and strictly decreasing on
(t_{k}^{*},\infty)
, where
t_{k}^{*}
is a constant.
Conversely, when
\phi_{bd,k}<\phi_{0}^{*}
,
u_{k}(t)\in(\phi_{bd,k},\phi_{0}^{*})
is strictly increasing for
t\in(0,\infty)
, and
v_{k}
is negative on
(0,\infty)
, strictly decreasing on
(0,t_{k}^{*})
and strictly increasing on
(t_{k}^{*},\infty)
(cf.
Propositions

B.1
and
C.1
).
The asymptotic formulas (
1.17
)–(
1.18
) thus provide approximations for the electric potential and electric field induced by an applied potential difference.
Additionally, the exponential decay estimates are given by (
1.31
) with respect to the variable
t
in Region II (
\Omega_{k,T,\epsilon,\beta}
), and by (
1.32
) with respect to the parameter
\epsilon
in Region III (
\Omega_{\epsilon,\beta}
), highlighting the localized nature of boundary layer phenomena.
However,
w_{k}
, which arises from the nonlocal nonlinearity, may not behave like
u_{k}
and
v_{k}
because the constants
\hat{m}_{i}
do not have the same sign.

As the previous discussion for total ionic charge density of (
1.1
), we use formulas (
1.17
), (
1.31
), (
1.32
), (
1.34
), and the Taylor expansions of
f_{0}
and
f_{1}
to derive the asymptotic expansion of the total ionic charge density
f_{\epsilon}(\phi_{\epsilon})
as follows.
Let
k\in\{0,1,\dots,K\}
,
p\in\partial\Omega_{k}
,
T>0
, and
0<\beta<1/2
be arbitrary.

•

In Region I (
\Omega_{k,T,\epsilon}
),

\displaystyle f_{\epsilon}(\phi_{\epsilon}(p-t\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p}))=f_{0}(u_{k}(t))+\sqrt{\epsilon}[(d-1)H(p)f_{0}^{\prime}(u_{k}(t))v_{k}(t)+f_{0}^{\prime}(u_{k}(t))w_{k}(t)+f_{1}(u_{k}(t))+o_{\epsilon}(1)]

(1.35)

for
0\leq t\leq T
;

•

In Region II (
\Omega_{k,T,\epsilon,\beta}
),

|f_{\epsilon}(\phi_{\epsilon}(p-t\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p}))|\leq M^{\prime}\exp(-Mt)\quad\text{for}~T\leq t\leq\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2};

•

In Region III (
\Omega_{\epsilon,\beta}
),

|f_{\epsilon}(\phi_{\epsilon}(x))|\leq M^{\prime}\exp(-M\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2})\quad\text{for}~x\in\overline{\Omega}_{\epsilon,\beta},

as
0<\epsilon<\epsilon^{*}
, where
\epsilon^{*}
comes from
Theorem

2
(b),
M^{\prime}
and
M
are generic positive constants independent of
\epsilon
.
Here we have used the condition
f_{\epsilon}(\phi_{\epsilon}^{*})=0
, which follows from
Proposition

3.2
, and
f_{0}(\phi_{0}^{*})=0
.

As in
Corollary

1
, we use
Theorem

2
to derive the asymptotic expansions of total ionic charge within regions
\overline{\Omega}_{k,T,\epsilon}
,
\overline{\Omega}_{k,T,\epsilon,\beta}
and
\overline{\Omega}_{\epsilon,\beta}
as below.

Corollary 2
.

Under the hypothesis of
Theorem

2
, if
\phi_{bd,k}>\phi_{0}^{*}
(or
\phi_{bd,k}<\phi_{0}^{*}
), then we have

(a)

\int_{\overline{\Omega}_{k,T,\epsilon}}\!f_{\epsilon}(\phi_{\epsilon}(x))\,\mathrm{d}x=\sqrt{\epsilon}|\partial\Omega_{k}|(u_{k}^{\prime}(0)-u_{k}^{\prime}(T))\\
{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\int_{\overline{\Omega}_{k,T,\epsilon}}\!f_{\epsilon}(\phi_{\epsilon}}+\epsilon\left[(d-1)\left(\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\!H(p)\,\mathrm{d}S_{p}\right)(Tu_{k}^{\prime}(T)+v_{k}^{\prime}(0)-v_{k}^{\prime}(T))+|\partial\Omega_{k}|(w_{k}^{\prime}(0)-w_{k}^{\prime}(T))+o_{\epsilon}(1)\right]<0
(or
>0
),

(b)

\int_{\overline{\Omega}_{k,T,\epsilon,\beta}}\!f_{\epsilon}(\phi_{\epsilon}(x))\,\mathrm{d}x=\sqrt{\epsilon}|\partial\Omega_{k}|u_{k}^{\prime}(T)

{\color[rgb]{1,1,1}\int_{\overline{\Omega}_{k,T,\epsilon,\beta}}\!f_{\epsilon}(\phi_{\epsilon}(x))\,\mathrm{d}x=}+\epsilon\left[(d-1)\left(\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\!H(p)\,\mathrm{d}S_{p}\right)(-Tu_{k}^{\prime}(T)+v_{k}^{\prime}(T))+|\partial\Omega_{k}|w_{k}^{\prime}(T)\right]+\epsilon o_{\epsilon}(1)\Big]<0
(or
>0
),

(c)

\left|\int_{\overline{\Omega}_{\epsilon,\beta}}\!f_{\epsilon}(\phi_{\epsilon}(x))\,\mathrm{d}x\right|\leq\sqrt{\epsilon}M^{\prime}\exp\left(-M\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2}\right)

for
0<\epsilon<\epsilon^{*}
and
0<\beta<1/2
, where
|\partial\Omega_{k}|
is the surface area of
\partial\Omega_{k}
.

Corollary

2
is consistent with the charge neutrality condition (
1.3
), as demonstrated by equations (
3.27
) and (
3.115
) in
Section

3
.

Corollary

2
(a) and
2
(b) also indicate that the total ionic charge in the near-boundary regions
\Omega_{k,T,\epsilon}
and
\Omega_{k,T,\epsilon,\beta}
is negative (or positive) when
\phi_{bd,k}>\phi_{0}^{*}
(or
\phi_{bd,k}<\phi_{0}^{*}
).
Such behavior is analogous to that described in
Corollary

1
, where the sign of the total ionic charge is similarly determined by the comparison between
\phi_{bd,k}
and
\phi_{0}^{*}
.
This redistribution of ionic species is driven by the applied electric potential difference, consistent with the behavior in
Corollary

1
.
Moreover, analogously to (
1.16
),

0<\frac{\int_{\overline{\Omega}_{k,T,\epsilon,\beta}}\!f_{\epsilon}(\phi_{\epsilon}(x))\,\mathrm{d}x}{\int_{\overline{\Omega}_{k,T,\epsilon}}\!f_{\epsilon}(\phi_{\epsilon}(x))\,\mathrm{d}x}\to\frac{u_{k}^{\prime}(T)}{u_{k}^{\prime}(0)-u_{k}^{\prime}(T)}\quad\text{and}\quad\frac{|\overline{\Omega}_{k,T,\epsilon}|}{|\overline{\Omega}_{k,T,\epsilon,\beta}|}\to 0\quad\text{as}~\epsilon\to 0^{+}.

Since
u_{k}^{\prime}(T)
decays exponentially to zero as
T\to\infty
(cf.
Proposition

B.2
(a)), this implies that for sufficiently large
T
, the majority of charged particles are concentrated within the region
\Omega_{k,T,\epsilon}
, despite its volume being significantly smaller than that of the region
\Omega_{k,T,\epsilon,\beta}
.
Additionally,
Corollary

2
(c) implies that the total ionic charge in the region
\Omega_{\epsilon,\beta}
decays exponentially as
\epsilon\to 0^{+}
as in
Corollary

1
(c).

For the proof of
Theorem

1
, we first establish the uniform boundedness of the solution
\phi_{\epsilon}
(cf.
Proposition

2.1
), and then employ the principal coordinate system (cf.
[
24
, Section 14.6]
) and rescale spatial variables by
\sqrt{\epsilon}
to obtain the local convergence and to derive equation (
2.10
) in
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}
with condition (
2.11
) (cf.
Lemma

2.3
).
Using the exponential-type estimate of (
2.10
)–(
2.11
) (cf.
Lemma

2.4
) and the moving plane arguments (cf.
Proposition

2.5
), we prove the first-order asymptotic expansion of
\phi_{\epsilon}
(see (
2.39
)–(
2.40
)).
Using the first-order asymptotic expansion, we derive the exponential-type estimate of
\phi_{\epsilon}
and
|\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}|
(cf.
Proposition

2.6
), which implies
Theorem

1
(b) holds true.
For the second-order asymptotic expansion of
\phi_{\epsilon}
, we study
\varphi_{k,\epsilon}(x)=\epsilon^{-1/2}[\phi_{\epsilon}(x)-u_{k}(\delta_{k}(x)/\sqrt{\epsilon})]
for
x\in\overline{\Omega}_{k,\epsilon,\beta}:=\{x\in\overline{\Omega}\,:\,\delta_{k}(x)\leq\epsilon^{\beta}\}
and
k=0,1,\dots,K
.
We prove the uniform boundedness of
\varphi_{k,\epsilon}
(cf.
Proposition

2.7
) and the local convergence to get equation (
2.67
) in
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}
with condition (
2.68
) (cf.
Lemma

2.8
).
Using the exponential-type estimate (
2.69
) and the moving plane arguments (cf.
Proposition

2.9
), we obtain (
1.5
) and (
1.6
) in
Theorem

1
(a).

For the proof of
Theorem

2
, we rewrite equation (
1.2
) as
-\epsilon\Delta\phi_{\epsilon}=f_{\epsilon}(\phi_{\epsilon})
in
\Omega
, where
f_{\epsilon}(\phi)=\sum_{i=1}^{I}(m_{i}z_{i}/A_{i,\epsilon})\exp(-z_{i}\phi)
for
\phi\in\mathbb{R}
,
A_{i,\epsilon}=\int_{\Omega}\!\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{\epsilon}(y))\,\mathrm{d}y
for
i=1,\dots,I
, and
\phi_{\epsilon}
is the solution to equation (
1.2
) with condition (
1.4
).
In
Section

3.1
, we first prove the uniform boundedness of
\phi_{\epsilon}
and show that
f_{\epsilon}
also satisfies conditions (A1)–(A2) with the unique zero
\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}
.
As in the proof in
Section

2.1
, we establish the first-order asymptotic expansions of
\phi_{\epsilon}
and
\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}
.
Moreover, we also determine the unique value of
\phi_{0}^{*}
by the algebraic equations (
3.26
)–(
3.27
).
However, unlike the proof of
Theorem

1
, the unique zero
\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}
of
f_{\epsilon}
may depend on the parameter
\epsilon
so it is pivotal to prove, by contradiction, the uniform boundedness of
|\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*}|/\sqrt{\epsilon}
(cf.
Section

3.3
).
More specifically, in
Section

3.3
, under the assumption
|\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*}|/\sqrt{\epsilon}\to\infty
as
\epsilon
goes to zero, we obtain the second-order asymptotic expansions of
A_{i,\epsilon}
,
f_{\epsilon}
, and
\phi_{\epsilon}
and the total ionic charge over three distinct regions, which contradicts the electroneutrality condition (
1.3
).
Then in
Section

3.4
, we use the uniform boundedness of
|\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*}|/\sqrt{\epsilon}
to get the asymptotic expansion of
A_{i,\epsilon}
(see
Remark

7
). This implies that the function
f_{\epsilon}
has the asymptotic expansion
f_{\epsilon}(\phi)=f_{0}(\phi)+\sqrt{\epsilon}(f_{1}(\phi)+o_{\epsilon}(1))
for
\phi\in\mathbb{R}
, where
f_{0}
and
f_{1}
are smooth functions (see (
1.28
)–(
1.29
)).
Thus, equation (
1.2
) can be transformed into
-\epsilon\Delta\phi_{\epsilon}=f_{0}(\phi_{\epsilon})+\sqrt{\epsilon}(f_{1}(\phi_{\epsilon})+o_{\epsilon}(1))
in
\Omega
(cf. (
3.97
)), where
f_{0}
satisfies (A1)–(A2) with a unique zero
\phi_{0}^{*}
and
f_{1}
is bounded on any compact subsets of
\mathbb{R}
.
Therefore, as in the proof of
Theorem

1
, we may apply the principal coordinate system and the moving plane arguments to prove
Theorem

2
(a).

The electric forces play a central role in the behavior of biological and physical systems (cf.
[
47
]
).
In
[
27
,
45
]
, the Maxwell stress tensor
\mathscr{T}_{\text{M}}
of equation (
1.1
) is defined as

\displaystyle\mathscr{T}_{\text{M}}=\varepsilon\nabla\phi_{\varepsilon}\otimes\nabla\phi_{\varepsilon}-\frac{\varepsilon}{2}|\nabla\phi_{\varepsilon}|^{2}I_{d},

(1.36)

where
\otimes
denotes the tensor product.
Then by (
1.6
) and (
1.36
), we get the Maxwell stress tensor
\mathscr{T}_{\text{M}}
at
p-t\sqrt{\varepsilon}\nu_{p}
:

\mathscr{T}_{\text{M}}(p-t\sqrt{\varepsilon}\nu_{p})=[u_{k}^{\prime 2}(t)+2\sqrt{\varepsilon}(d-1)H(p)u_{k}^{\prime}(t)v_{k}^{\prime}(t)]\left(\nu_{p}\otimes\nu_{p}-\frac{1}{2}I_{d}\right)+\sqrt{\varepsilon}o_{\varepsilon}(1)

for
0\leq t\leq T
and
p\in\partial\Omega_{k}
.
By (
B.8
) and (
B.13
) with
U=u_{k}
, we have
u_{k}^{\prime 2}(t)=2(F(\phi^{*})-F(u_{k}(t)))=-2F(u_{k}(t))
for all
t\geq 0
, where
F(\phi)=\int_{\phi^{*}}^{\phi}\!f(s)\,\mathrm{d}s
and
\phi^{*}
is the unique zero of
f
.
Hence

\displaystyle\mathscr{T}_{\text{M}}(p-t\sqrt{\varepsilon}\nu_{p})=[-F(u_{k}(t))+\sqrt{\varepsilon}(d-1)H(p)u_{k}^{\prime}(t)v_{k}^{\prime}(t)](2\nu_{p}\otimes\nu_{p}-I_{d})+\sqrt{\varepsilon}o_{\varepsilon}(1),

which gives

\displaystyle\mathscr{T}_{\text{M}}(p-t\sqrt{\varepsilon}\nu_{p})\nu_{p}=-F(u_{k}(t))\nu_{p}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}[(d-1)H(p)u_{k}^{\prime}(t)v_{k}^{\prime}(t)\nu_{p}+o_{\varepsilon}(1)]

(1.37)

for
0\leq t\leq T
and
p\in\partial\Omega_{k}
.
Note that
F(u_{k}(t))<0
for
t\geq 0
because
F(\phi^{*})=F^{\prime}(\phi^{*})=0
and
F^{\prime\prime}(\phi)=f^{\prime}(\phi)<0
for
\phi\in\mathbb{R}
.
In (
1.37
),
\mathscr{T}_{\text{M}}(p-t\sqrt{\varepsilon}\nu_{p})\nu_{p}
is the stress tensor
\mathscr{T}_{\text{M}}(p-t\sqrt{\varepsilon}\nu_{p})
acting on
\nu_{p}
, which provides the electric force at
p-t\sqrt{\varepsilon}\nu_{p}
for
0\leq t\leq T
,
p\in\partial\Omega_{k}
, and
k\in\{0,1,\dots,K\}
.

For equation (
1.2
) with condition (
1.4
), we can use (
1.5
)–(
1.12
), (
1.18
), and (
1.36
) directly to derive the asymptotic formula of the Maxwell stress tensor
\mathscr{T}_{\text{M}}
:

\displaystyle\mathscr{T}_{\text{M}}(p-t\sqrt{\varepsilon}\nu_{p})=\{u_{k}^{\prime 2}(t)+2\sqrt{\varepsilon}u_{k}^{\prime}(t)[(d-1)H(p)v_{k}^{\prime}(t)+w_{k}^{\prime}(t)]\}\left(\nu_{p}\otimes\nu_{p}-\frac{1}{2}I_{d}\right)+\sqrt{\varepsilon}o_{\varepsilon}(1)

for
0\leq t\leq T
and
p\in\partial\Omega_{k}
.
By (
B.8
) and (
B.13
) with
U=u_{k}
, we have
u_{k}^{\prime 2}(t)=2(F_{0}(\phi^{*})-F_{0}(u_{k}(t)))=-2F_{0}(u_{k}(t))
for all
t\geq 0
, where
F_{0}(\phi)=\int_{\phi_{0}^{*}}^{\phi}\!f_{0}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s
and
\phi_{0}^{*}
is the unique zero of
f_{0}
.
Hence

\displaystyle\mathscr{T}_{\text{M}}(p-t\sqrt{\varepsilon}\nu_{p})=\{-F_{0}(u_{k}(t))+\sqrt{\varepsilon}u_{k}^{\prime}(t)[(d-1)H(p)v_{k}^{\prime}(t)+w_{k}^{\prime}(t)]\}(2\nu_{p}\otimes\nu_{p}-I_{d})+\sqrt{\varepsilon}o_{\varepsilon}(1),

which implies

\displaystyle\mathscr{T}_{\text{M}}(p-t\sqrt{\varepsilon}\nu_{p})\nu_{p}=-F_{0}(u_{k}(t))\nu_{p}+\sqrt{\varepsilon}u_{k}^{\prime}(t)\{[(d-1)H(p)v_{k}^{\prime}(t)+w_{k}^{\prime}(t)]\nu_{p}+o_{\varepsilon}(1)\}

(1.38)

for
0\leq t\leq T
and
p\in\partial\Omega_{k}
.
Note that
F_{0}(u_{k}(t))<0
for
t\geq 0
because
F_{0}(\phi_{0}^{*})=F_{0}^{\prime}(\phi_{0}^{*})=0
and
F_{0}^{\prime\prime}(\phi)=f_{0}^{\prime}(\phi)<0
for
\phi\in\mathbb{R}
.
In (
1.38
),
\mathscr{T}_{\text{M}}(p-t\sqrt{\varepsilon}\nu_{p})\nu_{p}
is the stress tensor
\mathscr{T}_{\text{M}}(p-t\sqrt{\varepsilon}\nu_{p})
acting on
\nu_{p}
, which provides the electric force at
p-t\sqrt{\varepsilon}\nu_{p}
for
0\leq t\leq T
,
p\in\partial\Omega_{k}
, and
k\in\{0,1,\dots,K\}
.

Throughout the paper we shall use the following notations.

•

\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}(1)
is a bounded quantity independent of
\epsilon
, and
o_{\epsilon}(1)
is a small quantity tending to zero as
\epsilon
goes to zero.

•

\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}=\{(z=(z^{\prime},z^{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:z^{d}>0\}
,
\partial\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}=\{z=(z^{\prime},z^{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:z^{d}=0\}
, and
\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{d}=\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}\cup\partial\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}
.

•

e_{d}
stands for the unit vector in the positive direction of
z^{d}
-axis.

•

|\Omega|
denotes the volume of
\Omega
, and
|\partial\Omega_{k}|
stands for the surface area of
\partial\Omega_{k}
for
k=0,1,\dots,K
.

•

\Omega_{k,T,\epsilon}=\{x\in\Omega\,:\,\operatorname{dist}(x,\partial\Omega_{k})<T\sqrt{\epsilon}\}
,
\Omega_{k,T,\epsilon,\beta}=\{x\in\Omega\,:\,T\sqrt{\epsilon}<\operatorname{dist}(x,\partial\Omega_{k})<\epsilon^{\beta}\}
,
\Omega_{k,\epsilon,\beta}=\{x\in\Omega\,:\,\operatorname{dist}(x,\partial\Omega_{k})<\epsilon^{\beta}\}
and
\Omega_{\epsilon,\beta}=\{x\in\Omega\,:\,\operatorname{dist}(x,\partial\Omega)>\epsilon^{\beta}\}
, where
\operatorname{dist}
denotes the distance.

•

\delta_{k}(x)=\operatorname{dist}(x,\partial\Omega_{k})
and
\delta(x)=\operatorname{dist}(x,\partial\Omega)=\min\{\delta_{0}(x),\delta_{1}(x),\dots,\delta_{K}(x)\}
.

•

\displaystyle\underline{\phi_{bd}}=\min_{0\leq k\leq K}\phi_{bd,k}
and
\displaystyle\overline{\phi_{bd}}=\max_{0\leq k\leq K}\phi_{bd,k}
.

•

\epsilon^{*}>0
is a sufficiently small constant, and
M>0
is a generic constant independent of
\epsilon>0
.

The rest of the paper is organized as follows.
The proofs of
Theorems

1
and
2
are given in
Sections

2
and
3
, respectively.
For the analysis of ordinary differential equations (
1.7
)–(
1.12
), (
1.19
)–(
1.21
) and (
1.22
)–(
1.27
), one may refer to
Appendices

A
,
B
,
C
and
D
.

Acknowledgement
. The research of T.-C. Lin is partially supported by the National Center for Theoretical Sciences (NCTS) and NSTC grants 112-2115M-002-012MY3 and 113-2115-M-002-002.

2
Proof of
Theorem

1

In this section, we derive the first- and second-order terms in the asymptotic expansion of the solution
\phi_{\epsilon}
to equation (
1.1
) with condition (
1.4
) near
\partial\Omega_{k}
for
k=0,1,\dots,K
.
For the first-order term, we use the principal coordinate system (
2.1
) to straighten the boundary
\partial\Omega_{k}
locally and rescale spatial variables by
\sqrt{\epsilon}
so that equations (
1.1
) with condition (
1.4
) can be transformed into (
2.8
)–(
2.9
) in the upper half-ball
B_{b/\sqrt{\epsilon}}^{+}
.
We then show that the solution to (
2.8
)–(
2.9
) converges (locally) to the solution to (
2.10
)–(
2.11
) in the upper half-space
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}
(cf.
Lemma

2.3
).
By the moving plane arguments on (
2.10
)–(
2.11
), we prove that the solution to (
2.10
)–(
2.11
) depends only on the single variable
z^{d}
(cf.
Proposition

2.5
), which is the unique solution to (
1.7
)–(
1.9
).
Here we have to assume that
\phi_{bd,k}
are constants.
Hence we obtain the asymptotic expansions
\phi_{\epsilon}(p-t\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p})=u_{k}(t)+o_{\epsilon}(1)
and
\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}(p-t\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p})=-\epsilon^{-1/2}(u_{k}^{\prime}(t)\nu_{p}+o_{\epsilon}(1))
for
T>0
,
p\in\partial\Omega_{k}
and
0\leq t\leq T
, where
u_{k}
is the unique solution to (
1.7
)–(
1.9
) and
\nu_{p}
is the unit outer normal at
p
(cf.
Section

2.1
).

For the second-order term, we set
\varphi_{k,\epsilon}(x)=\epsilon^{-1/2}(\phi_{\epsilon}(x)-u_{k}(\delta_{k}(x)/\sqrt{\epsilon}))
for
x\in\overline{\Omega}_{k,\epsilon,\beta}
and
k=0,1,\dots,K
, where
\Omega_{k,\epsilon,\beta}=\{x\in\Omega:\delta_{k}(x)<\epsilon^{\beta}\}
and
0<\beta<1/2
.
Then by the exponential-type estimate of
u_{k,p}
and
\phi_{\epsilon}
(cf.
Lemma

2.4
and
Proposition

2.6
), we may prove the uniform boundedness of
\varphi_{k,\epsilon}
(cf.
Proposition

2.7
).
Moreover, following similar arguments as in
Section

2.1
, we use the principal coordinate system and the moving plane arguments to prove the local convergence of
\varphi_{k,\epsilon}
and obtain
\varphi_{k,\epsilon}(p-t\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p})=(d-1)H(p)v_{k}(t)+o_{\epsilon}(1)
and
\nabla\varphi_{k,\epsilon}(p-t\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p})=-\epsilon^{-1/2}[(d-1)H(p)v_{k}^{\prime}(t)\nu_{p}+o_{\epsilon}(1)]
for
T>0
,
p\in\partial\Omega_{k}
and
0\leq t\leq T
(as
\epsilon>0
sufficiently small), where
v_{k}
is the unique solution to (
1.10
)–(
1.12
) (cf.
Section

2.2
).
Therefore, we obtain equations (
1.5
)–(
1.6
) and complete the proof of
Theorem

1
.

2.1
First-order asymptotic expansion of
\phi_{\epsilon}

Equation (
1.1
) with the boundary condition (
1.4
) is the Euler–Lagrange equation of the following energy functional

E[\phi]=\int_{\Omega}\!\left(\frac{\epsilon}{2}|\nabla\phi|^{2}-F(\phi)\right)\,\mathrm{d}x+\sqrt{\epsilon}\sum_{k=0}^{K}\frac{1}{2\gamma_{k}}\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\!(\phi-\phi_{bd,k})^{2}\,\mathrm{d}S\quad\text{for}~\phi\in H^{1}(\Omega).

Since
F^{\prime\prime}=f^{\prime}<0
in
\mathbb{R}
, we can apply the direct method in the calculus of variations to get the unique minimizer of
E[\phi]
over
H^{1}(\Omega)
(cf.
[
46
]
).
Then by the standard bootstrap argument and smoothness of
f
, we obtain the existence and uniqueness of the smooth solution
\phi\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega})
to equation (
1.1
) with condition (
1.4
) (cf.
[
24
]
).
Because the nonlinear term
f
is strictly decreasing (cf. (A1)), we can prove the uniform boundedness of the solution
\phi_{\epsilon}
as follows.

Proposition 2.1

(Uniform boundedness of
\phi_{\epsilon}
)
.

For
\epsilon>0
, let
\phi_{\epsilon}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega})
be the solution to equation (
1.1
) with condition (
1.4
).
Then
|\phi_{\epsilon}(x)-\phi^{*}|\leq C_{1}
for
x\in\overline{\Omega}
and
\epsilon>0
, where
\phi^{*}
is the unique zero of
f
(cf. (A2)) and
C_{1}>0
is a constant independent of
\epsilon
.

Proof.

We first prove that
\phi_{\epsilon}(x)\leq\max\{\phi^{*},\overline{\phi_{bd}}\}
for
x\in\overline{\Omega}
and
\epsilon>0
.
Suppose that
\phi_{\epsilon}
attains its maximum value at an interior point
x_{0}
, which implies
\Delta\phi_{\epsilon}(x_{0})\leq 0
.
Then by (
1.1
) and (A2), we obtain
f(\phi^{*})=0\leq-\epsilon\Delta\phi_{\epsilon}(x_{0})=f(\phi_{\epsilon}(x_{0}))
.
Due to the strict decrease of
f
(cf. (A1)), we have
\phi_{\epsilon}(x_{0})\leq\phi^{*}
, which implies
\phi_{\epsilon}(x)\leq\phi^{*}
for
x\in\overline{\Omega}
.
On the other hand, we suppose that
\phi_{\epsilon}
attains its maximum value at a boundary point
x_{0}\in\partial\Omega_{k_{0}}
for some
k_{0}\in\{0,1,\dots,K\}
, which implies
\partial_{\nu}\phi_{\epsilon}(x_{0})\geq 0
.
By the boundary condition (
1.4
) with
\gamma_{k_{0}}>0
, we get

\phi_{\epsilon}(x)\leq\phi_{\epsilon}(x_{0})=\phi_{bd,k_{0}}-\gamma_{k_{0}}\sqrt{\epsilon}\partial_{\nu}\phi_{\epsilon}(x_{0})\leq\phi_{bd,k_{0}}\leq\overline{\phi_{bd}}:=\max_{0\leq k\leq K}\phi_{bd,k}\quad\text{for}~x\in\overline{\Omega}.

Thus,
\phi_{\epsilon}(x)\leq\max\{\phi^{*},\overline{\phi_{bd}}\}
for
x\in\overline{\Omega}
and
\epsilon>0
.
Similarly, we can get
\phi_{\epsilon}(x)\geq\min\{\phi^{*},\underline{\phi_{bd}}\}
for
x\in\overline{\Omega}
and
\epsilon>0
, where
\underline{\phi_{bd}}:=\min\limits_{0\leq k\leq K}\phi_{bd,k}
.
Hence
\min\{\phi^{*},\underline{\phi_{bd}}\}\leq\phi_{\epsilon}(x)\leq\max\{\phi^{*},\overline{\phi_{bd}}\}
, i.e.,
|\phi_{\epsilon}(x)-\phi^{*}|\leq C_{1}
for
x\in\overline{\Omega}
and
\epsilon>0
, where
C_{1}>0
is a constant independent of
\epsilon>0
.
Therefore, we complete the proof of
Proposition

2.1
.
∎

Now we introduce the principal coordinate system and the associated diffeomorphism
\Psi_{p}
, which straightens the boundary portion near each point
p\in\partial\Omega_{k}
for
k\in\{0,1,\dots,K\}
.
Fix
k\in\{0,1,\dots,K\}
arbitrarily.
After a suitable rotation of the coordinate system, we may write the boundary point as
p=(p^{\prime},p^{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1}\times\mathbb{R}
and assume that the inner normal to
\partial\Omega_{k}
at
p
points in the direction of the positive
x^{d}
-axis.
To straighten the portion of
\partial\Omega_{k}
, we employ the principal coordinate system
y=(y^{\prime},y^{d})
(cf.
[
24
, Section 14.6]
).
Thus, there exist a neighborhood
\mathcal{N}_{p}
of
p
, a constant
b_{p}^{\prime}>0
(depending on
p
and
\partial\Omega_{k}
) and a smooth function
\psi_{p}=\psi_{p}(x^{\prime})
(defined for
|x^{\prime}-p^{\prime}|<b_{p}^{\prime}
) satisfying

(D1)

\psi_{p}(p^{\prime})=p^{d}
,

(D2)

\nabla\psi_{p}(p^{\prime})=0
,

(D3)

\partial\Omega_{k}\cap\mathcal{N}_{p}=\{x=(x^{\prime},x^{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:x^{d}=\psi_{p}(x^{\prime}),\,|x^{\prime}-p^{\prime}|<b_{p}^{\prime}\}
,

(D4)

\mathcal{N}_{p}^{+}:=\Omega\cap\mathcal{N}_{p}=\{x=(x^{\prime},x^{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:x^{d}>\psi_{p}(x^{\prime}),\,|x^{\prime}-p^{\prime}|<b_{p}^{\prime}\}\cap\mathcal{N}_{p}
and
\overline{\mathcal{N}_{p}}^{+}=\Psi_{p}(\overline{B}_{b}^{+})
.

Here the diffeomorphism
\Psi_{p}:\overline{B}_{b}^{+}\to\mathbb{R}^{d}
is defined by

\displaystyle x=(x^{\prime},x^{d})=\Psi_{p}(y)=(p^{\prime}+y^{\prime},\psi_{p}(p^{\prime}+y^{\prime}))-y^{d}\nu\quad\text{for}~y=(y^{\prime},y^{d})\in\overline{B}_{b}^{+},

(2.1)

where
\overline{B}_{b}^{+}=\{y=(y^{\prime},y^{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,:\,|y|\leq b,\,y^{d}\geq 0\}
,
b
is a positive constant (depending on
\Omega
) and
\nu=(\nu^{\prime},\nu^{d})=(\nabla\psi_{p}(p^{\prime}+y^{\prime}),-1)/\sqrt{1+|\nabla\psi_{p}(p^{\prime}+y^{\prime})|^{2}}
is the unit outer normal at
(p^{\prime}+y^{\prime},\psi_{p}(p^{\prime}+y^{\prime}))
with respect to
\Omega
(see
Figure

3
).
Moreover,
y^{d}=\operatorname{dist}(x,\partial\Omega_{k})
and
(y^{\prime},y^{d})=\Psi_{p}^{-1}(x^{\prime},x^{d})
for
x=(x^{\prime},x^{d})\in\overline{\mathcal{N}}_{p}^{+}
.

Figure 3
:
The function
\psi_{p}
describes the portion of
\partial\Omega_{k}
near
p\in\partial\Omega_{k}
.
The diffeomorphism
x=\Psi_{p}(y)
maps an upper half ball
\overline{B}_{b}^{+}
to the neighborhood
\overline{\mathcal{N}}_{p}^{+}
, and
z=y/\sqrt{\epsilon}
dilates
\overline{B}_{b}^{+}
into
\overline{B}_{b/\sqrt{\epsilon}}^{+}
.

By (
2.1
) and rescaling
y
by
\sqrt{\epsilon}
, we obtain

Lemma 2.2
.

For
\phi\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega})
,
p\in\partial\Omega=\bigcup_{k=0}^{K}\partial\Omega_{k}
and
\epsilon>0
, let
u_{p,\epsilon}:\overline{B}_{b/\sqrt{\epsilon}}^{+}\to\mathbb{R}
satisfy
u_{p,\epsilon}(z)=\phi(\Psi_{p}(\sqrt{\epsilon}z))
for
z\in\overline{B}_{b/\sqrt{\epsilon}}^{+}
(see
Figure

3
).
Then
u_{p,\epsilon}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\overline{B}_{b/\sqrt{\epsilon}}^{+})
, and

\displaystyle\epsilon\Delta_{x}\phi(\Psi_{p}(\sqrt{\epsilon}z))=\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}a_{ij}(z)\frac{\partial^{2}u_{p,\epsilon}}{\partial z^{i}\partial z^{j}}(z)+\sum_{j=1}^{d}b_{j}(z)\frac{\partial u_{p,\epsilon}}{\partial z^{j}}(z)\quad\text{for}~z\in B_{b/\sqrt{\epsilon}}^{+},

(2.2)

\displaystyle\partial_{\nu_{p}}\phi(\Psi_{p}(\sqrt{\epsilon}(z^{d}e_{d})))=-\epsilon^{-1/2}\partial_{z^{d}}u_{p,\epsilon}(z^{d}e_{d})\quad~\text{for}~0\leq z^{d}<b/\sqrt{\epsilon},

(2.3)

\displaystyle\phi(p-t\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p})=u_{p,\epsilon}(te_{d})\quad\text{for}~0\leq t<b/\sqrt{\epsilon},

(2.4)

where
e_{d}
and
\nu_{p}
are the unit vector in the positive direction of
z^{d}
-axis and the unit outer normal at
p
with respect to
\Omega
, respectively. The coefficients
a_{ij}
and
b_{j}
satisfy

\displaystyle a_{ij}(z)

\displaystyle=\delta_{ij}+2\psi_{ij}\sqrt{\epsilon}z^{d}+\epsilon|z|^{2}\mathcal{O}(1)\quad\text{for}~1\leq i,j\leq d~\text{and}~z\in B_{b/\sqrt{\epsilon}}^{+},

(2.5)

\displaystyle b_{j}(z)

\displaystyle=-\delta_{jd}\sqrt{\epsilon}(d-1)H(p)+\epsilon|z|\mathcal{O}(1)\quad\text{for}~1\leq j\leq d~\text{and}~z\in B_{b/\sqrt{\epsilon}}^{+}.

(2.6)

Here
\delta_{ij}=\begin{cases}1&\text{if}~i=j\\
0&\text{if}~i\neq j\end{cases}
is the Kronecker symbol,
\mathcal{O}(1)
is a bounded quantity independent of
\epsilon
and
z
, and

\displaystyle\psi_{ij}=\psi_{ji}:=\partial_{x^{i}x^{j}}\psi_{p}(p^{\prime})\quad\text{for}~1\leq i,j\leq d-1,

\displaystyle\psi_{id}=\psi_{di}:=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{n=1}^{d-1}\psi_{in}\quad\text{for}~1\leq i\leq d-1,

\displaystyle\psi_{dd}:=0.

Besides,
H(p)=(d-1)^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}\psi_{ii}
stands for the mean curvature at the boundary point
p
with respect to
\Omega
due to (D2) (see
[
24
, (14.92)]
).

Note that (
2.5
)–(
2.6
) imply that
a_{ij}
and
b_{j}
satisfy

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}a_{ij}(z)=\delta_{ij}\quad\lim_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}b_{j}(z)=0\quad\text{for}~R>0,\,1\leq i,j\leq d,~|z|<R~\text{and}~z^{d}>0.

The proof of
Lemma

2.2
follows standard calculations of the principal coordinate system (cf.
[
24
]
) so we omit it here.

To get the first-order asymptotic expansion of
\phi_{\epsilon}
near the boundary
\partial\Omega_{k}
, we define

\displaystyle u_{k,p,\epsilon}(z)=\phi_{\epsilon}(\Psi_{p}(\sqrt{\epsilon}z))\quad\text{for}~z\in\overline{B}_{b/\sqrt{\epsilon}}^{+},~p\in\partial\Omega_{k}~\text{and}~k=0,1,\dots,K,

(2.7)

where
\phi_{\epsilon}
is the solution to equation (
1.1
) with condition (
1.4
).
Then by (
2.2
), (
2.3
), and (
2.7
),
u_{k,p,\epsilon}
satisfies

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}a_{ij}(z)\frac{\partial^{2}u_{k,p,\epsilon}}{\partial z^{i}\partial z^{j}}+\sum_{j=1}^{d}b_{j}(z)\frac{\partial u_{k,p,\epsilon}}{\partial z^{j}}+f(u_{k,p,\epsilon})

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\quad\text{in}~B_{b/\sqrt{\epsilon}}^{+},

(2.8)

\displaystyle u_{k,p,\epsilon}-\gamma_{k}\partial_{z^{d}}u_{k,p,\epsilon}

\displaystyle=\phi_{bd,k}

\displaystyle\quad\text{on}~\overline{B}_{b/\sqrt{\epsilon}}^{+}\cap\partial\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d},

(2.9)

where
\partial\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}=\{z=(z^{\prime},z^{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:z^{d}=0\}
,
a_{ij}
and
b_{j}
are given in (
2.5
)–(
2.6
).
Based on the uniform boundedness of
\phi_{\epsilon}
(cf.
Proposition

2.1
), together with the standard elliptic
L^{q}
-estimate and Schauder’s estimate, we obtain the
\mathcal{C}_{\text{loc}}^{2,\alpha}
-estimate of
u_{k,p,\epsilon}
.
Using a diagonal process, we can prove the convergence of
u_{k,p,\epsilon}
in
\mathcal{C}^{2,\alpha}(\overline{B}_{m}^{+})
as
\epsilon
tends to zero (up to a subsequence) for
m\in\mathbb{N}
.
The result is stated below.

Lemma 2.3
.

For any sequence
\{\epsilon_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
of positive numbers with
\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\epsilon_{n}=0
,
\alpha\in(0,1)
and
p\in\partial\Omega_{k}
(
k\in\{0,1,\dots,K\}
), there exists a subsequence
\{\epsilon_{nn}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
such that
\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\|u_{k,p,\epsilon_{nn}}-u_{k,p}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{2,\alpha}(\overline{B}_{m}^{+})}=0
for
m\in\mathbb{N}
, where
\overline{B}_{m}^{+}=\{z=(z^{\prime},z^{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,:\,|z|\leq m,\,z^{d}\geq 0\}
and
u_{k,p}\in\mathcal{C}^{2,\alpha}_{\text{loc}}(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{d})
satisfies

\displaystyle\Delta u_{k,p}+f(u_{k,p})

\displaystyle=0\quad

\displaystyle\text{in}~\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d},

(2.10)

\displaystyle u_{k,p}-\gamma_{k}\partial_{z^{d}}u_{k,p}

\displaystyle=\phi_{bd,k}\quad

\displaystyle\text{on}~\partial\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}.

(2.11)

Here
\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}=\{z=(z^{\prime},z^{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d}\,:\,z^{d}>0\}
.

For the equation (
2.10
) together with the boundary condition (
2.11
), we can establish the following exponential-type estimate of
u_{k,p}
.

Lemma 2.4
.

For
p\in\partial\Omega_{k}
and
k\in\{0,1,\dots,K\}
, the solution
u_{k,p}
to (
2.10
)–(
2.11
) satisfies

\displaystyle|u_{k,p}(z)-\phi^{*}|\leq 2C_{1}\exp(-C_{2}z^{d})\quad\text{for}~z=(z^{\prime},z^{d})\in\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{d}~\text{and}~z^{d}\geq(d-1)/(4C_{2}),

(2.12)

where
C_{1}>0
is a constant (independent of
\epsilon
) given in
Proposition

2.1
such that
|\phi_{\epsilon}(x)-\phi^{*}|\leq C_{1}
for
x\in\overline{\Omega}
, and
C_{2}=m_{f}([\phi^{*}-C_{1},\phi^{*}+C_{1}])/8>0
.

Proof.

Let
\overline{z}=(\overline{z}^{\prime},\overline{z}^{d})\in\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{d}
arbitrarily with
\overline{z}^{d}\geq(d-1)/(4C_{2})
.
Then let
u^{\pm}
be the solution to

\displaystyle\Delta u^{\pm}+f(u^{\pm})

\displaystyle=0\quad

\displaystyle\text{in}~B_{\overline{z}^{d}}(\overline{z}),

(2.13)

\displaystyle u^{\pm}

\displaystyle=\phi^{*}\pm C_{1}\quad

\displaystyle\text{on}~\partial B_{\overline{z}^{d}}(\overline{z}).

(2.14)

We claim that

\displaystyle u^{-}\leq u_{k,p}\leq u^{+}\quad\text{in}~\overline{B}_{\overline{z}^{d}}(\overline{z}).

(2.15)

We first prove
u^{+}\geq u_{k,p}
and set
\overline{u}_{k,p}^{+}:=u^{+}-u_{k,p}
in
\overline{B}_{\overline{z}^{d}}(\overline{z})
.
Then by (
2.10
) and (
2.13
)–(
2.14
),
\overline{u}_{k,p}^{+}
satisfies

\displaystyle\Delta\overline{u}_{k,p}^{+}+c(z)\overline{u}_{k,p}^{+}=0\quad\text{in}~B_{\overline{z}^{d}}(\overline{z}),

(2.16)

\displaystyle\overline{u}_{k,p}^{+}=C_{1}-(u_{k,p}-\phi^{*})\geq 0\quad\text{on}~\partial B_{\overline{z}^{d}}(\overline{z}),

(2.17)

where
c(z)=(f(u^{+}(z))-f(u_{k,p}(z)))/(u^{+}(z)-u_{k,p}(z))
if
u^{+}(z)\neq u_{k,p}(z)
;
c(z)=f^{\prime}(u_{k,p}(z))
if
u^{+}(z)=u_{k,p}(z)
.
Here we have used (
2.7
),
Proposition

2.1
and
Lemma

2.3
.
Note that
c(z)<0
for
z\in B_{\overline{z}^{d}}(\overline{z})
because
f
is strictly decreasing on
\mathbb{R}
(cf. (A1)).
Thus, we apply the maximum principle to (
2.16
)–(
2.17
) and obtain
\overline{u}_{k,p}^{+}\geq 0
in
\overline{B}_{\overline{z}^{d}}(\overline{z})
, which implies
u^{+}\geq u_{k,p}
in
\overline{B}_{\overline{z}^{d}}(\overline{z})
.
Similarly, we can prove
u^{-}\leq u_{k,p}
in
B_{\overline{z}^{d}}(\overline{z})
, and get (
2.15
).
Since
u^{\pm}
are the radial solutions to (
2.13
)–(
2.14
), then by
Proposition

A.2
in
Appendix

A
with
\epsilon=1
and replacing
B_{R}(0)
,
r
,
\Phi_{\epsilon}
,
\Phi_{bd}
,
m_{f}
by
B_{\overline{z}^{d}}(\overline{z})
,
|z-\overline{z}|
,
u^{\pm}
,
\phi^{*}\pm C_{1}
, we obtain

\displaystyle|u^{\pm}(z)-\phi^{*}|\leq 2C_{1}\exp(-C_{2}(\overline{z}^{d}-|z-\overline{z}|))\quad\text{for}~z\in B_{\overline{z}^{d}}(\overline{z}).

(2.18)

Here we have used the fact that
\overline{z}^{d}\geq(d-1)/(4C_{2})
.
Along with (
2.15
) and (
2.18
), we obtain

|u_{k,p}(\overline{z})-\phi^{*}|=|u_{k,p}(\overline{z}^{\prime},\overline{z}^{d})-\phi^{*}|\leq|u^{\pm}(\overline{z}^{\prime},\overline{z}^{d})-\phi^{*}|\leq 2C_{1}\exp(-C_{2}\overline{z}^{d})

for
\overline{z}\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}
and
\overline{z}^{d}\geq(d-1)/(4C_{2})
, which gives (
2.12
).
Therefore, we complete the proof of
Lemma

2.4
.
∎

From
Lemma

2.3
, the solution
u_{k,p}
to (
2.10
)–(
2.11
) may, a priori, depend on the sequence
\{\epsilon_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
and on the point
p\in\partial\Omega_{k}
.
To establish the independence from the sequence and the point, we employ the moving plane arguments to prove that
u_{k,p}
satisfies
u_{k,p}(z)=u_{k}(z^{d})
for
z=(z^{\prime},z^{d})\in\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{d}
(see
Proposition

2.5
), where
u_{k}
is the unique solution to (
1.7
)–(
1.9
), and
u_{k}
is independent of the sequence
\{\epsilon_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
and the point
p\in\partial\Omega_{k}
. Consequently, we improve the results of
Lemma

2.3
as

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}\|u_{k,p,\epsilon}-u_{k}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{2,\alpha}(\overline{B}_{m}^{+})}=0\quad\text{for}~m\in\mathbb{N}~\text{and}~\alpha\in(0,1),

(2.19)

and

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}u_{k,p,\epsilon}(z)=u_{k}(z^{d})\quad\text{for}~z=(z^{\prime},z^{d})\in\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{d},

(2.20)

where
u_{k,p,\epsilon}
is defined in (
2.7
). Below are the details.

Proposition 2.5
.

For
p\in\partial\Omega_{k}
and
k\in\{0,1,\dots,K\}
, the solution
u_{k,p}
to (
2.10
)–(
2.11
) satisfies

(a)

u_{k,p}
depends only on the variable
z^{d}
, i.e.,
u_{k,p}(z)=u_{k,p}(z^{d})
for
z=(z^{\prime},z^{d})\in\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{d}
.

(b)

u_{k,p}
is independent of
p
and depends only on
k
, i.e.,
u_{k,p}(z^{d})=u_{k}(z^{d})
for
z^{d}\in[0,\infty)
, where
u_{k}
is the unique solution to (
1.7
)–(
1.9
).

Proof.

To prove (a), we fix
p\in\partial\Omega_{k}
,
k\in\{0,1,\dots,K\}
and
h\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1}-\{0\}
arbitrarily.
For notational convenience, we denote
u_{k,p}
by
u
in the proof of (a).
To use the moving plane arguments, we define
\tilde{u}
by

\displaystyle\tilde{u}(z)=u(z^{\prime}+h,z^{d})-u(z)\quad\text{for}~z=(z^{\prime},z^{d})\in\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{d}.

(2.21)

For the proof of (a), it suffices to show
\tilde{u}\equiv 0
, which is equivalent to showing
\displaystyle\sup_{\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{d}}\tilde{u}=0
and
\displaystyle\inf_{\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{d}}\tilde{u}=0
.
We first prove
\displaystyle\sup_{\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{d}}\tilde{u}=0
.
Suppose by contradiction that

\displaystyle\zeta:=\sup_{\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{d}}\tilde{u}>0.

By (
2.12
) and (
2.21
), we have

\displaystyle|\tilde{u}(z)|\leq|u(z^{\prime}+h,z^{d})-\phi^{*}|+|u(z)-\phi^{*}|\leq 4C_{1}\exp(-C_{2}z^{d})

for
z=(z^{\prime},z^{d})\in\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{d}
and
z^{d}\geq(d-1)/(4C_{2})
.
Clearly, there exists
L>(d-1)/(4C_{2})
such that
4C_{1}\exp(-C_{2}L)\leq\zeta/2
and

\displaystyle|\tilde{u}(z)|\leq\zeta/2\quad\text{for}~z=(z^{\prime},z^{d})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}\quad\text{and}\quad z^{d}\geq L.

(2.22)

Note that
\tilde{u}
(defined in (
2.21
)) may depend on
h
, but
L
is independent of
h
(because
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
are independent of
h
).
Thus

\displaystyle\zeta=\sup_{\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{d}}\tilde{u}=\sup_{\mathbb{R}^{d-1}\times[0,L]}\tilde{u}>0.

(2.23)

Then from (
2.10
)–(
2.11
),
\tilde{u}
satisfies

\displaystyle\Delta\tilde{u}+c_{1}(z)\tilde{u}

\displaystyle=0\quad

\displaystyle\text{in}~\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d},

(2.24)

\displaystyle\tilde{u}-\gamma_{k}\partial_{z^{d}}\tilde{u}

\displaystyle=0\quad

\displaystyle\text{on}~\partial\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d},

(2.25)

where

c_{1}(z)=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{f(u(z^{\prime}+h,z^{d}))-f(u(z))}{u(z^{\prime}+h,z^{d})-u(z)}&\text{if}~u(z^{\prime}+h,z^{d})\neq u(z),\\
f^{\prime}(u(z))&\text{if}~u(z^{\prime}+h,z^{d})=u(z).\end{cases}

Note that
c_{1}(z)<0
for
z\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}
because of the strict decrease of
f
(cf. (A1)).
By the strong maximum principle applied to (
2.24
) in
\mathbb{R}^{d-1}\times(0,L)
(cf.
[
24
, Theorem 3.5]
),
\tilde{u}
has no interior maximum point in
\mathbb{R}^{d-1}\times(0,L)
.
Moreover, by (
2.22
) and (
2.23
), the maximum point of
\tilde{u}
cannot be located on
\mathbb{R}^{d-1}\times\{L\}
.
On the other hand, by (
2.25
), the maximum point of
\tilde{u}
cannot be located on
\partial\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}
because
\partial_{z^{d}}\tilde{u}(z^{\prime},0)=\tilde{u}(z^{\prime},0)/\gamma_{k}=\zeta/\gamma_{k}>0
if
(z^{\prime},0)
is the maximum point of
\tilde{u}
, i.e.,
\tilde{u}(z^{\prime},0)=\zeta
.
Hence there exists a sequence
z_{n}=(z_{n}^{\prime},z_{n}^{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1}\times[0,L]
with
\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}|z^{\prime}_{n}|=\infty
and

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}z_{n}^{d}=z_{\infty}^{d}\in[0,L]

(2.26)

such that

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\tilde{u}(z_{n}^{\prime},z_{n}^{d})=\zeta.

(2.27)

Let

\displaystyle u_{n}(z)=u(z^{\prime}+z_{n}^{\prime},z^{d})\quad\text{for}~n\in\mathbb{N}~\text{and}~z=(z^{\prime},z^{d})\in\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{d}.

(2.28)

By the translation invariance (to
z^{\prime}
) of (
2.10
)–(
2.11
),
u_{n}
satisfies

\displaystyle\Delta u_{n}+f(u_{n})

\displaystyle=0\quad

\displaystyle\text{in}~\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d},

\displaystyle u_{n}-\gamma_{k}\partial_{z^{d}}u_{n}

\displaystyle=\phi_{bd,k}\quad

\displaystyle\text{on}~\partial\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}.

Note that by (
2.7
),
Proposition

2.1
, and
Lemma

2.3
,
\|u_{n}\|_{L^{\infty}(\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d})}\leq C_{1}+|\phi^{*}|
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Then similar to
Lemma

2.3
, we can apply the elliptic
L^{q}
-estimate, Sobolev’s compact embedding theorem, Schauder’s estimate and the diagonal process to get a subsequence
\{u_{n_{jj}}\}_{j=1}^{\infty}
of
\{u_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
such that

\displaystyle\lim_{j\to\infty}\|u_{n_{jj}}-u_{\infty}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{2,\alpha}(\overline{B}_{m}^{+})}=0\quad\text{for}~m\in\mathbb{N}~\text{and}~\alpha\in(0,1),

(2.29)

where
u_{\infty}\in\mathcal{C}^{2,\alpha}_{\text{loc}}(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{d})
satisfies

\displaystyle\Delta u_{\infty}+f(u_{\infty})

\displaystyle=0\quad

\displaystyle\text{in}~\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d},

(2.30)

\displaystyle u_{\infty}-\gamma_{k}\partial_{z^{d}}u_{\infty}

\displaystyle=\phi_{bd,k}\quad

\displaystyle\text{on}~\partial\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}.

(2.31)

Now we derive the contradiction and conclude that
\displaystyle\zeta=\sup_{\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{d}}\tilde{u}=\sup_{\mathbb{R}^{d-1}\times[0,L]}\tilde{u}=0
(cf. (
2.23
)). Let

\displaystyle\tilde{u}_{\infty}(z)=u_{\infty}(z^{\prime}+h,z^{d})-u_{\infty}(z)\quad\text{for}~z=(z^{\prime},z^{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1}\times[0,L].

(2.32)

From (
2.23
), we have
\zeta\geq\tilde{u}(z)
for
z\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1}\times[0,L]
.
Then by (
2.21
) and (
2.28
) with
n=n_{jj}
, we get

\displaystyle\zeta\geq u_{n_{jj}}(z^{\prime}+h,z^{d})-u_{n_{jj}}(z)\quad\text{for}~z\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1}\times[0,L]~\text{and}~j\in\mathbb{N}.

(2.33)

We prove that
\zeta
is the maximum value of
\tilde{u}_{\infty}
as follows.

Claim 1
.

\tilde{u}_{\infty}
attains its maximum value
\zeta
at
(0,z_{\infty}^{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1}\times[0,L]
.

Proof of
1
.

Fix
z=(z^{\prime},z^{d})\in\mathbb{R}^{d-1}\times[0,L]
arbitrarily. Then there exists
m_{1}\in\mathbb{N}
such that
z\in\overline{B}_{m_{1}}^{+}
and
(z^{\prime}+h,z^{d})\in\overline{B}_{m_{1}}^{+}
.
By (
2.29
) with
m=m_{1}
, we have

\displaystyle\lim_{j\to\infty}u_{n_{jj}}(z^{\prime}+h,z^{d})=u_{\infty}(z^{\prime}+h,z^{d})\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{j\to\infty}u_{n_{jj}}(z)=u_{\infty}(z).

(2.34)

Taking
j\to\infty
in (
2.33
), we use (
2.32
) and (
2.34
) to obtain

\zeta\geq u_{\infty}(z^{\prime}+h,z^{d})-u_{\infty}(z)=\tilde{u}_{\infty}(z).

(2.35)

By (
2.26
),
\displaystyle\lim_{j\to\infty}z_{n_{jj}}^{d}=z_{\infty}^{d}\in[0,L]
and hence there exists a positive integer
m_{2}
such that
(h,z_{n_{jj}}^{d})\in\overline{B}_{m_{2}}^{+}
and
(0,z_{n_{jj}}^{d})\in\overline{B}_{m_{2}}^{+}
for
j\in\mathbb{N}
. Then we use (
2.29
) with
m=m_{2}
to get

\displaystyle\lim_{j\to\infty}u_{n_{jj}}(h,z_{n_{jj}}^{d})=u_{\infty}(h,z_{\infty}^{d})\quad\text{and}\quad\lim_{j\to\infty}u_{n_{jj}}(0,z_{n_{jj}}^{d})=u_{\infty}(0,z_{\infty}^{d}).

(2.36)

By (
2.21
), (
2.27
) and (
2.28
) with
n=n_{jj}
,

\zeta=\lim_{j\to\infty}[u_{n_{jj}}(h,z_{n_{jj}}^{d})-u_{n_{jj}}(0,z_{n_{jj}}^{d})],

and then along with (
2.32
), (
2.35
) and (
2.36
), we have

\zeta=u_{\infty}(h,z_{\infty}^{d})-u_{\infty}(0,z_{\infty}^{d})=\tilde{u}_{\infty}(0,z_{\infty}^{d}),

which completes the proof of
1
.∎

By (
2.30
)–(
2.32
),
\tilde{u}_{\infty}
satisfies

\displaystyle\Delta\tilde{u}_{\infty}+c_{2}(z)\tilde{u}_{\infty}

\displaystyle=0\quad

\displaystyle\text{in}~\mathbb{R}^{d-1}\times(0,L),

(2.37)

\displaystyle\tilde{u}_{\infty}-\gamma_{k}\partial_{z^{d}}\tilde{u}_{\infty}

\displaystyle=0\quad

\displaystyle\text{on}~\partial\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d},

(2.38)

where

c_{2}(z)=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{f(u_{\infty}(z^{\prime}+h,z^{d}))-f(u_{\infty}(z))}{u_{\infty}(z^{\prime}+h,z^{d})-u_{\infty}(z)}&\text{if}~u_{\infty}(z^{\prime}+h,z^{d})\neq u_{\infty}(z),\\
f^{\prime}(u_{\infty}(z))&\text{if}~u_{\infty}(z^{\prime}+h,z^{d})=u_{\infty}(z).\end{cases}

Note that
c_{2}(z)<0
for
z\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}
because of (A1).
Then by the strong maximum principle to (
2.37
) (cf.
[
24
, Theorem 3.5]
), the maximum point
(0,z_{\infty}^{d})
of
\tilde{u}_{\infty}
cannot be an interior point of
\mathbb{R}^{d-1}\times(0,L)
.
Hence either
z_{\infty}^{d}=0
or
z_{\infty}^{d}=L
.
From (
2.22
), (
2.28
) with
n=n_{jj}
, (
2.32
) and (
2.36
), we have
\tilde{u}_{\infty}(0,L)\leq\zeta/2
.
Thus, by
1
,
z_{\infty}^{d}\neq L
,
z_{\infty}^{d}=0
,
\tilde{u}_{\infty}(0,0)=\zeta
and
(0,0)
is the maximum point of
\tilde{u}_{\infty}
in
\mathbb{R}^{d-1}\times[0,L]
.
However, (
2.38
) gives

0<\zeta=\tilde{u}_{\infty}(0,0)=\gamma_{k}\partial_{z^{d}}\tilde{u}_{\infty}(0,0)\leq 0,

which leads to a contradiction.
Hence we obtain
\zeta=\displaystyle\sup_{\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{d}}\tilde{u}=0
.
Similarly, we can prove
\displaystyle\inf_{\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{d}}\tilde{u}=0
by contradiction. Suppose that
\displaystyle\zeta^{\prime}:=-\inf_{\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{d}}\tilde{u}>0
. Then by (
2.12
) and (
2.21
), we have
|\tilde{u}(z)|\leq\zeta^{\prime}/2
for
z=(z^{\prime},z^{d})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}
and
z^{d}\geq L
for some
L>0
independent of
h
, and thus
\displaystyle\zeta^{\prime}=-\inf_{{\mathbb{R}}^{d-1}\times[0,L]}\tilde{u}
.
Arguing as in (
2.24
)–(
2.36
), we can prove
\tilde{u}_{\infty}
attains its minimum value at
(0,z_{\infty}^{d})
, where
z_{\infty}^{d}\in[0,L]
. Then we can apply the maximum principle to (
2.37
)–(
2.38
) to get a contradiction and complete the proof of (a).

To complete the proof, it remains to prove (b). By (a),
u_{k,p}=u_{k,p}(z^{d})
solves the ordinary differential equation (
1.7
) with the initial condition (
1.8
). Moreover, by the exponential-type estimate (
2.12
),
u_{k,p}
satisfies the asymptotic formula (
1.9
).
Note that the equations (
1.7
)–(
1.9
) are independent of
p
.
Hence by the uniqueness of (
1.7
)–(
1.9
) (cf.
Appendix

B
),
u_{k,p}
is independent of
p
; we therefore denote it by
u_{k}
for
k\in\{0,1,\dots,K\}
, which finalizes the proof of (b). Therefore, we complete the proof of
Proposition

2.5
.
∎

Using
Proposition

2.5
, we prove (
2.19
) and (
2.20
).
Let
T>0
,
k\in\{0,1,\dots,K\}
, and
p\in\partial\Omega_{k}
.
Then by (
2.4
), (
2.7
), and (
2.19
)–(
2.20
), we find the first-order asymptotic expansions of
\phi_{\epsilon}
and
\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}
:

\displaystyle\phi_{\epsilon}(p-t\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p})=u_{k,p,\epsilon}(te_{d})=u_{k}(t)+o_{\epsilon}(1),

(2.39)

\displaystyle\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}(p-t\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p})=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}(u_{k}^{\prime}(t)\nu_{p}+o_{\epsilon}(1))

(2.40)

for
0\leq t\leq T
as
\epsilon\to 0^{+}
.

To derive the second-order asymptotic expansions of
\phi_{\epsilon}
and
\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}
and capture the asymptotic behavior of the term
o_{\epsilon}(1)/\sqrt{\epsilon}
in the next section, it is crucial to establish the following exponential-type estimates of
\phi_{\epsilon}
and
\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}
:

Proposition 2.6

(Exponential-type estimates of
\phi_{\epsilon}
and
\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}
)
.

Let
\Omega
satisfy the uniform interior sphere condition, i.e., there exists
R>0
such that
B_{R}(p-R\nu_{p})\subseteq\Omega
and
\partial B_{R}(p-R\nu_{p})\cap\partial\Omega=\{p\}
for
p\in\partial\Omega
, where
\nu_{p}
is the unit outer normal to
\partial\Omega
at
p
.
Then we have

\displaystyle|\phi_{\epsilon}(x)-\phi^{*}|\leq 2C_{1}\exp\left(-\frac{C_{2}\delta(x)}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\right),

(2.41)

\displaystyle|\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}(x)|\leq\frac{M}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\exp\left(-\frac{C_{2}\delta(x)}{\sqrt{2\epsilon}}\right)

(2.42)

for
x\in\overline{\Omega}
and
0<\epsilon<\epsilon^{*}
, where
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
are positive constants given in
Proposition

2.1
and
Lemma

2.4
, respectively.
Here
\epsilon^{*}
is a sufficiently small constant,
M>0
is a positive constant independent of
\epsilon
, and
\delta(x)=\operatorname{dist}(x,\partial\Omega)
for
x\in\overline{\Omega}
.

Hereafter
\epsilon^{*}>0
is a sufficiently small constant, and
M>0
is a generic constant independent of
\epsilon>0
.

Proof of
Proposition

2.6
.

For
x\in\Omega
, then by the uniform interior sphere condition of
\Omega
, there exists
B_{R_{0}}(x_{0})\subseteq\Omega
with
R_{0}\geq R
such that
x\in B_{R_{0}}(x_{0})
and
\partial B_{R_{0}}(x_{0})\cap\partial\Omega\neq\emptyset
.
Note that if
\delta(x)\geq R
, then we may set
x_{0}=x
and
R_{0}=\delta(x)
; if
\delta(x)<R
, then we take
x_{0}=p_{x}+(x-p_{x})R/|x-p_{x}|
and
R_{0}=R
, where
p_{x}\in\partial\Omega
is the unique closest point to
x
.
Hence we have

\displaystyle R_{0}-|x-x_{0}|=\operatorname{dist}(x,\partial B_{R_{0}}(x_{0}))=\delta(x)\quad\text{for}~x\in\Omega.

(2.43)

To show (
2.41
), we let
\phi_{\epsilon}^{\pm}
be radial solutions to

\displaystyle-\epsilon\Delta\phi_{\epsilon}^{\pm}

\displaystyle=f(\phi_{\epsilon}^{\pm})

\displaystyle\quad\text{in}~B_{R_{0}}(x_{0}),

(2.44)

\displaystyle\phi_{\epsilon}^{\pm}

\displaystyle=\phi^{*}\pm C_{1}

\displaystyle\quad\text{on}~\partial B_{R_{0}}(x_{0}).

(2.45)

The existence of solutions to (
2.44
)–(
2.45
) follows from the standard variational method. Now we claim

\displaystyle\phi_{\epsilon}^{-}(x)\leq\phi_{\epsilon}(x)\leq\phi_{\epsilon}^{+}(x)\quad\text{for}~x\in\overline{B}_{R_{0}}(x_{0}).

(2.46)

We first prove
\phi_{\epsilon}^{+}\geq\phi_{\epsilon}
.
Let
\overline{\phi}_{\epsilon}^{+}:=\phi_{\epsilon}^{+}-\phi_{\epsilon}
in
\overline{B}_{R_{0}}(x_{0})
.
Then by (
1.1
), (
2.44
)–(
2.45
) and
Proposition

2.1
,
\overline{\phi}_{\epsilon}^{+}
satisfies

\displaystyle\epsilon\Delta\overline{\phi}_{\epsilon}^{+}+c(x)\overline{\phi}_{\epsilon}^{+}=0\quad

\displaystyle\text{in}~B_{R_{0}}(x_{0}),

(2.47)

\displaystyle\overline{\phi}_{\epsilon}^{+}=C_{1}-(\phi_{\epsilon}-\phi^{*})\geq 0\quad

\displaystyle\text{on}~\partial B_{R_{0}}(x_{0}),

(2.48)

where
c(x)=(f(\phi_{\epsilon}^{+}(x))-f(\phi_{\epsilon}(x)))/(\phi_{\epsilon}^{+}(x)-\phi_{\epsilon}(x))
if
\phi_{\epsilon}^{+}(x)\neq\phi_{\epsilon}(x)
;
c(x)=f^{\prime}(\phi_{\epsilon}(x))
if
\phi_{\epsilon}^{+}(x)=\phi_{\epsilon}(x)
.
Note that
c(x)<0
for
x\in B_{R_{0}}(x_{0})
because
f
is strictly decreasing on
\mathbb{R}
(cf. (A1)).
Thus, we apply the maximum principle to (
2.47
)–(
2.48
) and obtain
\overline{\phi}_{\epsilon}^{+}\geq 0
in
\overline{B}_{R_{0}}(x_{0})
, which implies
\phi_{\epsilon}^{+}\geq\phi_{\epsilon}
in
\overline{B}_{R_{0}}(x_{0})
.
Similarly, we prove
\phi_{\epsilon}^{-}\leq\phi_{\epsilon}
in
\overline{B}_{R_{0}}(x_{0})
, and get (
2.46
).
Since
\phi_{\epsilon}^{\pm}
are the radial solutions to (
2.44
)–(
2.45
), then by (
2.43
) and
Proposition

A.2
(in
Appendix

A
) by replacing
B_{R}(0)
with
B_{R_{0}}(x_{0})
, we may use the fact that
R_{0}\geq R
and obtain

\displaystyle|\phi_{\epsilon}^{\pm}(x)-\phi^{*}|\leq 2C_{1}\exp\left(-\frac{C_{2}\operatorname{dist}(x,\partial B_{R_{0}}(x_{0}))}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\right)=2C_{1}\exp\left(-\frac{C_{2}\delta(x)}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\right)

(2.49)

for
x\in\overline{B}_{R_{0}}(x_{0})
and
0<\epsilon<8(C_{2}R)^{2}/(d-1)^{2}
, which gives (
2.41
).

To get (
2.42
), we first claim

\displaystyle|\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}(x)|\leq M/\sqrt{\epsilon}\quad\text{for}~x\in\overline{\Omega}\quad\text{and}\quad 0<\epsilon<(\delta(x))^{2},

(2.50)

where
M>0
is independent of
\epsilon>0
.
When
x\in\partial\Omega
, we may use (
2.40
) to obtain (
2.50
).
Note that
B_{\sqrt{\epsilon}}(x_{1})\subseteq\Omega
for
x_{1}\in\Omega
and
0<\epsilon<(\delta(x_{1}))^{2}
.
Set
y=(x-x_{1})/\sqrt{\epsilon}
and
\tilde{\phi}_{\epsilon}(y)=\phi_{\epsilon}(x_{1}+\sqrt{\epsilon}y)
.
Then from (
1.1
), we have
-\Delta\tilde{\phi}_{\epsilon}=f(\tilde{\phi}_{\epsilon})
in
B_{1}(0)
.
By the uniform boundedness of
\phi_{\epsilon}
(cf.
Proposition

2.1
), we can apply the elliptic
L^{q}
-estimate and obtain
\|\tilde{\phi}_{\epsilon}\|_{W^{2,q}(B_{1/2}(0))}\leq M
for
q>1
.
Using Sobolev’s compact embedding theorem, we get
\|\tilde{\phi}_{\epsilon}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1,\alpha}(B_{1/4}(0))}\leq M
for
\alpha\in(0,1)
.
In particular, we have
|\nabla\tilde{\phi}_{\epsilon}(0)|\leq M
, which implies (
2.50
) holds true for
x\in\Omega
.

Now we prove (
2.42
).
By (
1.1
), we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \epsilon\Delta|\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}|^{2}&=2\epsilon\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}\left(\frac{\partial^{2}\phi_{\epsilon}}{\partial x^{j}\partial x^{i}}\right)^{2}+2\epsilon\sum_{j=1}^{d}\frac{\partial\phi_{\epsilon}}{\partial x^{j}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}\Delta\phi_{\epsilon}\\
&\geq-2\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}\cdot\nabla(f(\phi_{\epsilon}))=-2f^{\prime}(\phi_{\epsilon})|\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}|^{2}\quad\text{in}~B_{R_{0}}(x_{0}).\end{aligned}

(2.51)

Along with
Proposition

2.1
and (A1), we have
\epsilon\Delta|\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}|^{2}\geq 128C_{2}^{2}|\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}|^{2}
in
B_{R_{0}}(x_{0})
, where
C_{2}=m_{f}([\phi^{*}-C_{1},\phi^{*}+C_{1}])/8>0
is given in
Lemma

2.4
.
Let
\overline{\phi}_{\epsilon}
be the solution to
\epsilon\Delta\overline{\phi}_{\epsilon}=128C_{2}^{2}\overline{\phi}_{\epsilon}
in
B_{R_{0}}(x_{0})
with the Dirichlet boundary condition
\overline{\phi}_{\epsilon}=\max\limits_{\partial B_{R_{0}}(x_{0})}|\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}|^{2}
on
\partial B_{R_{0}}(x_{0})
.
Then by the standard comparison principle, we obtain

|\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}(x)|^{2}\leq|\overline{\phi}_{\epsilon}(x)|\leq 2\left(\max_{\partial B_{R_{0}}(x_{0})}|\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}|^{2}\right)\exp\left(-\frac{\sqrt{2}C_{2}\operatorname{dist}(x,\partial B_{R_{0}}(x_{0}))}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\right)

for
x\in\overline{B}_{R_{0}}(x_{0})
and
0<\epsilon<16(C_{2}R)^{2}/(d-1)^{2}
.
Combining (
2.50
), we obtain

|\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}(x_{0})|\leq\frac{M}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\exp\left(-\frac{C_{2}\delta(x_{0})}{\sqrt{2\epsilon}}\right)\quad\text{for}~0<\epsilon<16(C_{2}R)^{2}/(d-1)^{2},

which implies (
2.42
).
Therefore, we complete the proof of
Proposition

2.6
.
∎

By
Proposition

2.6
, it is clear that
Theorem

1
(b) holds true.

2.2
Second-order asymptotic expansion of
\phi_{\epsilon}
in
\Omega_{k,\epsilon}

In this section, we prove the second-order asymptotic expansion of
\phi_{\epsilon}
near the boundary
\partial\Omega_{k}
for
k\in\{0,1,\dots,K\}
.
Since
\operatorname{dist}(\Omega_{i},\Omega_{j})>0
for
i\neq j\in\{0,1,\dots,K\}
(cf. (A3)), there exists
\epsilon^{*}>0
sufficiently small such that
\operatorname{dist}(\Omega_{i,\epsilon,\beta},\Omega_{j,\epsilon,\beta})\geq d_{0}>0
for
i\neq j
and
\Omega_{k,\epsilon,\beta}=\{x\in\Omega:\,\delta_{k}(x):=\operatorname{dist}(x,\partial\Omega_{k})<\epsilon^{\beta}\}
contains no focal points for
0<\beta<1/2
and
0<\epsilon<\epsilon^{*}
(cf.
[
10
]
), where
d_{0}>0
is independent of
\epsilon
.
Then for
x\in\overline{\Omega}_{k,\epsilon,\beta}
, there exists a unique
p_{x}\in\partial\Omega_{k}
(the closest point to
x
) and
0\leq t_{x}=\delta_{k}(x)/\sqrt{\epsilon}\leq\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2}
such that
x=p_{x}-t_{x}\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p_{x}}
, where
\nu_{p_{x}}
is the unit outer normal at
p_{x}
with respect to
\Omega=\Omega_{0}-\bigcup_{k=1}^{K}\Omega_{k}
.
Hence (
2.39
)–(
2.40
) become

\displaystyle\phi_{\epsilon}(x)=u_{k}(\delta_{k}(x)/\sqrt{\epsilon})+o_{\epsilon}(1),

(2.52)

\displaystyle\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}(x)=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}[u_{k}^{\prime}(\delta_{k}(x)/\sqrt{\epsilon})\nu_{p_{x}}+o_{\epsilon}(1)].

(2.53)

For the second-order term of
\phi_{\epsilon}
, we use (
2.52
) to define the following function:

\displaystyle\varphi_{k,\epsilon}(x)=\frac{\phi_{\epsilon}(x)-u_{k}(\delta_{k}(x)/\sqrt{\epsilon})}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\quad\text{for}~x\in\overline{\Omega}_{k,\epsilon,\beta}~\text{and}~k=0,1,\dots,K,

(2.54)

where
u_{k}
is the unique solution to (
1.7
)–(
1.9
), and
\delta_{k}(x)=\operatorname{dist}(x,\partial\Omega_{k})
for
k=0,1,\dots,K
.
Then by (
1.1
), (
1.7
), and (
2.54
),
\varphi_{k,\epsilon}
satisfies

\displaystyle\epsilon\Delta\varphi_{k,\epsilon}(x)+c_{\epsilon}(x)\varphi_{k,\epsilon}(x)=g_{\epsilon}(x)\quad\text{for}~x\in\Omega_{k,\epsilon,\beta},

(2.55)

where

\displaystyle c_{\epsilon}(x)=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{f(\phi_{\epsilon}(x))-f(u_{k}(\delta_{k}(x)/\sqrt{\epsilon}))}{\phi_{\epsilon}(x)-u_{k}(\delta_{k}(x)/\sqrt{\epsilon})}&\text{if}~\phi_{\epsilon}(x)\neq u_{k}(\delta_{k}(x)/\sqrt{\epsilon});\\
f^{\prime}(\phi_{\epsilon}(x))&\text{if}~\phi_{\epsilon}(x)=u_{k}(\delta_{k}(x)/\sqrt{\epsilon}),\end{cases}

(2.56)

\displaystyle g_{\epsilon}(x)=(d-1)H(p_{x})u_{k}^{\prime}(\delta_{k}(x)/\sqrt{\epsilon})+\epsilon^{\beta}\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}(1).

(2.57)

Here we have used the fact that
0\leq\delta_{k}(x)\leq\epsilon^{\beta}
,
|\nabla\delta_{k}(x)|=1
, and

\displaystyle\Delta\delta_{k}(x)=-\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}\frac{\kappa_{i}}{1-\kappa_{i}\delta_{k}(x)}=-(d-1)H(p_{x})+\epsilon^{\beta}\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}(1)\quad\text{for}~x\in\Omega_{k,\epsilon,\beta},

(2.58)

where
\kappa_{i}
are the principal curvatures at
p_{x}\in\partial\Omega_{k}
, and
H(p_{x})=(d-1)^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{d-1}\kappa_{i}
is the mean curvature at
p_{x}\in\partial\Omega_{k}
(cf.
[
24
, Lemma 14.17]
).
By (A1), (
2.12
) and
Proposition

2.1
,
c_{\epsilon}(x)\leq-64C_{2}^{2}<0
for
x\in\overline{\Omega}_{k,\epsilon,\beta}
, where
C_{2}=m_{f}([\phi^{*}-C_{1},\phi^{*}+C_{1}])/8
.
Moreover,
g_{\epsilon}
is uniformly bounded with respect to
\epsilon
in
\Omega_{k,\epsilon}
because
u_{k}^{\prime}
is bounded on
[0,\infty)
by
Proposition

B.1
in
Appendix

B
. For the boundary condition of
\varphi_{k,\epsilon}
, we use (
1.4
), (
1.8
), (
2.3
) and (
2.54
) to get
