\displaystyle\varphi_{k,\epsilon}+\gamma_{k}\sqrt{\epsilon}\partial_{\nu}\varphi_{k,\epsilon}=0\quad\text{on}~\partial\Omega_{k}.

(2.59)

Then we prove the uniform boundedness of
\varphi_{k,\epsilon}
as follows.

Proposition 2.7

(Uniform boundedness of
\varphi_{k,\epsilon}
)
.

There exists a constant
M>0
independent of
\epsilon
such that
\displaystyle\max_{\overline{\Omega}_{k,\epsilon,\beta}}|\varphi_{k,\epsilon}|\leq M
for
k=0,1,\dots,K
and
0<\epsilon<\epsilon^{*}
.

Proof.

Fix
k\in\{0,1,\dots,K\}
.
It is equivalent to proving that
\displaystyle\max_{\overline{\Omega}_{k,\epsilon,\beta}}\varphi_{k,\epsilon}\leq M
and
\displaystyle\min_{\overline{\Omega}_{k,\epsilon,\beta}}\varphi_{k,\epsilon}\geq-M
for some constant
M>0
independent of
\epsilon
.
First, we show that
\displaystyle\max_{\overline{\Omega}_{k,\epsilon,\beta}}\varphi_{k,\epsilon}\leq M
for
0<\epsilon<\epsilon^{*}
.
Suppose by contradiction that there exists a sequence
\{\epsilon_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
of positive numbers with
\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\epsilon_{n}=0
and
\{x_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}\subset\overline{\Omega}_{k,\epsilon_{n},\beta}
such that
\displaystyle\varphi_{k,\epsilon_{n}}(x_{n})=\max_{\overline{\Omega}_{k,\epsilon_{n},\beta}}\varphi_{k,\epsilon_{n}}\geq n
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Since
0<\beta<1/2
, we may, without loss of generality, assume that
8C_{1}\exp(-C_{2}\epsilon_{n}^{(2\beta-1)/2})\leq\sqrt{\epsilon_{n}}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, where
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
are given in
Proposition

2.1
and
Lemma

2.4
, respectively.
Note that the maximum point
x_{n}
cannot lie on the boundary
\partial\Omega_{k}
because from (
2.59
),
\partial_{\nu}\varphi_{k,\epsilon_{n}}(x_{n})=-\varphi_{k,\epsilon_{n}}(x_{n})/(\gamma_{k}\sqrt{\epsilon_{n}})\leq-n/(\gamma_{k}\sqrt{\epsilon_{n}})<0
if the maximum point
x_{n}\in\partial\Omega_{k}
.
On the other hand, by (
2.12
) (with
Proposition

2.5
) and (
2.41
), we have

\displaystyle|\varphi_{k,\epsilon_{n}}(x)|\leq\frac{|\phi_{\epsilon_{n}}(x)-\phi^{*}|+|u_{k}(\delta_{k}(x)/\sqrt{\epsilon_{n}})-\phi^{*}|}{\sqrt{\epsilon_{n}}}\leq\frac{4C_{1}}{\sqrt{\epsilon_{n}}}\exp\left(-C_{2}\epsilon_{n}^{(2\beta-1)/2}\right)\leq\frac{1}{2}

(2.60)

for
\delta_{k}(x)=\epsilon_{n}^{\beta}
and
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
This shows that
x_{n}
cannot lie on the boundary
\partial\Omega_{k,\epsilon_{n},\beta}
.
Hence
x_{n}\in\Omega_{k,\epsilon_{n},\beta}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, which implies
\nabla\varphi_{k,\epsilon_{n}}(x_{n})=0
and
\Delta\varphi_{k,\epsilon_{n}}(x_{n})\leq 0
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Thus by (
2.55
),

\displaystyle 0\leq-\epsilon_{n}\Delta\varphi_{k,\epsilon_{n}}(x_{n})=c_{\epsilon_{n}}(x_{n})\varphi_{k,\epsilon_{n}}(x_{n})-g_{\epsilon_{n}}(x_{n}).

(2.61)

Recall that
g_{\epsilon}
is uniformly bounded,
\varphi_{k,\epsilon_{n}}(x_{n})\geq n
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and
c_{\epsilon}\leq-64C_{2}^{2}<0
in
\overline{\Omega}_{k,\epsilon}
for
\epsilon>0
.
Letting
n\to\infty
, we obtain
\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}c_{\epsilon_{n}}(x_{n})\varphi_{k,\epsilon_{n}}(x_{n})=-\infty
, which contradicts (
2.61
).
Thus,
\displaystyle\max_{\overline{\Omega}_{k,\epsilon,\beta}}\varphi_{k,\epsilon}\leq M
for some constant
M>0
independent of
\epsilon
.
Similarly, we may use (
2.12
), (
2.41
), (
2.55
) and (
2.59
) to prove
\displaystyle\min_{\overline{\Omega}_{k,\epsilon,\beta}}\varphi_{k,\epsilon}\geq-M
for
0<\epsilon<\epsilon^{*}
, where
M>0
is independent of
\epsilon>0
.
Therefore, we complete the proof of
Proposition

2.7
.
∎

To study the asymptotic expansion of
\varphi_{k,\epsilon}
, we first claim that
\Psi_{p}(\overline{B}_{\epsilon^{(2\beta+1)/2}}^{+})\subseteq\overline{\Omega}_{k,\epsilon,\beta}
for
p\in\partial\Omega_{k}
,
k\in\{0,1,\dots,K\}
and sufficiently small
\epsilon>0
because

\displaystyle\delta_{k}(\Psi_{p}(y))=|\delta_{k}(\Psi_{p}(y))-\delta_{k}(p)|\leq C|\Psi_{p}(y)-p|\leq C^{\prime}|y|\leq\epsilon^{\beta}

for
y\in\overline{B}_{\epsilon^{(2\beta+1)/2}}^{+}
,
p\in\partial\Omega_{k}
,
k\in\{0,1,\dots,K\}
and sufficiently small
\epsilon>0
, where
\Psi_{p}
is the diffeomorphism in (
2.1
),
C=C(\Omega)
and
C^{\prime}=C^{\prime}(p,\Omega)
are positive constants independent of
y
.
Here we have used the facts that
\delta_{k}(p)=0
,
\psi_{p}(p^{\prime})=p^{d}
(cf. (D1)) and the smoothness of
\psi_{p}
and
\delta_{k}
.
Hence there exists
\epsilon^{*}>0
sufficiently small such that
\Psi_{p}(\overline{B}_{\epsilon^{(2\beta+1)/2}}^{+})\subset\overline{\Omega}_{k,\epsilon,\beta}\cap\Psi_{p}(\overline{B}_{b}^{+})
for
0<\epsilon<\epsilon^{*}
.
As in
Section

2.1
, we apply the principal coordinate system (
2.1
) to the neighborhood
\Psi_{p}(\overline{B}_{\epsilon^{(2\beta+1)/2}}^{+})
, rescale the spatial variable
y
by
\sqrt{\epsilon}
and define

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{k,p,\epsilon}(z)=\varphi_{k,\epsilon}(\Psi_{p}(\sqrt{\epsilon}z))\quad\text{for}~z\in\overline{B}_{\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2}}^{+}~\text{and}~0<\epsilon<\epsilon^{*}.

(2.62)

Note that
\mathcal{W}_{k,p,\epsilon}
is well-defined due to
\Psi_{p}(\overline{B}_{\epsilon^{(2\beta+1)/2}}^{+})\subset\overline{\Omega}_{k,\epsilon,\beta}\cap\Psi_{p}(\overline{B}_{b}^{+})
for
0<\epsilon<\epsilon^{*}
.

By
Lemma

2.2
and equations (
2.55
)–(
2.59
),
\mathcal{W}_{k,p,\epsilon}
satisfies

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}a_{ij}(z)\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{W}_{k,p,\epsilon}}{\partial z^{i}\partial z^{j}}+\sum_{j=1}^{d}b_{j}(z)\frac{\partial\mathcal{W}_{k,p,\epsilon}}{\partial z^{j}}+c_{\epsilon}(z)\mathcal{W}_{k,p,\epsilon}

\displaystyle=g_{\epsilon}(z)\quad

\displaystyle\text{in}~B_{\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2}}^{+},

(2.63)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{k,p,\epsilon}-\gamma_{k}\partial_{z^{d}}\mathcal{W}_{k,p,\epsilon}

\displaystyle=0\quad

\displaystyle\text{on}~\overline{B}_{\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2}}^{+}\cap\partial\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}

(2.64)

for
0<\epsilon<\epsilon^{*}
, where
a_{ij}
and
b_{j}
are given in (
2.5
)–(
2.6
), and

\displaystyle c_{\epsilon}(z)=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{f(u_{k}(z^{d})+\sqrt{\epsilon}\mathcal{W}_{k,p,\epsilon}(z))-f(u_{k}(z^{d}))}{\sqrt{\epsilon}\mathcal{W}_{k,p,\epsilon}(z)}&\text{if}~\mathcal{W}_{k,p,\epsilon}(z)\neq 0;\\
f^{\prime}(u_{k}(z^{d}))&\text{if}~\mathcal{W}_{k,p,\epsilon}(z)=0,\end{cases}

(2.65)

\displaystyle g_{\epsilon}(z)=(d-1)H(p)u_{k}^{\prime}(z^{d})+\epsilon^{\beta}\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}(1).

(2.66)

As in the proofs of
Lemmas

2.3
and
2.4
, we may use the uniform boundedness of
\varphi_{k,\epsilon}
(cf.
Proposition

2.7
) to prove the convergence of
\mathcal{W}_{k,p,\epsilon}
in
\mathcal{C}^{2,\alpha}(\overline{B}_{m}^{+})
as
\epsilon
tends to zero (up to a subsequence) for
m\in\mathbb{N}
and then prove the exponential-type estimate of
\mathcal{W}_{k,p}
.

Lemma 2.8
.

For any sequence
\{\epsilon_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
of positive numbers with
\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\epsilon_{n}=0
,
\alpha\in(0,1)
, and
p\in\partial\Omega_{k}
(
k\in\{0,1,\dots,K\}
), there exists a subsequence
\{\epsilon_{nn}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
such that
\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\|\mathcal{W}_{k,p,\epsilon_{nn}}-\mathcal{W}_{k,p}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{2,\alpha}(\overline{B}_{m}^{+})}=0
for
m\in\mathbb{N}
, where
\mathcal{W}_{k,p}\in\mathcal{C}^{2,\alpha}_{\text{loc}}(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{d})
satisfies

\displaystyle\Delta\mathcal{W}_{k,p}+f^{\prime}(u_{k})\mathcal{W}_{k,p}

\displaystyle=(d-1)H(p)u_{k}^{\prime}(z^{d})\quad

\displaystyle\text{in}~\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d},

(2.67)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{k,p}-\gamma_{k}\partial_{z^{d}}\mathcal{W}_{k,p}

\displaystyle=0\quad

\displaystyle\text{on}~\partial\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}.

(2.68)

Moreover, there exists
M>0
such that
\mathcal{W}_{k,p}
satisfies

\displaystyle|\mathcal{W}_{k,p}(z)-(d-1)H(p)v_{k}(z^{d})|\leq M\exp(-C_{2}z^{d})

(2.69)

for
z=(z^{\prime},z^{d})\in\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{d}
and
z^{d}\geq(d-1)/(4C_{2})
, where
v_{k}
is the solution to (
1.10
)–(
1.12
) and
C_{2}
is given in
Lemma

2.4
.

From
Lemma

2.8
, the solution
\mathcal{W}_{k,p}
to (
2.67
)–(
2.68
) may, a priori depend on the sequence
\{\epsilon_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
.
To establish the independence from the sequence, we apply the moving plane arguments to prove
\mathcal{W}_{k,p}(z)=\mathcal{W}_{k,p}(z^{d})=(d-1)H(p)v_{k}(z^{d})
for
z=(z^{\prime},z^{d})\in\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{d}
(see
Proposition

2.9
below), where
v_{k}
is the unique solution to (
1.10
)–(
1.12
), and
v_{k}
is independent of the choice of sequence
\{\epsilon_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
and point
p\in\partial\Omega_{k}
.
Therefore, the results of
Lemma

2.8
can be improved as

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}\|\mathcal{W}_{k,p,\epsilon}-(d-1)H(p)v_{k}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{2,\alpha}(\overline{B}_{m}^{+})}=0\quad\text{for}~m\in\mathbb{N}~\text{and}~\alpha\in(0,1),

(2.70)

and

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}\mathcal{W}_{k,p,\epsilon}(z)=(d-1)H(p)v_{k}(z^{d})\quad\text{for}~z=(z^{\prime},z^{d})\in\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{d},

(2.71)

where
\mathcal{W}_{k,p,\epsilon}
is defined in (
2.62
).
Below are the details.

Proposition 2.9
.

For
p\in\partial\Omega_{k}
and
k\in\{0,1,\dots,K\}
, the solution
\mathcal{W}_{k,p}
to (
2.67
)–(
2.68
) satisfies

(a)

\mathcal{W}_{k,p}
depends only on the variable
z^{d}
, i.e.,
\mathcal{W}_{k,p}(z)=\mathcal{W}_{k,p}(z^{d})
for
z=(z^{\prime},z^{d})\in\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{d}
.

(b)

\mathcal{W}_{k,p}(z^{d})=(d-1)H(p)v_{k}(z^{d})
for
z^{d}\in[0,\infty)
, where
v_{k}
is the unique solution to (
1.10
)–(
1.12
).

As in
Proposition

2.5
, we may use the moving plane arguments to prove
Proposition

2.9
.
The proof of
Proposition

2.9
is similar to that of
Proposition

2.5
so we omit it here.

We may use
Proposition

2.9
to prove (
2.70
), (
2.71
), and
\mathcal{W}_{k,p}(z)=(d-1)H(p)v_{k}(z^{d})
for
z=(z^{\prime},z^{d})\in\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{d}
, where
v_{k}
is the solution to (
1.10
)–(
1.12
).
Let
T>0
,
k\in\{0,1,\dots,K\}
, and
p\in\partial\Omega_{k}
.
Then by (
2.1
), (
2.62
), (
2.70
)–(
2.71
) and
\nabla\delta_{k}(p-t\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p})=-\nu_{p}
, we have

\displaystyle\varphi_{k,\epsilon}(p-t\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p})=\mathcal{W}_{k,p,\epsilon}(te_{d})=\mathcal{W}_{k,p}(t)+o_{\epsilon}(1)=(d-1)H(p)v_{k}(t)+o_{\epsilon}(1)

(2.72)

and

\displaystyle\nabla\varphi_{k,\epsilon}(p-t\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p})=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}(\mathcal{W}_{k,p}^{\prime}(t)\nu_{p}+o_{\epsilon}(1))=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}((d-1)H(p)v_{k}^{\prime}(t)\nu_{p}+o_{\epsilon}(1))

(2.73)

for
0\leq t\leq T
as
\epsilon\to 0^{+}
.
Combining (
2.54
), (
2.62
), and (
2.72
)–(
2.73
), we obtain

\displaystyle\phi_{\epsilon}(p-t\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p})=u_{k}(t)+\sqrt{\epsilon}\Big((d-1)H(p)v_{k}(t)+o_{\epsilon}(1)\Big),

\displaystyle\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}(p-t\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p})=-\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}u_{k}^{\prime}(t)+(d-1)H(p)v_{k}^{\prime}(t)\right)\nu_{p}+o_{\epsilon}(1)

for
0\leq t\leq T
as
\epsilon\to 0^{+}
, which implies (
1.5
)–(
1.6
).
Therefore, we complete the proof of
Theorem

1
(a).

2.3
Proof of
Corollary

1

We present the proof of
Corollary

1
.
To prove (a), we integrate (
1.15
) over
\overline{\Omega}_{k,T,\epsilon}
and apply the coarea formula (see
[
18
,
37
]
) to obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\overline{\Omega}_{k,T,\epsilon}}\!f(\phi_{\epsilon}(x))\,\mathrm{d}x=\sqrt{\epsilon}\int_{0}^{T}\!\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\!\{f(u_{k}(t))+\sqrt{\epsilon}[(d-1)H(p)f^{\prime}(u_{k}(t))v_{k}(t)+o_{\epsilon}(1)]\}\mathcal{J}(t,p)\,\mathrm{d}S_{p}\,\mathrm{d}t,

(2.74)

where

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}(t,p)=1-t\sqrt{\epsilon}(d-1)H(p)+t\sqrt{\epsilon}o_{\epsilon}(1)\quad\text{for}~p\in\partial\Omega_{k}~\text{and}~t\geq 0,

(2.75)

follows from Steiner’s formula (cf.
[
1
,
24
,
25
]
).
Combining (
1.7
), (
1.10
), and (
2.74
)–(
2.75
), we have

\displaystyle\int_{\overline{\Omega}_{k,T,\epsilon}}\!f(\phi_{\epsilon}(x))\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\epsilon}\int_{0}^{T}\!\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\!f(u_{k}(t))\,\mathrm{d}S_{p}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\quad+\epsilon(d-1)\left(\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\!H(p)\,\mathrm{d}S_{p}\right)\left(\int_{0}^{T}\![f^{\prime}(u_{k}(t))v_{k}(t)-tf(u_{k}(t))]\,\mathrm{d}t\right)+\epsilon o_{\epsilon}(1)

\displaystyle=-\sqrt{\epsilon}|\partial\Omega_{k}|\int_{0}^{T}\!u_{k}^{\prime\prime}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\quad+\epsilon(d-1)\left(\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\!H(p)\,\mathrm{d}S_{p}\right)\left(\int_{0}^{T}\![u_{k}^{\prime}(t)-v_{k}^{\prime\prime}(t)+tu_{k}^{\prime\prime}(t)]\,\mathrm{d}t\right)+\epsilon o_{\epsilon}(1)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\epsilon}|\partial\Omega_{k}|(u_{k}^{\prime}(0)-u_{k}^{\prime}(T))

\displaystyle\quad+\epsilon(d-1)\left(\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\!H(p)\,\mathrm{d}S_{p}\right)(Tu_{k}^{\prime}(T)+v_{k}^{\prime}(0)-v_{k}^{\prime}(T))+\epsilon o_{\epsilon}(1),

which completes the proof of (a).

We now prove (b). Note that

\partial\Omega_{k,T,\epsilon,\beta}=\{p-T\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p}\in\Omega\,:\,p\in\partial\Omega_{k}\}\cup\{p-\epsilon^{\beta}\nu_{p}\in\Omega\,:\,p\in\partial\Omega_{k}\}

is the union of two disjoint parallel surfaces to the boundary
\partial\Omega_{k}
.
Integrating (
1.1
) over
\overline{\Omega}_{k,T,\epsilon,\beta}
and applying the divergence theorem, we obtain

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \int_{\overline{\Omega}_{k,T,\epsilon,\beta}}\!f(\phi_{\epsilon}(x))\,\mathrm{d}x&=-\epsilon\int_{\overline{\Omega}_{k,T,\epsilon,\beta}}\!\Delta\phi_{\epsilon}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\\
&=-\epsilon\int_{\partial\Omega_{k,T,\epsilon,\beta}}\partial_{\nu_{x}}\phi_{\epsilon}(x)\,\mathrm{d}S_{x}\\
&=-\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\!(\epsilon\partial_{\nu_{p}}\phi_{\epsilon}(p-T\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p}))\mathcal{J}(T,p)\,\mathrm{d}S_{p}\\
&\quad+\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\!(\epsilon\partial_{\nu_{p}}\phi_{\epsilon}(p-\epsilon^{\beta}\nu_{p}))\mathcal{J}(\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2},p)\,\mathrm{d}S_{p},\end{aligned}

(2.76)

where
\nu_{x}
is the unit outer normal at
x\in\partial\Omega_{k,T,\epsilon,\beta}
with respect to
\Omega_{k,T,\epsilon,\beta}
and

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}(T,p)=1-T\sqrt{\epsilon}(d-1)H(p)+\sqrt{\epsilon}o_{\epsilon}(1),\quad\mathcal{J}(\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2},p)=1-\epsilon^{\beta}(d-1)H(p)+\epsilon^{\beta}o_{\epsilon}(1).

(2.77)

Here we have used Steiner’s formula in differential geometry (cf.
[
1
,
25
]
).
By (
1.6
), we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\quad-\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\!(\epsilon\partial_{\nu_{p}}\phi_{\epsilon}(p-T\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p}))\mathcal{J}(T,p)\,\mathrm{d}S_{p}\\
&=-\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\!(\epsilon\partial_{\nu_{p}}\phi_{\epsilon}(p-T\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p}))[1-T\sqrt{\epsilon}(d-1)H(p)+\sqrt{\epsilon}o_{\epsilon}(1)]\,\mathrm{d}S_{p}\\
&=\sqrt{\epsilon}\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\!\{u_{k}^{\prime}(T)+\sqrt{\epsilon}[(d-1)H(p)v_{k}^{\prime}(T)+o_{\epsilon}(1)]\}[1-T\sqrt{\epsilon}(d-1)H(p)+\sqrt{\epsilon}o_{\epsilon}(1)]\,\mathrm{d}S_{p}\\
&=\sqrt{\epsilon}|\partial\Omega_{k}|u_{k}^{\prime}(T)+\epsilon(d-1)\left(\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\!H(p)\,\mathrm{d}S_{p}\right)(-Tu_{k}^{\prime}(T)+v_{k}^{\prime}(T))+\epsilon o_{\epsilon}(1).\end{aligned}

(2.78)

On the other hand, by
Theorem

1
(b,ii), we have

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\!(\epsilon\partial_{\nu_{p}}\phi_{\epsilon}(p-\epsilon^{\beta}\nu_{p}))\mathcal{J}(\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2},p)\,\mathrm{d}S_{p}\right|\leq\sqrt{\epsilon}|\partial\Omega_{k}|M^{\prime}\exp\left(-M\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2}\right).

(2.79)

Combining (
2.76
)–(
2.79
) with
0<\beta<1/2
, we get (b).
By an argument similar to that used in the proof of (b), one may use (
2.79
) to establish (c).
Therefore, we complete the proof of
Corollary

1
.

3
Proof of
Theorem

2

In this section, we derive the first- and second-order asymptotic expansions of the solution
\phi_{\epsilon}
to equation (
1.2
) with condition (
1.4
) under the charge neutrality condition (
1.3
). Equation (
1.2
) with condition (
1.4
) can be denoted by

\displaystyle-\epsilon\Delta\phi_{\epsilon}

\displaystyle=f_{\epsilon}(\phi_{\epsilon})\quad

\displaystyle\text{in}~\Omega,

(3.1)

\displaystyle\phi_{\epsilon}+\gamma_{k}\sqrt{\epsilon}\partial_{\nu}\phi_{\epsilon}

\displaystyle=\phi_{bd,k}\quad

\displaystyle\text{on}~\partial\Omega_{k}~\text{for}~k=0,1,\dots,K,

(3.2)

where
f_{\epsilon}=f_{\epsilon}(\phi)
is defined as

\displaystyle f_{\epsilon}(\phi)=\sum_{i=1}^{I}\frac{m_{i}z_{i}}{A_{i,\epsilon}}\exp(-z_{i}\phi)\quad\text{for}~\phi\in\mathbb{R},\quad\text{and}\quad A_{i,\epsilon}=\int_{\Omega}\!\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{\epsilon}(y))\,\mathrm{d}y.

(3.3)

In
Section

3.1
, we prove the uniform boundedness of
\phi_{\epsilon}
and show that
f_{\epsilon}
satisfies conditions (A1)–(A2) with the unique zero
\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}
for
\epsilon>0
.
Thus,
f_{\epsilon}(\phi)=f_{0}(\phi)+o_{\epsilon}(1)
for
\phi\in\mathbb{R}
, and (
3.1
) can be approximated by
-\epsilon\Delta\phi_{\epsilon}=f_{0}(\phi_{\epsilon})+o_{\epsilon}(1)
in
\Omega
, where

\displaystyle f_{0}(\phi)=\frac{1}{|\Omega|}\sum_{i=1}^{I}m_{i}z_{i}\exp(-z_{i}(\phi-\phi_{0}^{*}))\quad\text{for}~\phi\in\mathbb{R},

and
\phi_{0}^{*}
is the pointwise limit of
\phi_{\epsilon}
as
\epsilon
tends to zero (see
Remark

4
).
Note that
f_{0}
is strictly decreasing on
\mathbb{R}
and has a unique zero
\phi_{0}^{*}
. Hence we can use the method of
Section

2.1
to prove the first-order asymptotic expansions
\phi_{\epsilon}(p-t\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p})=u_{k}(t)+o_{\epsilon}(1)
and
\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}(p-t\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p})=-\epsilon^{-1/2}(u_{k}^{\prime}(t)\nu_{p}+o_{\epsilon}(1))
for
T>0
,
p\in\partial\Omega_{k}
, and
0\leq t\leq T
, where
u_{k}
is the unique solution to (
1.19
)–(
1.21
) (cf.
Section

3.2
).
To obtain the second-order asymptotic expansions of
\phi_{\epsilon}
and
\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}
, it is essential to show the uniform boundedness of
|\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*}|/\sqrt{\epsilon}
in
Section

3.3
.
We suppose, by contradiction, that
|\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*}|/\sqrt{\epsilon}\to\infty
as
\epsilon\to 0^{+}
, and then derive the asymptotic expansions of the integral terms
A_{i,\epsilon}
in (
3.3
) and the nonlinear term
f_{\epsilon}
.
Then, as in
Sections

2.2
and
2.3
, we obtain the asymptotic expansions of
\phi_{\epsilon}
and
\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}
, and hence the asymptotic expansions of total ionic charge over the regions
\overline{\Omega}_{k,T,\epsilon}
,
\overline{\Omega}_{k,T,\epsilon,\beta}
, and
\overline{\Omega}_{\epsilon,\beta}
(see
Proposition

3.12
), which contradicts the charge neutrality condition (
1.3
).
As a result, we show
\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}=\phi_{0}^{*}+\sqrt{\epsilon}(Q+o_{\epsilon}(1))
(see (
3.76
) in
Remark

6
).
In
Section

3.4
, we use
\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}=\phi_{0}^{*}+\sqrt{\epsilon}(Q+o_{\epsilon}(1))
and follow a similar argument of
Section

3.3
to establish the asymptotic expansions of
A_{i,\epsilon}
and get the further asymptotic expansion of
f_{\epsilon}
:
f_{\epsilon}(\phi)=f_{0}(\phi)+\sqrt{\epsilon}(f_{1}(\phi)+o_{\epsilon}(1))
for
\phi\in\mathbb{R}
(cf. (
3.96
)), where

\displaystyle f_{1}(\phi)=-Qf_{0}^{\prime}(\phi)+\hat{f}_{1}(\phi)\quad\text{for}~\phi\in\mathbb{R}.

(3.4)

Here

\displaystyle\hat{f}_{1}(\phi)=\frac{1}{|\Omega|}\sum_{i=1}^{I}\hat{m}_{i}z_{i}\exp(-z_{i}(\phi-\phi_{0}^{*}))\quad\text{for}~\phi\in\mathbb{R},

\displaystyle\hat{m}_{i}=\frac{m_{i}}{|\Omega|}\sum_{k=0}^{K}|\partial\Omega_{k}|\int_{0}^{\infty}\![1-\exp(-z_{i}(u_{k}(s)-\phi_{0}^{*}))]\,\mathrm{d}s\quad\text{for}~i=1,\dots,I.

Consequently, equation (
3.1
) becomes

-\epsilon\Delta\phi_{\epsilon}=f_{0}(\phi_{\epsilon})+\sqrt{\epsilon}(f_{1}(\phi_{\epsilon})+o_{\epsilon}(1))\quad\text{in}~\Omega,

and we can apply the method of
Section

2.2
to prove the second-order asymptotic expansions of
\phi_{\epsilon}
and
\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}
(cf. (
1.17
)–(
1.18
)).
In
Section

3.4
, using the asymptotic expansions of
\phi_{\epsilon}
and
\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}
, we prove
Corollary

2
and then apply
Corollary

2
to determine the unique value of
Q
, which verifies
Remark

6
.
Therefore, we complete the proof of
Theorem

2
.

3.1
Estimate of the solution
\phi_{\epsilon}
and
f_{\epsilon}

Equation (
1.2
) with the boundary condition (
1.4
) is the Euler–Lagrange equation of the following energy functional

E[\phi]=\frac{\epsilon}{2}\int_{\Omega}\!|\nabla\phi|^{2}\,\mathrm{d}x-\sum_{i=1}^{I}m_{i}\ln\left(\int_{\Omega}\!\exp(-z_{i}\phi)\,\mathrm{d}x\right)+\sqrt{\epsilon}\sum_{k=0}^{K}\frac{1}{2\gamma_{k}}\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\!(\phi-\phi_{bd,k})^{2}\,\mathrm{d}S\quad\text{for}~\phi\in H^{1}(\Omega).

The existence and uniqueness of the smooth solution
\phi_{\epsilon}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega})
was proved in
[
32
]
.
Due to the charge neutrality condition (
1.3
), we prove the uniform boundedness of the solution
\phi_{\epsilon}
as follows.

Proposition 3.1

(Uniform boundedness of
\phi_{\epsilon}
)
.

Assume that
\phi_{bd,k}
are not equal and (
1.3
) holds true.
Let
\phi_{\epsilon}\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\overline{\Omega})
be the unique solution to equations (
3.1
)–(
3.2
).
Then
\phi_{\epsilon}
satisfies

\displaystyle\underline{\phi_{bd}}\leq\phi_{\epsilon}(x)\leq\overline{\phi_{bd}}\quad\text{for}~x\in\overline{\Omega}~\text{and}~\epsilon>0,

(3.5)

where
\underline{\phi_{bd}}=\min\limits_{0\leq k\leq K}\phi_{bd,k}
and
\overline{\phi_{bd}}=\max\limits_{0\leq k\leq K}\phi_{bd,k}
.

Proof.

We first prove that
\phi_{\epsilon}
attains its maximum value at a boundary point.
Suppose by contradiction that
\phi_{\epsilon}
attains the maximum value at an interior point
x_{0}\in\Omega
. It is clear that
\phi_{\epsilon}(x_{0})\geq\phi_{\epsilon}(y)
for
y\in\overline{\Omega}
and
\Delta\phi_{\epsilon}(x_{0})\leq 0
.
Then by (
1.3
) with
m_{i}>0
for
i=1,\dots,I
, (
3.1
) and (
3.3
),

\displaystyle 0\leq-\epsilon\Delta\phi_{\epsilon}(x_{0})=\sum_{i=1}^{I}\frac{m_{i}z_{i}\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{\epsilon}(x_{0}))}{\int_{\Omega}\!\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{\epsilon}(y))\,\mathrm{d}y}=\sum_{i=1}^{I}\frac{m_{i}z_{i}}{\int_{\Omega}\!\exp(-z_{i}(\phi_{\epsilon}(y)-\phi_{\epsilon}(x_{0})))\,\mathrm{d}y}\leq\sum_{i=1}^{I}\frac{m_{i}z_{i}}{|\Omega|}=0,

which implies
\phi_{\epsilon}(x)=\phi_{\epsilon}(x_{0})
for
x\in\overline{\Omega}
.
Hence by (
1.4
),
\phi_{bd,k}
are equal but this contradicts the assumption that
\phi_{bd,k}
are not equal.
Thus,
\phi_{\epsilon}
must attain the maximum value at a boundary point
x_{k_{0}}\in\partial\Omega_{k_{0}}
for some
k_{0}\in\{0,1,\dots,K\}
and
\partial_{\nu}\phi_{\epsilon}(x_{k_{0}})\geq 0
.
Moreover, from (
1.4
) with
\gamma_{k_{0}}>0
, we obtain

\phi_{\epsilon}(x)\leq\phi_{\epsilon}(x_{k_{0}})=\phi_{bd,k_{0}}-\gamma_{k_{0}}\sqrt{\epsilon}\partial_{\nu}\phi_{\epsilon}(x_{k_{0}})\leq\phi_{bd,k_{0}}\leq\overline{\phi_{bd}}\quad\text{for}~x\in\overline{\Omega}.

Similarly, we can prove that
\phi_{\epsilon}
attains the minimum value at a boundary point and hence
\phi_{\epsilon}\geq\underline{\phi_{bd}}
in
\overline{\Omega}
. Therefore, we complete the proof of
Proposition

3.1
.
∎

By (
3.3
) and
Proposition

3.1
, we prove
f_{\epsilon}
satisfies conditions (A1)–(A2).

Proposition 3.2
.

Assume that
\phi_{bd,k}
are not equal and (
1.3
) holds true.
Function
f_{\epsilon}=f_{\epsilon}(\phi)
is strictly decreasing on
\mathbb{R}
and has a unique zero denoted by
\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}\in(\underline{\phi_{bd}},\overline{\phi_{bd}})
(which depends on
\epsilon
). Moreover,
f_{\epsilon}
satisfies

\displaystyle|f_{\epsilon}(\phi)|\leq M\quad\text{for}~\phi\in[\underline{\phi_{bd}},\overline{\phi_{bd}}]~\text{and}~\epsilon>0,

(3.6)

\displaystyle f_{\epsilon}^{\prime}(\phi)\leq-C_{3}^{2}\quad\text{for}~\phi\in\mathbb{R}~\text{and}~\epsilon>0,

(3.7)

where
M
and
C_{3}
are positive constants independent of
\epsilon>0
.

Hereafter
M>0
denotes a generic constant independent of
\epsilon>0
.

Proof.

By (
3.5
), the integral term
A_{i,\epsilon}
satisfies the following estimate:

\displaystyle\alpha_{1}\leq A_{i,\epsilon}=\int_{\Omega}\!\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{\epsilon}(y))\,\mathrm{d}y\leq\alpha_{2}\quad\text{for}~i=1,\dots,I,

(3.8)

where
\alpha_{1}
and
\alpha_{2}
are positive constants independent of
\epsilon
.
Note that
z_{i}\neq 0
for
i=1,\dots,I
.
Then from (
3.3
), we can use (
3.8
) to find

\displaystyle f_{\epsilon}(\phi)=\sum_{i=1}^{I}\frac{m_{i}z_{i}}{A_{i,\epsilon}}\exp(-z_{i}\phi)

\displaystyle=\left(\sum_{z_{i}>0}+\sum_{z_{i}<0}\right)\frac{m_{i}z_{i}}{A_{i,\epsilon}}\exp(-z_{i}\phi)

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{z_{i}>0}\frac{m_{i}z_{i}}{\alpha_{2}}\exp(-z_{i}\phi)+\sum_{z_{i}<0}\frac{m_{i}z_{i}}{\alpha_{1}}\exp(-z_{i}\phi):=g_{1}(\phi)\quad\text{for}~\phi\in\mathbb{R},

and

\displaystyle f_{\epsilon}(\phi)=\left(\sum_{z_{i}>0}+\sum_{z_{i}<0}\right)\frac{m_{i}z_{i}}{A_{i,\epsilon}}\exp(-z_{i}\phi)\leq\sum_{z_{i}>0}\frac{m_{i}z_{i}}{\alpha_{1}}\exp(-z_{i}\phi)+\sum_{z_{i}<0}\frac{m_{i}z_{i}}{\alpha_{2}}\exp(-z_{i}\phi):=g_{2}(\phi)\quad\text{for}~\phi\in\mathbb{R},

which gives

\displaystyle g_{1}(\phi)\leq f_{\epsilon}(\phi)\leq g_{2}(\phi)\quad\text{for}~\phi\in\mathbb{R}.

(3.9)

Clearly,
g_{1}
and
g_{2}
are independent of
\epsilon
, strictly decreasing on
\mathbb{R}
and

\displaystyle\lim_{\phi\to\pm\infty}g_{i}(\phi)=\mp\infty\quad\text{for}~i=1,2.

(3.10)

Hence by (
3.9
),
g_{1}(\overline{\phi_{bd}})\leq f_{\epsilon}(\phi)\leq g_{2}(\underline{\phi_{bd}})
for
\phi\in[\underline{\phi_{bd}},\overline{\phi_{bd}}]
, which implies (
3.6
).
Moreover, by (
3.3
) and (
3.8
), there exists
C_{3}>0
independent of
\epsilon
such that

\displaystyle f_{\epsilon}^{\prime}(\phi)=-\sum_{i=1}^{I}\frac{m_{i}z_{i}^{2}}{A_{i,\epsilon}}\exp(-z_{i}\phi)\leq-\sum_{i=1}^{I}\frac{m_{i}z_{i}^{2}}{\alpha_{1}}\exp(-z_{i}\phi)\leq-C_{3}^{2}\quad\text{for}~\phi\in\mathbb{R},

which gives (
3.7
).
Here
C_{3}^{2}=\alpha_{1}^{-1}\min\left\{\sum\limits_{z_{i}<0}m_{i}z_{i}^{2},\sum\limits_{z_{i}>0}m_{i}z_{i}^{2}\right\}
comes from

\sum_{i=1}^{I}\frac{m_{i}z_{i}^{2}}{\alpha_{1}}\exp(-z_{i}\phi)\geq\sum_{z_{i}<0}\frac{m_{i}z_{i}^{2}}{\alpha_{1}}\quad\text{for}~\phi\geq 0\quad\text{and}\quad\sum_{i=1}^{I}\frac{m_{i}z_{i}^{2}}{\alpha_{1}}\exp(-z_{i}\phi)\geq\sum_{z_{i}>0}\frac{m_{i}z_{i}^{2}}{\alpha_{1}}\quad\text{for}~\phi\leq 0

because of
z_{i}z_{j}<0
for some
i,j\in\{1,\dots,I\}
.
By (
3.7
) and (
3.9
)–(
3.10
),
f_{\epsilon}
is strictly decreasing and has a unique zero
\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}\in\mathbb{R}
.
To complete the proof, it remains to show
\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}\in(\underline{\phi_{bd}},\overline{\phi_{bd}})
for
\epsilon>0
.
If
\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}\geq\overline{\phi_{bd}}
for some
\epsilon>0
, then by (
3.5
),
\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}\geq\phi_{\epsilon}(x)
for
x\in\overline{\Omega}
.
Due to the strict decrease of
f_{\epsilon}
, we have
0=f_{\epsilon}(\phi_{\epsilon}^{*})\leq f_{\epsilon}(\phi_{\epsilon}(x))
for
x\in\overline{\Omega}
.
Then by (
1.3
) and (
3.3
), we obtain

0=\int_{\Omega}\!f_{\epsilon}(\phi_{\epsilon}^{*})\,\mathrm{d}x\leq\int_{\Omega}\!f_{\epsilon}(\phi_{\epsilon}(x))\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\Omega}\!\sum_{i=1}^{I}\frac{m_{i}z_{i}\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{\epsilon}(x))}{\int_{\Omega}\!\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{\epsilon}(y))\,\mathrm{d}y}\,\mathrm{d}x=\sum_{i=1}^{I}m_{i}z_{i}=0,

which implies
f_{\epsilon}(\phi_{\epsilon})\equiv f_{\epsilon}(\phi_{\epsilon}^{*})=0
and hence
\phi_{\epsilon}\equiv\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}
in
\overline{\Omega}
.
By (
1.4
),
\phi_{bd,k}
are equal but this is impossible because
\phi_{bd,k}
are not equal. Thus
\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}<\overline{\phi_{bd}}
for
\epsilon>0
.
Similarly, we can prove
\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}>\underline{\phi_{bd}}
for
\epsilon>0
.
Therefore, the proof of
Proposition

3.2
is complete.
∎

By
Propositions

3.1
and
3.2
, we prove the asymptotic limit of
\phi_{\epsilon}
in
\Omega
as below.

Proposition 3.3
.

There exists a constant
\phi_{0}^{*}\in[\underline{\phi_{bd}},\overline{\phi_{bd}}]
such that
\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}\phi_{\epsilon}(x)=\phi_{0}^{*}
for
x\in\Omega
(up to a subsequence).

Proof.

We begin by proving

\displaystyle|\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}(x)|\leq M^{\prime}/\sqrt{\epsilon}\quad\text{for}~x\in\Omega~\text{and}~0<\epsilon<(\delta(x))^{2},

(3.11)

where
M^{\prime}>0
is generic constant independent of
\epsilon>0
and
\delta(x)=\operatorname{dist}(x,\partial\Omega)
for
x\in\overline{\Omega}
.
Let
x_{0}\in\Omega
be arbitrary and
B_{\sqrt{\epsilon}}(x_{0})\subseteq\Omega
for
0<\epsilon<(\delta(x_{0}))^{2}
.
Set
y=(x-x_{0})/\sqrt{\epsilon}
and
\tilde{\phi}_{\epsilon}(y)=\phi_{\epsilon}(x_{0}+\sqrt{\epsilon}y)
.
Then from (
3.1
), we have
-\Delta\tilde{\phi}_{\epsilon}=f_{\epsilon}(\tilde{\phi}_{\epsilon})
in
B_{1}(0)
.
By the uniform boundedness of
\phi_{\epsilon}
and
f_{\epsilon}(\phi_{\epsilon})
(cf.
Propositions

3.1
and
3.2
), we apply the elliptic
L^{q}
-estimate to obtain
\|\tilde{\phi}_{\epsilon}\|_{W^{2,q}(B_{1/2}(0))}\leq M^{\prime}
for
q>1
.
Then using Sobolev’s compact embedding theorem, we get
\|\tilde{\phi}_{\epsilon}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{1,\alpha}(B_{1/4}(0))}\leq M^{\prime}
for
\alpha\in(0,1)
.
In particular, we have
|\nabla\tilde{\phi}_{\epsilon}(0)|\leq M^{\prime}
, which gives
|\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}(x_{0})|\leq M^{\prime}/\sqrt{\epsilon}
and (
3.11
).

Suppose that
\Omega^{\prime}
is a smooth subdomain of
\Omega
such that
\Omega^{\prime}\subset\subset\Omega
.
We claim that

\displaystyle|\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}(x)|\leq\frac{M^{\prime}}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\exp\left(-\frac{\sqrt{2}C_{3}\operatorname{dist}(\Omega^{\prime},\partial\Omega)}{16\sqrt{\epsilon}}\right)\quad\text{for}~x\in\Omega^{\prime}~\text{and}~0<\epsilon<\epsilon^{*},

(3.12)

where
\epsilon^{*}>0
is a sufficiently small constant depending on
\Omega^{\prime}
and
C_{3}>0
is given in (
3.7
).
Let
x_{1}\in\Omega^{\prime}
be arbitrary.
Clearly,
B_{R_{1}}(x_{1})\subset\subset\Omega
with
R_{1}=\operatorname{dist}(\Omega^{\prime},\partial\Omega)/2
.
Then we use (
3.1
) to obtain

\epsilon\Delta|\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}|^{2}=2\epsilon\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}\left(\frac{\partial^{2}\phi_{\epsilon}}{\partial x^{j}\partial x^{i}}\right)^{2}+2\epsilon\sum_{j=1}^{d}\frac{\partial\phi_{\epsilon}}{\partial x^{j}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{j}}\Delta\phi_{\epsilon}\geq-2\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}\cdot\nabla(f_{\epsilon}(\phi_{\epsilon}))=-2f_{\epsilon}^{\prime}(\phi_{\epsilon})|\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}|^{2}\quad\text{in}~B_{R_{1}}(x_{1}).

Along with (
3.5
) and (
3.7
), we obtain
\epsilon\Delta|\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}|^{2}\geq 2C_{3}^{2}|\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}|^{2}
in
B_{R_{1}}(x_{1})
.
Let
\overline{\phi}_{\epsilon}
be the solution to
\epsilon\Delta\overline{\phi}_{\epsilon}=2C_{3}^{2}\overline{\phi}_{\epsilon}
in
B_{R_{1}}(x_{1})
with the Dirichlet boundary condition
\overline{\phi}_{\epsilon}=\max\limits_{\partial B_{R_{1}}(x_{1})}|\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}|^{2}
.
Then the standard comparison principle yields

\displaystyle|\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}(x)|^{2}\leq|\overline{\phi}_{\epsilon}(x)|\leq 2\left(\max_{\partial B_{R_{1}}(x_{1})}|\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}|^{2}\right)\exp\left(-\frac{\sqrt{2}C_{3}\operatorname{dist}(x,\partial B_{R_{1}}(x_{1}))}{8\sqrt{\epsilon}}\right)

(3.13)

for
x\in\overline{B}_{R_{1}}(x_{1})
and
0<\epsilon<\epsilon^{*}
. By (
3.11
) and (
3.13
), there exists
\epsilon^{*}>0
(depending on
\Omega^{\prime}
) such that

|\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}(x_{1})|\leq\frac{M^{\prime}}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\exp\left(-\frac{\sqrt{2}C_{3}R_{1}}{8\sqrt{\epsilon}}\right)=\frac{M^{\prime}}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\exp\left(-\frac{\sqrt{2}C_{3}\operatorname{dist}(\Omega^{\prime},\partial\Omega)}{16\sqrt{\epsilon}}\right)

for
0<\epsilon<\epsilon^{*}
, which gives (
3.12
).

To complete the proof, let
\{\epsilon_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
be a sequence of positive numbers with
\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\epsilon_{n}=0
.
Since the sequence
\{\phi_{\epsilon_{n}}(x_{1})\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
is bounded (cf.
Proposition

3.1
), by the Bolzano–Weierstrass theorem, there exists a subsequence
\{\epsilon_{n_{k}}\}_{k=1}^{\infty}
of
\{\epsilon_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
such that
\{\phi_{\epsilon_{n_{k}}}(x_{1})\}_{k=1}^{\infty}
converges to a number denoted by
\phi_{0}^{*}
.
Let
y\in\Omega^{\prime}
arbitrarily.
Then by (
3.12
) and
Proposition

3.1
, we have
|\phi_{\epsilon_{n_{k}}}(y)-\phi_{\epsilon_{n_{k}}}(x_{1})|\to 0
as
k\to\infty
, which implies
\displaystyle\lim_{k\to\infty}\phi_{\epsilon_{n_{k}}}(y)=\phi_{0}^{*}
.
Due to the arbitrary choice of
\Omega^{\prime}
, this finalizes the proof of
Proposition

3.3
.
∎

Remark 4
.

In
Section

3.2
, we will prove that
\phi_{0}^{*}\in(\underline{\phi_{bd}},\overline{\phi_{bd}})
is uniquely determined by the algebraic equations (
3.26
)–(
3.27
), which improves upon
Proposition

3.3
to yield

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}\phi_{\epsilon}(x)=\phi_{0}^{*}\quad\text{for}~x\in\Omega.

(3.14)

Moreover, we will use the first-order asymptotic expansion of
\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}
near the boundary to improve the interior exponential-type estimate (
3.12
) (in the proof of
Proposition

3.3
) and obtain the global exponential-type estimate (
3.37
) in
Proposition

3.7
.

By (
3.14
) and the uniform boundedness of
\phi_{\epsilon}
(cf.
Proposition

3.1
), we apply Lebesgue’s dominated convergence theorem to get

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}f_{\epsilon}(\phi)=\sum_{i=1}^{I}\frac{m_{i}z_{i}\exp(-z_{i}\phi)}{\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}\int_{\Omega}\!\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{\epsilon}(y))\,\mathrm{d}y}=\frac{1}{|\Omega|}\sum_{i=1}^{I}m_{i}z_{i}\exp(-z_{i}(\phi-\phi_{0}^{*})):=f_{0}(\phi)

(3.15)

for
\phi\in\mathbb{R}
, and

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}f_{\epsilon}^{\prime}(\phi)=-\sum_{i=1}^{I}\frac{m_{i}z_{i}^{2}\exp(-z_{i}\phi)}{\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}\int_{\Omega}\!\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{\epsilon}(y))\,\mathrm{d}y}=-\frac{1}{|\Omega|}\sum_{i=1}^{I}m_{i}z_{i}^{2}\exp(-z_{i}(\phi-\phi_{0}^{*}))=f_{0}^{\prime}(\phi)

(3.16)

for
\phi\in\mathbb{R}
.
Note that
\phi_{0}^{*}
may depend on the choice of sequence
\{\epsilon_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
, so may
f_{0}
.
By (
1.3
), (
3.7
), and (
3.15
)–(
3.16
), it is clear that
\phi_{0}^{*}
is the unique zero of
f_{0}
, and
f_{0}
satisfies

\displaystyle f_{0}^{\prime}(\phi)\leq-C_{3}^{2}\quad\text{for}~\phi\in\mathbb{R}.

(3.17)

3.2
First-order asymptotic expansion of
\phi_{\epsilon}

To obtain the first-order asymptotic expansion of
\phi_{\epsilon}
near the boundary
\partial\Omega_{k}
, we define

\displaystyle u_{k,p,\epsilon}(z)=\phi_{\epsilon}(\Psi_{p}(\sqrt{\epsilon}z))\quad\text{for}~z\in B_{b/\sqrt{\epsilon}}^{+}~\text{and}~p\in\partial\Omega_{k},

(3.18)

where
\phi_{\epsilon}
is the solution to (
3.1
)–(
3.2
) and
\Psi_{p}
is defined in (
2.1
).
As in (
2.8
)–(
2.9
), we substitute (
2.2
)–(
2.3
) into (
3.1
)–(
3.2
) and get

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}a_{ij}(z)\frac{\partial^{2}u_{k,p,\epsilon}}{\partial z^{i}\partial z^{j}}+\sum_{j=1}^{d}b_{j}(z)\frac{\partial u_{k,p,\epsilon}}{\partial z^{j}}+f_{\epsilon}(u_{k,p,\epsilon})

\displaystyle=0\quad

\displaystyle\text{in}~B_{b/\sqrt{\epsilon}}^{+},

(3.19)

\displaystyle u_{k,p,\epsilon}-\gamma_{k}\partial_{z^{d}}u_{k,p,\epsilon}

\displaystyle=\phi_{bd,k}\quad

\displaystyle\text{on}~\overline{B}_{b/\sqrt{\epsilon}}^{+}\cap\partial\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d},

(3.20)

where
a_{ij}
and
b_{j}
are given in (
2.5
)–(
2.6
) and
f_{\epsilon}
is defined in (
3.3
).
As for
Lemmas

2.3
and
2.4
, we may use the uniform boundedness of
\phi_{\epsilon}
(cf.
Proposition

3.1
) and (
3.15
) to prove

Lemma 3.4
.

For any sequence
\{\epsilon_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
of positive numbers with
\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\epsilon_{n}=0
,
\alpha\in(0,1)
, and
p\in\partial\Omega_{k}
(
k\in\{0,1,\dots,K\}
), there exists a subsequence
\{\epsilon_{nn}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
such that
\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\|u_{k,p,\epsilon_{nn}}-u_{k,p}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{2,\alpha}(\overline{B}_{m}^{+})}=0
for
m\in\mathbb{N}
, where
u_{k,p}\in\mathcal{C}^{2,\alpha}_{\text{loc}}(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{d})
satisfies

\displaystyle\Delta u_{k,p}+f_{0}(u_{k,p})

\displaystyle=0\quad

\displaystyle\text{in}~\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d},

(3.21)

\displaystyle u_{k,p}-\gamma_{k}\partial_{z^{d}}u_{k,p}

\displaystyle=\phi_{bd,k}\quad

\displaystyle\text{on}~\partial\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}.

(3.22)

Moreover,
u_{k,p}
satisfies the following exponential-type estimate

\displaystyle|u_{k,p}(z)-\phi_{0}^{*}|\leq 2(\overline{\phi_{bd}}-\underline{\phi_{bd}})\exp(-C_{3}z^{d}/8)

(3.23)

for
z=(z^{\prime},z^{d})\in\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{d}
and
z^{d}\geq 2(d-1)/C_{3}
,
where
\phi_{0}^{*}
is defined in
Proposition

3.3
and
C_{3}
is the positive constant given in (
3.7
).

From
Lemma

3.4
, the solution
u_{k,p}
to (
3.21
)–(
3.22
) may, a priori, depend on the sequence
\{\epsilon_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
and on the point
p\in\partial\Omega_{k}
.
To establish the independence, we first use the moving plane arguments (as in
Proposition

2.5
) to prove that
u_{k,p}
satisfies
u_{k,p}(z)=u_{k}(z^{d})
for
z=(z^{\prime},z^{d})\in\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{d}
, where
u_{k}
is the unique solution to (
1.19
)–(
1.21
), and
u_{k}
is independent of the point
p\in\partial\Omega_{k}
.
However, unlike
Section

2
, the solution
u_{k}
to (
1.19
)–(
1.21
) may still depend on the sequence
\{\epsilon_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
because
\phi_{0}^{*}
(the unique zero of
f_{0}
) may depend on the sequence
\{\epsilon_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
(see
Proposition

3.3
).
To remove the dependence on the sequence, we prove that
\phi_{0}^{*}
is the unique solution to the algebraic equations (
3.26
)–(
3.27
), which are independent of the sequence
\{\epsilon_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
, in
Proposition

3.6
.
Consequently, as in (
2.19
)–(
2.20
), we may apply
Propositions

3.5
and
3.6
to prove that
u_{k,p,\epsilon}
(defined in (
3.18
)) satisfies

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}\|u_{k,p,\epsilon}-u_{k}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{2,\alpha}(\overline{B}_{m}^{+})}=0\quad\text{for}~m\in\mathbb{N}~\text{and}~\alpha\in(0,1),

(3.24)

and

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}u_{k,p,\epsilon}(z)=u_{k}(z^{d})\quad\text{for}~z=(z^{\prime},z^{d})\in\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{d},

(3.25)

which improve the results of
Lemma

3.4
. Below are the details.

Proposition 3.5
.

For
p\in\partial\Omega_{k}
and
k\in\{0,1,\dots,K\}
, the solution
u_{k,p}
to (
3.21
)–(
3.22
) satisfies

(a)

u_{k,p}
depends only on the variable
z^{d}
, i.e.,
u_{k,p}(z)=u_{k,p}(z^{d})
for
z=(z^{\prime},z^{d})\in\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{d}
.

(b)

u_{k,p}
is independent of
p
and depends only on
k
, i.e.,
u_{k,p}(z^{d})=u_{k}(z^{d})
for
z^{d}\in[0,\infty)
, where
u_{k}
is the unique solution to (
1.19
)–(
1.21
).

The proof of
Proposition

3.5
is similar to that of
Proposition

2.5

because (
3.21
)–(
3.22
) have the same form as (
2.10
)–(
2.11
) with
f=f_{0}
, and
f_{0}
satisfies (A1)–(A2) (cf.
Proposition

3.3
).

For the uniqueness of
\phi_{0}^{*}
in
Remark

4
, we use
Propositions

3.3
and
3.5
to prove

Proposition 3.6
.

The value of
\phi_{0}^{*}\in(\underline{\phi_{bd}},\overline{\phi_{bd}})
, defined in
Proposition

3.3
, is uniquely determined by the following equations.

\displaystyle\phi_{bd,k}-u_{k}(0)=\operatorname{sgn}(\phi_{bd,k}-\phi_{0}^{*})\gamma_{k}\sqrt{\frac{2}{|\Omega|}\sum_{i=1}^{I}m_{i}[\exp(-z_{i}(u_{k}(0)-\phi_{0}^{*}))-1]}\quad\text{for}~k=0,1,\dots,K,

(3.26)

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{K}|\partial\Omega_{k}|u_{k}^{\prime}(0)=\sum_{k=0}^{K}|\partial\Omega_{k}|\frac{\phi_{bd,k}-u_{k}(0)}{\gamma_{k}}=0,

(3.27)

where
u_{k}
is the unique solution to (
1.19
)–(
1.21
).

Proof.

It is clear that (
3.26
) follows from (
B.10
) (in
Appendix

B
) with
\Phi_{bd}=\phi_{bd,k}
,
U_{0}=u_{k}(0)
,
\phi^{*}=\phi_{0}^{*}
,
\gamma=\gamma_{k}
and
F(\phi)=F_{0}(\phi)=|\Omega|^{-1}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{I}m_{i}(1-\exp(-z_{i}(\phi-\phi_{0}^{*})))
.
On the other hand, we integrate (
3.1
) over
\Omega
and apply the divergence theorem to get

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{K}\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\!\frac{\phi_{bd,k}-\phi_{\epsilon}}{\gamma_{k}}\,\mathrm{d}S=\sqrt{\epsilon}\int_{\partial\Omega}\!\partial_{\nu}\phi_{\epsilon}\,\mathrm{d}S=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\int_{\Omega}\!\Delta\phi_{\epsilon}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\int_{\Omega}\!f_{\epsilon}(\phi_{\epsilon})\,\mathrm{d}x=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\sum_{i=1}^{I}m_{i}z_{i}=0.

(3.28)

Here we have used the conditions (
1.3
) and (
1.4
).
By (
2.4
) and (
3.25
),
\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}\phi_{\epsilon}(p)=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}u_{k,p,\epsilon}(0)=u_{k}(0)
for
p\in\partial\Omega_{k}
. Thus, letting
\epsilon\to 0^{+}
, (
3.28
) implies (
3.27
). Hence
\phi_{0}^{*}
satisfies (
3.26
)–(
3.27
).

We now prove
\phi_{0}^{*}\in(\underline{\phi_{bd}},\overline{\phi_{bd}})
. Recall that
\phi_{0}^{*}\in[\underline{\phi_{bd}},\overline{\phi_{bd}}]
(cf.
Proposition

3.3
).
If
\phi_{0}^{*}=\overline{\phi_{bd}}
, then
\phi_{0}^{*}\geq\phi_{bd,k}
for
k=0,1,\dots,K
.
When
\phi_{0}^{*}=\phi_{bd,k}
,
u_{k}\equiv\phi_{0}^{*}
on
[0,\infty)
; when
\phi_{0}^{*}>\phi_{bd,k}
,
u_{k}
is strictly increasing on
[0,\infty)
and
u_{k}(0)>\phi_{bd,k}
(cf.
Proposition

B.1
).
Thus,
u_{k}(0)\geq\phi_{bd,k}
for
k=0,1,\dots,K
.
Along with (
3.27
),
u_{k}(0)=\phi_{bd,k}
for
k=0,1,\dots,K
, which means
\phi_{0}^{*}=\phi_{bd,k}
for
k=0,1,\dots,K
.
This cannot happen because
\phi_{bd,k}
are not equal.
Hence
\phi_{0}^{*}<\overline{\phi_{bd}}
.
Similarly, we can prove
\phi_{0}^{*}\neq\underline{\phi_{bd}}
.
Consequently,
\phi_{0}^{*}\in(\underline{\phi_{bd}},\overline{\phi_{bd}})
.

To complete the proof, it remains to show that algebraic equations (
3.26
)–(
3.27
) admit a unique solution
\phi_{0}^{*}
.
Suppose that there exist
\{\epsilon_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
and
\{\tilde{\epsilon}_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
such that
\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\phi_{\epsilon_{n}}^{*}=\phi_{0}^{*}
and
\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\phi_{\tilde{\epsilon}_{n}}^{*}=\tilde{\phi}_{0}^{*}
.
For
k=0,1,\dots,K
, let
u_{k}
be the solutions to

\displaystyle u_{k}^{\prime\prime}+f_{0}(u_{k})=0\quad\text{in}~(0,\infty),

\displaystyle u_{k}(0)-\gamma_{k}u_{k}^{\prime}(0)=\phi_{bd,k},

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}u_{k}(t)=\phi_{0}^{*},

and let
\tilde{u}_{k}
be the solutions to

\displaystyle\tilde{u}_{k}^{\prime\prime}+\tilde{f}_{0}(\tilde{u}_{k})=0\quad\text{in}~(0,\infty),

\displaystyle\tilde{u}_{k}(0)-\gamma_{k}\tilde{u}_{k}^{\prime}(0)=\phi_{bd,k},

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\tilde{u}_{k}(t)=\tilde{\phi}_{0}^{*}.

where

f_{0}(\phi)=\frac{1}{|\Omega|}\sum_{i=1}^{I}m_{i}z_{i}\exp(-z_{i}(\phi-\phi_{0}^{*}))\quad\text{and}\quad\tilde{f}_{0}(\phi)=\frac{1}{|\Omega|}\sum_{i=1}^{I}m_{i}z_{i}\exp(-z_{i}(\phi-\tilde{\phi}_{0}^{*}))\quad\text{for}~\phi\in\mathbb{R}.

As
\phi_{0}^{*}=\tilde{\phi}_{0}^{*}
, it is clear that
f_{0}=\tilde{f}_{0}
and
u_{k}=\tilde{u}_{k}
for
k=0,1,\dots,K
.
Note that
(u_{0}(0),u_{1}(0),\dots,u_{K}(0),\phi_{0}^{*})
and
(\tilde{u}_{0}(0),\tilde{u}_{1}(0),\dots,\tilde{u}_{K}(0),\tilde{\phi}_{0}^{*})
satisfy (
3.26
)–(
3.27
).
Consequently, the values of
u_{k}(0)
and
\tilde{u}_{k}(0)
depend on
\phi_{0}^{*}
and
\tilde{\phi}_{0}^{*}
, respectively.
Now we prove

Claim 2
.

\phi_{0}^{*}>\tilde{\phi}_{0}^{*}
(
\phi_{0}^{*}<\tilde{\phi}_{0}^{*}
, resp.) if and only if
u_{k}(0)>\tilde{u}_{k}(0)
(
u_{k}(0)<\tilde{u}_{k}(0)
, resp.) for all
k=0,1,\dots,K
.

Proof of
2
.

We first suppose
\phi_{0}^{*}>\tilde{\phi}_{0}^{*}
.
Let

g(\phi,s)=\frac{1}{|\Omega|}\sum_{i=1}^{I}m_{i}z_{i}\exp(-z_{i}(\phi-s))\quad\text{for}~\phi,s\in\mathbb{R}.

It is obvious that
g(\phi,\phi_{0}^{*})=f_{0}(\phi)
and
g(\phi,\tilde{\phi}_{0}^{*})=\tilde{f}_{0}(\phi)
for
\phi\in\mathbb{R}
.
Since

\partial_{s}g(\phi,s)=\frac{1}{|\Omega|}\sum_{i=1}^{I}m_{i}z_{i}^{2}\exp(-z_{i}(\phi-s))>0\quad\text{for}~s\in\mathbb{R},

the function
g
is increasing in
s
for
\phi\in\mathbb{R}
.
Hence by the assumption
\phi_{0}^{*}>\tilde{\phi}_{0}^{*}
, we get

\displaystyle f_{0}(\phi)>\tilde{f}_{0}(\phi)\quad\text{for}~\phi\in\mathbb{R}.

(3.29)

Suppose, to the contrary, that
u_{k_{0}}(0)\leq\tilde{u}_{k_{0}}(0)
for some
k_{0}\in\{0,1,\dots,K\}
.
Let
\overline{u}_{k_{0}}=u_{k_{0}}-\tilde{u}_{k_{0}}
on
[0,\infty)
.
Then it is easy to verify that
\overline{u}_{k_{0}}(0)\leq 0
and

\displaystyle\overline{u}_{k_{0}}^{\prime\prime}+f_{0}(u_{k_{0}})-\tilde{f}_{0}(\tilde{u}_{k_{0}})=0\quad\text{in}~(0,\infty),

(3.30)

\displaystyle\overline{u}_{k_{0}}(0)-\gamma_{k}\overline{u}_{k_{0}}^{\prime}(0)=0,

(3.31)

\displaystyle\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\overline{u}_{k_{0}}(t)=\phi_{0}^{*}-\tilde{\phi}_{0}^{*}>0.

(3.32)

Since
\overline{u}_{k_{0}}(0)\leq 0
, we have
\overline{u}_{k_{0}}^{\prime}(0)\leq 0
by (
3.31
).
Moreover, since
\tilde{f}_{0}
is strictly decreasing, we use (
3.29
)–(
3.30
) to obtain

\overline{u}_{k_{0}}^{\prime\prime}(0)=\tilde{f}_{0}(\tilde{u}_{k_{0}}(0))-f_{0}(u_{k_{0}}(0))\leq\tilde{f}_{0}(u_{k_{0}}(0))-f_{0}(u_{k_{0}}(0))<0.

Thus, by (
3.32
), there exists
t_{1}\in(0,\infty)
such that
\overline{u}_{k_{0}}(t)<0
on
(0,t_{1})
,
\overline{u}_{k_{0}}(t_{1})=0
and
\overline{u}_{k_{0}}^{\prime}(t_{1})\geq 0
.
Integrating (
3.30
) over
[0,t_{1}]
, we get

\displaystyle\overline{u}_{k_{0}}^{\prime}(t_{1})-\overline{u}_{k_{0}}^{\prime}(0)+\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\![f_{0}(u_{k_{0}}(s))-\tilde{f}_{0}(\tilde{u}_{k_{0}}(s))]\,\mathrm{d}s=0,

which implies

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t_{1}}\![f_{0}(u_{k_{0}}(s))-\tilde{f}_{0}(\tilde{u}_{k_{0}}(s))]\,\mathrm{d}s=\overline{u}_{k_{0}}^{\prime}(0)-\overline{u}_{k_{0}}^{\prime}(t_{1})\leq 0.

(3.33)

Since
f_{0}
is strictly decreasing on
\mathbb{R}
(cf. (
3.17
)) and
u_{k_{0}}(t)-\tilde{u}_{k_{0}}(t)=\overline{u}_{k_{0}}(t)<0
on
(0,t_{1})
, it follows that

\displaystyle f_{0}(u_{k_{0}}(t))-\tilde{f}_{0}(\tilde{u}_{k_{0}}(t))>f_{0}(\tilde{u}_{k_{0}}(t))-\tilde{f}_{0}(\tilde{u}_{k_{0}}(t))>0\quad\text{for}~0<t<t_{1},

which contradicts (
3.33
).
Here we have used (
3.29
) in the last inequality.
Therefore, we arrive at
\overline{u}_{k}(0)>0
, which means
u_{k}(0)>\tilde{u}_{k}(0)
for all
k=0,1,\dots,K
.
Similarly, we can prove
\phi_{0}^{*}<\tilde{\phi}_{0}^{*}
implies
u_{k}(0)<\tilde{u}_{k}(0)
and thus complete proof of
2
.
∎

To complete the proof of
Proposition

3.6
, we combine
2
and (
3.27
) for
u_{k}
and
\tilde{u}_{k}
to get the following contradiction:

0=\sum_{k=0}^{K}|\partial\Omega_{k}|\frac{\phi_{bd,k}-u_{k}(0)}{\gamma_{k}}\neq\sum_{k=0}^{K}|\partial\Omega_{k}|\frac{\phi_{bd,k}-\tilde{u}_{k}(0)}{\gamma_{k}}=0\quad\text{if}~\phi_{0}^{*}\neq\tilde{\phi}_{0}^{*},

which implies
\phi_{0}^{*}=\tilde{\phi}_{0}^{*}
and
u_{k}(0)=\tilde{u}_{k}(0)
.
Therefore, we complete the proof of
Proposition

3.6
.
∎

Consequently, we can use (
2.1
), (
2.4
), (
3.18
), and (
3.24
)–(
3.25
) to obtain the first-order asymptotic expansions of
\phi_{\epsilon}
and
\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}
:

\displaystyle\phi_{\epsilon}(p-t\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p})=u_{k}(t)+o_{\epsilon}(1),

(3.34)

\displaystyle\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}(p-t\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p})=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}(u_{k}^{\prime}(t)\nu_{p}+o_{\epsilon}(1))

(3.35)

for
0\leq t\leq T
as
\epsilon\to 0^{+}
, where
T>0
,
k\in\{0,1,\dots,K\}
, and
p\in\partial\Omega_{k}
(as in (
2.39
)–(
2.40
)).

To derive the second-order asymptotic expansions of
\phi_{\epsilon}
and
\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}
and capture the asymptotic behavior of the term
o_{\epsilon}(1)/\sqrt{\epsilon}
in the subsequent analysis, it is crucial to establish the following exponential-type estimates of
\phi_{\epsilon}
and
\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}
analogously to
Proposition

2.6
:

Proposition 3.7

(Exponential-type estimates of
\phi_{\epsilon}
and
\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}
)
.

Under the hypothesis of
Theorem

2
, let
\Omega
satisfy the uniform interior sphere condition, i.e., there exists
R>0
such that
B_{R}(p-R\nu_{p})\subseteq\Omega
and
\partial B_{R}(p-R\nu_{p})\cap\partial\Omega=\{p\}
for
p\in\partial\Omega
, where
\nu_{p}
is the unit outer normal of
\partial\Omega
at
p
.
Then we have

\displaystyle|\phi_{\epsilon}(x)-\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}|\leq 2(\overline{\phi_{bd}}-\underline{\phi_{bd}})\exp\left(-\frac{C_{3}\delta(x)}{8\sqrt{\epsilon}}\right),

(3.36)

\displaystyle|\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}|\leq\frac{M^{\prime}}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\exp\left(-\frac{M\delta(x)}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\right)

(3.37)

for
x\in\overline{\Omega}
and
0<\epsilon<\epsilon^{*}
, where
C_{3}
(given in (
3.7
)) is independent of
\epsilon
and
\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}
is defined in
Proposition

3.2
.

Proof.

As in the proof of
Proposition

2.6
, the uniform interior sphere condition of
\Omega
ensures that there exists
B_{R_{0}}(x_{0})\subseteq\Omega
with
R_{0}\geq R
; hence (
2.43
) holds.
To show (
3.36
), we let
\phi_{\epsilon}^{\pm}
be the solutions to

\displaystyle-\epsilon\Delta\phi_{\epsilon}^{\pm}

\displaystyle=f_{\epsilon}(\phi_{\epsilon}^{\pm})\quad

\displaystyle\text{in}~B_{R_{0}}(x_{0}),

(3.38)

\displaystyle\phi_{\epsilon}^{\pm}

\displaystyle=\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}\pm(\overline{\phi_{bd}}-\underline{\phi_{bd}})\quad

\displaystyle\text{on}~\partial B_{R_{0}}(x_{0}),

(3.39)

The existence of solutions to (
3.38
)–(
3.39
) follows from the standard variational method because of the strict decrease of
f_{\epsilon}
(cf.
Proposition

3.2
).
Following the argument of
Proposition

2.6
for (
2.46
), with replacing
f
by
f_{\epsilon}
, we obtain
\phi_{\epsilon}^{-}\leq\phi_{\epsilon}\leq\phi_{\epsilon}^{+}
in
\overline{B}_{R_{0}}(x_{0})
.
Then by (
2.43
) and
Proposition

A.4
in
Appendix

A
with
M=C_{3}
,
|\Phi_{bd}-\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}|=\overline{\phi_{bd}}-\underline{\phi_{bd}}
and
B_{R}(0)=B_{R_{0}}(x_{0})
, we have

\displaystyle|\phi_{\epsilon}^{\pm}(x)-\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}|\leq 2(\overline{\phi_{bd}}-\underline{\phi_{bd}})\exp\left(-\frac{C_{3}\delta(x)}{8\sqrt{\epsilon}}\right)\quad\text{for}~x\in\overline{B}_{R_{0}}(x_{0})~\text{and}~0<\epsilon<\epsilon^{*},

(3.40)

which implies (
3.36
).
As in (
2.50
), we can use (
3.35
) to improve the estimate (
3.11
) as
|\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}(x)|\leq M^{\prime}/\sqrt{\epsilon}
for
x\in\overline{\Omega}
and
0<\epsilon<(\delta(x))^{2}
.
Then (
3.37
) follows from (
3.7
) and the standard comparison principle on (
2.51
) (with replacing
f
by
f_{\epsilon}
).
Therefore, we complete the proof of
Proposition

3.7
.
∎

Recall that
\delta(x)=\operatorname{dist}(x,\partial\Omega)
.
By
Proposition

3.7
, it is clear that
Theorem

2
(b) holds true.

3.3
Uniform boundedness of
|\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*}|/\sqrt{\epsilon}

From (
3.14
)–(
3.15
) and (
3.36
), the first-order asymptotic expansions of
\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}
,
A_{i,\epsilon}
, and
f_{\epsilon}
are represented by

\displaystyle\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}

\displaystyle=\phi_{0}^{*}+o_{\epsilon}(1),

\displaystyle A_{i,\epsilon}

\displaystyle=|\Omega|\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{0}^{*})+o_{\epsilon}(1)

\displaystyle\quad\text{for}~i=1,\dots,I,

\displaystyle f_{\epsilon}(\phi)

\displaystyle=f_{0}(\phi)+o_{\epsilon}(1)

\displaystyle\quad\text{for}~\phi\in\mathbb{R},

where
A_{i,\epsilon}=\int_{\Omega}\!\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{\epsilon}(y))\,\mathrm{d}y
,
f_{\epsilon}(\phi)=\sum_{i=1}^{I}(m_{i}z_{i}/A_{i,\epsilon})\exp(-z_{i}\phi)
, and
f_{0}(\phi)
is given in (
1.28
).
To obtain the second-order asymptotic expansion of
\phi_{\epsilon}
, we require the second-order asymptotic expansions of
f_{\epsilon}
and
A_{i,\epsilon}
.
Thus, it is necessary to show the uniform boundedness of
(\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*})/\sqrt{\epsilon}
.

Suppose by contradiction that there exists a sequence
\{\epsilon_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
such that
\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}|\phi_{\epsilon_{n}}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*}|/\sqrt{\epsilon_{n}}=\infty
and
\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\epsilon_{n}=0
.
For notational convenience, we henceforth drop the subscript
n
in this section so the assumption becomes

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}\frac{|\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*}|}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}=\infty.

(3.41)

A direct computation gives

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} f_{1,\epsilon}(\phi)&:=\frac{f_{\epsilon}(\phi)-f_{0}(\phi)}{\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*}}\\
&=\frac{1}{\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*}}\sum_{i=1}^{I}m_{i}z_{i}\left(\frac{\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{0}^{*})}{A_{i,\epsilon}}-\frac{1}{|\Omega|}\right)\exp(-z_{i}(\phi-\phi_{0}^{*}))\\
&=-\sum_{i=1}^{I}\frac{m_{i}z_{i}B_{i,\epsilon}}{|\Omega|[|\Omega|\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{0}^{*})+(\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*})B_{i,\epsilon}]}\exp(-z_{i}(\phi-\phi_{0}^{*}))\quad\text{for}~\phi\in\mathbb{R},\end{aligned}

(3.42)

where

\displaystyle B_{i,\epsilon}=\frac{A_{i,\epsilon}-|\Omega|\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{0}^{*})}{\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*}}=\int_{\Omega}\!\frac{\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{\epsilon}(y))-\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{0}^{*})}{\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*}}\,\mathrm{d}y

(3.43)

for
i=1,\dots,I
and
\epsilon>0
.
Note that
B_{i,\epsilon}
can be expressed by

\displaystyle B_{i,\epsilon}=J_{i,1}+\sum_{k=0}^{K}J_{i,k,2}\quad\text{for}~i=1,\dots,I,

(3.44)

where

\displaystyle J_{i,1}=\int_{\overline{\Omega}_{\epsilon,\beta}}\!\frac{\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{\epsilon}(y))-\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{0}^{*})}{\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*}}\,\mathrm{d}y,

(3.45)

\displaystyle J_{i,k,2}=\int_{\Omega_{k,\epsilon,\beta}}\!\frac{\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{\epsilon}(y))-\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{0}^{*})}{\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*}}\,\mathrm{d}y\quad\text{for}~k=0,1,\dots,K.

(3.46)

Here
\overline{\Omega}_{\epsilon,\beta}=\{x\in\Omega\,:\,\delta(x)\geq\epsilon^{\beta}\}
and
\Omega_{k,\epsilon,\beta}=\{x\in\Omega\,:\delta_{k}(x)<\epsilon^{\beta}\}
for
0<\beta<1/2
and
k=0,1,\dots,K
. Clearly,
\Omega_{k,\epsilon,\beta}
are disjoint for sufficiently small
\epsilon>0
.
Then we may apply (
3.36
), (
3.41
), and (
3.43
) to get

Lemma 3.8
.

B_{i,\epsilon}=-z_{i}\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{0}^{*})|\Omega|+o_{\epsilon}(1)
for
i=1,\dots,I
.

Henceforth
\epsilon^{*}>0
is a sufficiently small constant, and
M
and
M^{\prime}
are generic constants independent of
\epsilon
.

Proof.

Fix
i\in\{1,\dots,I\}
.
In view of (
3.44
), it suffices to determine the limits of
J_{i,1}
and
J_{i,k,2}
.
We begin with an analysis of
J_{i,1}
when
z_{i}>0
.
By (
3.36
), we have

\exp\left[-z_{i}\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-z_{i}M^{\prime}\exp\left(-\frac{M\delta(x)}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\right)\right]\leq\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{\epsilon}(x))\leq\exp\left[-z_{i}\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}+z_{i}M^{\prime}\exp\left(-\frac{M\delta(x)}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\right)\right]

for
x\in\overline{\Omega}
and
0<\epsilon<\epsilon^{*}
, which implies

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{0}^{*})\left\{\exp[-z_{i}M^{\prime}\exp(-M\delta(x)/\sqrt{\epsilon})]\exp[-z_{i}(\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*})]-1\right\}\\
\leq&\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{\epsilon}(x))-\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{0}^{*})\\
\leq&\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{0}^{*})\left\{\exp[z_{i}M^{\prime}\exp(-M\delta(x)/\sqrt{\epsilon})]\exp[-z_{i}(\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*})]-1\right\}\end{aligned}

(3.47)

for
x\in\overline{\Omega}
and
0<\epsilon<\epsilon^{*}
.
For
x\in\overline{\Omega}_{\epsilon,\beta}
(i.e.,
\delta(x)\geq\epsilon^{\beta}
) and
0<\epsilon<\epsilon^{*}
, (
3.47
) becomes

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{0}^{*})\left\{\exp[-z_{i}M^{\prime}\exp(-M\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2})]\exp[-z_{i}(\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*})]-1\right\}\\
\leq&\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{\epsilon}(x))-\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{0}^{*})\\
\leq&\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{0}^{*})\left\{\exp[z_{i}M^{\prime}\exp(-M\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2})]\exp[-z_{i}(\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*})]-1\right\}.\end{aligned}

(3.48)

Now we can combine (
3.45
) and (
3.48
) to get

\displaystyle\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{0}^{*})|\overline{\Omega}_{\epsilon,\beta}|\frac{\exp[-z_{i}M^{\prime}\exp(-M\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2})]\exp[-z_{i}(\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*})]-1}{\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle J_{i,1}\leq\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{0}^{*})|\overline{\Omega}_{\epsilon,\beta}|\frac{\exp[z_{i}M^{\prime}\exp(-M\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2})]\exp[-z_{i}(\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*})]-1}{\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*}}.

Along with the fact that

\displaystyle\quad\lim_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}\frac{\exp[z_{i}M^{\prime}\exp(-M\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2})]\exp[-z_{i}(\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*})]-1}{\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}\frac{\exp[z_{i}M^{\prime}\exp(-M\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2})-z_{i}(\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*})]-1}{\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*}}=-z_{i},

which follows from (
3.41
) and
0<\beta<1/2
, we obtain

\displaystyle J_{i,1}=-z_{i}\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{0}^{*})|\Omega|+o_{\epsilon}(1)\quad\text{for}~i=1,\dots,I.

(3.49)

For the case of
z_{i}<0
, we can follow a similar argument to get (
3.49
).

Now it remains to estimate
J_{i,k,2}
.
Fix
k\in\{0,1,\dots,K\}
.
For
y\in\overline{\Omega}_{k,\epsilon,\beta}
and sufficiently small
\epsilon>0
, there exist unique
p\in\partial\Omega_{k}
and
0\leq s\leq\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2}
such that
y=p-s\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p}
.
By the coarea formula (cf.
[
18
,
37
]
), (
3.46
) becomes

\displaystyle J_{i,k,2}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega_{k,\epsilon,\beta}}\!\frac{\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{\epsilon}(y))-\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{0}^{*})}{\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*}}\,\mathrm{d}y

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{\epsilon}}{\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*}}\int_{0}^{\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2}}\!\!\!\!\!\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\![\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{\epsilon}(p-s\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p}))-\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{0}^{*})]\mathcal{J}(s,p)\,\mathrm{d}S_{p}\,\mathrm{d}s

for
i=1,\dots,I
and
k=0,1,\dots,K
.
Along with (
2.75
), we have

\displaystyle|J_{i,k,2}|\leq\frac{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}{|\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*}|}\int_{0}^{\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2}}\!\!\!\!\!\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\!|\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{\epsilon}(p-s\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p}))-\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{0}^{*})|\,\mathrm{d}S_{p}\,\mathrm{d}s.

(3.50)

From
Propositions

3.1
and
3.3
,
|\phi_{\epsilon}-\phi_{0}^{*}|
is uniformly bounded in
\overline{\Omega}
and
\epsilon>0
, which implies that there exists
M>1
such that
|\exp[-z_{i}(\phi_{\epsilon}(x)-\phi_{0}^{*})]-1|\leq M|\phi_{\epsilon}(x)-\phi_{0}^{*}|
for
x\in\overline{\Omega}
and
0<\epsilon<\epsilon^{*}
.
Then by (
3.36
), we get

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} |\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{\epsilon}(x))-\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{0}^{*})|&=\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{0}^{*})|\exp[-z_{i}(\phi_{\epsilon}(x)-\phi_{0}^{*})]-1|\\
&\leq M^{\prime}\exp\left(-\frac{M\delta(x)}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\right)+M^{\prime}|\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*}|\quad\text{for}~x\in\overline{\Omega}~\text{and}~0<\epsilon<\epsilon^{*}.\end{aligned}

(3.51)

Combining (
3.41
), (
3.50
) and (
3.51
), we arrive at

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} |J_{i,k,2}|&\leq\frac{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}{|\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*}|}\int_{0}^{\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2}}\!\!\!\!\!\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\![M^{\prime}\exp(-Ms)+M^{\prime}|\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*}|]\,\mathrm{d}S_{p}\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&=\frac{M^{\prime}\sqrt{\epsilon}}{|\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*}|}(1-\exp(M\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2}))+M^{\prime}\epsilon^{\beta}\to 0\quad\text{as}~\epsilon\to 0^{+},\end{aligned}

(3.52)

for
i=1,\dots,I
and
k=0,1,\dots,K
.
Thus, combining (
3.44
), (
3.49
), and (
3.52
), we complete the proof of
Lemma

3.8
.
∎

Applying
Lemma

3.8
to (
3.42
), we obtain

\displaystyle f_{1,\epsilon}(\phi)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i=1}^{I}\frac{m_{i}z_{i}B_{i,\epsilon}}{|\Omega|[|\Omega|\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{0}^{*})+(\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*})B_{i,\epsilon}]}\exp(-z_{i}(\phi-\phi_{0}^{*}))

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\Omega|}\sum_{i=1}^{I}m_{i}z_{i}^{2}\exp(-z_{i}(\phi-\phi_{0}^{*}))+o_{\epsilon}(1)=-f_{0}^{\prime}(\phi)+o_{\epsilon}(1),

which implies

\displaystyle f_{\epsilon}(\phi)=f_{0}(\phi)-(\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*})f_{0}^{\prime}(\phi)+(\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*})o_{\epsilon}(1)\quad\text{for}~\phi\in\mathbb{R}.

(3.53)

Regarding (
2.54
), we now define

\displaystyle\varphi_{k,\epsilon}=\frac{\phi_{\epsilon}(x)-u_{k}(\delta_{k}(x)/\sqrt{\epsilon})}{\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*}}\quad\text{for}~x\in\overline{\Omega}_{k,\epsilon,\beta}~\text{and}~k=0,1,\dots,K,

(3.54)

where
u_{k}
is the unique solution to (
1.19
)–(
1.21
) and
\delta_{k}(x)=\operatorname{dist}(x,\partial\Omega_{k})
for
k=0,1,\dots,K
.
Then by (
1.2
), (
1.19
), (
2.58
), and (
3.53
)–(
3.54
),
\varphi_{k,\epsilon}
satisfies

\displaystyle\epsilon\Delta\varphi_{k,\epsilon}+c_{\epsilon}(x)\varphi_{k,\epsilon}=f_{0}^{\prime}(u_{k}(\delta_{k}(x)/\sqrt{\epsilon}))+o_{\epsilon}(1)\quad\text{for}~x\in\overline{\Omega}_{k,\epsilon,\beta},

(3.55)

where

c_{\epsilon}(x)=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{f_{\epsilon}(\phi_{\epsilon}(x))-f_{\epsilon}(u_{k}(\delta_{k}(x)/\sqrt{\epsilon}))}{\phi_{\epsilon}(x)-u_{k}(\delta_{k}(x)/\sqrt{\epsilon})}&\text{if}~\phi_{\epsilon}(x)\neq u_{k}(\delta_{k}(x)/\sqrt{\epsilon});\\
f_{\epsilon}^{\prime}(\phi_{\epsilon}(x))&\text{if}~\phi_{\epsilon}(x)=u_{k}(\delta_{k}(x)/\sqrt{\epsilon}).\end{cases}

For the boundary condition of
\varphi_{k,\epsilon}
, we use (
1.4
), (
1.19
), (
2.3
) and (
3.54
) to get

\displaystyle\varphi_{k,\epsilon}+\gamma_{k}\sqrt{\epsilon}\partial_{\nu}\varphi_{k,\epsilon}=0\quad\text{on}~\partial\Omega_{k}.

(3.56)

Following
Proposition

2.7
, we prove the uniform boundedness of
\varphi_{k,\epsilon}
:

Proposition 3.9
.

Suppose that (
3.41
) holds true.
Then there exists a constant
M>0
independent of
\epsilon
such that
\displaystyle\max_{\overline{\Omega}_{k,\epsilon,\beta}}|\varphi_{k,\epsilon}|\leq M
for
k=0,1,\dots,K
and
0<\epsilon<\epsilon^{*}
.

Proof.

Fix
k\in\{0,1,\dots,K\}
.
It is equivalent to showing that
\displaystyle\max_{\overline{\Omega}_{k,\epsilon,\beta}}\varphi_{k,\epsilon}\leq M
and
\displaystyle\min_{\overline{\Omega}_{k,\epsilon,\beta}}\varphi_{k,\epsilon}\geq-M
for some constant
M>0
independent of
\epsilon
.
We first prove that
\displaystyle\max_{\overline{\Omega}_{k,\epsilon,\beta}}\varphi_{k,\epsilon}\leq M
for
0<\epsilon<\epsilon^{*}
.
Suppose by contradiction that there exists a sequence
\{\epsilon_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
of positive numbers with
\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\epsilon_{n}=0
and
\{x_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}\subset\overline{\Omega}_{k,\epsilon_{n},\beta}
such that
\displaystyle\varphi_{k,\epsilon_{n}}(x_{n})=\max_{\overline{\Omega}_{k,\epsilon_{n},\beta}}\varphi_{k,\epsilon_{n}}\geq n
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Since
0<\beta<1/2
, we may, without loss of generality, assume that
8(\overline{\phi_{bd}}-\underline{\phi_{bd}})\exp(-C_{3}\epsilon_{n}^{(2\beta-1)/2}/8)\leq|\phi_{\epsilon_{n}}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*}|
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, , respectively.
Note that the maximum point
x_{n}
cannot lie on the boundary
\partial\Omega_{k}
because from (
3.56
),
\partial_{\nu}\varphi_{k,\epsilon_{n}}(x_{n})=-\varphi_{k,\epsilon_{n}}(x_{n})/(\gamma_{k}\sqrt{\epsilon_{n}})\leq-n/(\gamma_{k}\sqrt{\epsilon_{n}})<0
if the maximum point
x_{n}\in\partial\Omega_{k}
.
On the other hand, by (
3.23
) (with
Proposition

3.5
) and (
3.36
), we have

\displaystyle|\varphi_{k,\epsilon_{n}}(x)|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{|\phi_{\epsilon_{n}}(x)-\phi_{\epsilon_{n}}^{*}|+|u_{k}(\delta_{k}(x)/\sqrt{\epsilon_{n}})-\phi_{0}^{*}|+|\phi_{\epsilon_{n}}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*}|}{|\phi_{\epsilon_{n}}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*}|}

\displaystyle\leq 1+\frac{4(\overline{\phi_{bd}}-\underline{\phi_{bd}})}{|\phi_{\epsilon_{n}}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*}|}\exp\left(-\frac{C_{3}}{8}\epsilon_{n}^{(2\beta-1)/2}\right)\leq\frac{3}{2}\quad\text{for}~\delta_{k}(x)=\epsilon_{n}^{\beta}~\text{and}~n\in\mathbb{N}.

This shows that
x_{n}
cannot lie on the boundary
\partial\Omega_{k,\epsilon_{n},\beta}
.
Hence
x_{n}\in\Omega_{k,\epsilon_{n},\beta}
for all
n\in\mathbb{N}
, which implies
\nabla\varphi_{k,\epsilon_{n}}(x_{n})=0
and
\Delta\varphi_{k,\epsilon_{n}}(x_{n})\leq 0
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
.
Thus by (
3.55
),

\displaystyle 0\leq-\epsilon_{n}\Delta\varphi_{k,\epsilon_{n}}(x_{n})=c_{\epsilon_{n}}(x_{n})\varphi_{k,\epsilon_{n}}(x_{n})-f_{0}^{\prime}(u_{k}(\delta_{k}(x)/\sqrt{\epsilon_{n}}))+o_{\epsilon_{n}}(1).

(3.57)

Recall that
f_{0}^{\prime}(u_{k}(t))
is uniformly bounded for
t\in[0,\infty)
,
\varphi_{k,\epsilon_{n}}(x_{n})\geq n
for
n\in\mathbb{N}
, and
c_{\epsilon}\leq-C_{3}^{2}<0
in
\overline{\Omega}_{k,\epsilon}
for
\epsilon>0
.
Letting
n\to\infty
, we obtain
\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}c_{\epsilon_{n}}(x_{n})\varphi_{k,\epsilon_{n}}(x_{n})=-\infty
, which contradicts (
3.57
).
Thus,
\displaystyle\max_{\overline{\Omega}_{k,\epsilon,\beta}}\varphi_{k,\epsilon}\leq M
for some constant
M>0
independent of
\epsilon
.
Similarly, we may use (
3.23
), (
3.36
), (
3.55
), and (
3.56
) to prove
\displaystyle\min_{\overline{\Omega}_{k,\epsilon,\beta}}\varphi_{k,\epsilon}\geq-M
for
0<\epsilon<\epsilon^{*}
, where
M>0
is independent of
\epsilon>0
.
Therefore, we complete the proof of
Proposition

3.9
.
∎

To derive a contradiction from (
3.41
), we study the asymptotic expansion of
\varphi_{k,\epsilon}
in
\Omega_{k,\epsilon,\beta}
and define

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{k,p,\epsilon}(z)=\varphi_{k,\epsilon}(\Psi_{p}(\sqrt{\epsilon}z))\quad\text{for}~z\in\overline{B}_{\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2}}^{+}~\text{and}~0<\epsilon<\epsilon^{*}

(3.58)

(cf. (
2.62
)), where
\Psi_{p}
is given in (
2.1
).
Similar to (
2.63
)–(
2.66
),
\mathcal{W}_{k,p,\epsilon}
satisfies

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}a_{ij}(z)\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{W}_{k,p,\epsilon}}{\partial z^{i}\partial z^{j}}+\sum_{j=1}^{d}b_{j}(z)\frac{\partial\mathcal{W}_{k,p,\epsilon}}{\partial z^{j}}+c_{\epsilon}(z)\mathcal{W}_{k,p,\epsilon}

\displaystyle=f_{0}(u_{k}(z^{d}))+o_{\epsilon}(1)\quad

\displaystyle\text{in}~B_{\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2}}^{+},

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{k,p,\epsilon}-\gamma_{k}\partial_{z^{d}}\mathcal{W}_{k,p,\epsilon}

\displaystyle=0\quad

\displaystyle\text{on}~\overline{B}_{\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2}}^{+}\cap\partial\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}

for
0<\epsilon<\epsilon^{*}
, where
a_{ij}
and
b_{j}
are given in (
2.5
)–(
2.6
), and

\displaystyle c_{\epsilon}(z)=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{f_{\epsilon}(u_{k}(z^{d})+(\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*})\mathcal{W}_{k,p,\epsilon}(z))-f_{\epsilon}(u_{k}(z^{d})}{(\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*})\mathcal{W}_{k,p,\epsilon}(z)}&\text{if}~\mathcal{W}_{k,p,\epsilon}(z)\neq 0;\\
f_{\epsilon}^{\prime}(u_{k}(z^{d}))&\text{if}~\mathcal{W}_{k,p,\epsilon}(z)=0.\end{cases}

As in
Lemma

2.8
of
Section

2.2
, we use the uniform boundedness of
\varphi_{k,\epsilon}
(cf.
Proposition

3.9
) to prove

Lemma 3.10
.

Suppose that (
3.41
) holds true.
For any sequence
\{\epsilon_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
of positive numbers with
\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\epsilon_{n}=0
,
\alpha\in(0,1)
, and
p\in\partial\Omega_{k}
(
k\in\{0,1,\dots,K\}
), there exists a subsequence
\{\epsilon_{nn}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
such that
\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\|\mathcal{W}_{k,p,\epsilon_{nn}}-\mathcal{W}_{k,p}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{2,\alpha}(\overline{B}_{m}^{+})}=0
for
m\in\mathbb{N}
, where
\mathcal{W}_{k,p}\in\mathcal{C}_{\text{loc}}^{2,\alpha}(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{d})
satisfies

\displaystyle\Delta\mathcal{W}_{k,p}+f_{0}^{\prime}(u_{k})\mathcal{W}_{k,p}

\displaystyle=f_{0}^{\prime}(u_{k})

\displaystyle\quad\text{in}~\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d},

(3.59)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{k,p}-\gamma_{k}\partial_{z^{d}}\mathcal{W}_{k,p}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\quad\text{on}~\partial\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}.

(3.60)

Moreover, there exists
M>0
such that
\mathcal{W}_{k,p}
satisfies the following estimate

\displaystyle|\mathcal{W}_{k,p}(z)-\theta_{k}(z^{d})|\leq M\exp(-C_{3}z^{d}/8)\quad\text{for}~z=(z^{\prime},z^{d})\in\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{d}~\text{and}~z^{d}\geq 2(d-1)/C_{3},

(3.61)

where
C_{3}
is given in (
3.7
) and
\theta_{k}=\theta_{k}(t)
is the solution to ordinary differential equation

\displaystyle\theta_{k}^{\prime\prime}+f_{0}^{\prime}(u_{k})\theta_{k}=f_{0}^{\prime}(u_{k})\quad\text{in}~(0,\infty),

(3.62)

\displaystyle\theta_{k}(0)-\gamma_{k}\theta_{k}^{\prime}(0)=0,

(3.63)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\theta_{k}(t)=1.

(3.64)

Remark 5
.

By the standard linear ODE theory, the unique solution
\theta_{k}
to (
3.62
)–(
3.64
) can be expressed explicitly as

\displaystyle\theta_{k}(t)=1-\frac{u_{k}^{\prime}(t)}{u_{k}^{\prime}(0)+\gamma_{k}f_{0}(u_{k}(0))}\quad\text{for}~t\geq 0.

(3.65)

By (A1)–(A2),
Proposition

B.1
, together with (
3.65
), it follows that

\displaystyle\theta_{k}^{\prime}(0)=\frac{f_{0}(u_{k}(0))}{u_{k}^{\prime}(0)+\gamma_{k}f_{0}(u_{k}(0))}>0.

(3.66)

From
Lemma

3.10
, the solution
\mathcal{W}_{k,p}
to (
3.59
)–(
3.60
) may, a priori, depend on the sequence
\{\epsilon_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
and on the point.
To establish the independence from the sequence and the point, we apply the moving plane arguments, as in
Propositions

2.5
and
2.9
, to prove that
\mathcal{W}_{k,p}=\mathcal{W}_{k,p}(z^{d})=\theta_{k}(z^{d})
for
z=(z^{\prime},z^{d})\in\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{d}
, where
\theta_{k}
is the unique solution to (
3.62
)–(
3.64
).
Here
\theta_{k}
is independent of the sequence
\{\epsilon_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
and the point
p\in\partial\Omega_{k}
.
Consequently, the results of
Lemma

3.10
can be improved as

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}\|\mathcal{W}_{k,p}-\theta_{k}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{2,\alpha}(\overline{B}_{m}^{+})}=0\quad\text{for}~m\in\mathbb{N}~\text{and}~\alpha\in(0,1),

(3.67)

and

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}\mathcal{W}_{k,p,\epsilon}(z)=\theta_{k}(z^{d})\quad\text{for}~z=(z^{\prime},z^{d})\in\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{d},

(3.68)

where
\mathcal{W}_{k,p,\epsilon}
is defined in (
3.58
).
The details are provided below.

Proposition 3.11
.

Suppose that (
3.41
) holds true.
For
p\in\partial\Omega_{k}
and
k\in\{0,1,\dots,K\}
, the solution
\mathcal{W}_{k,p}
to (
3.59
)–(
3.60
) satisfies

(a)

\mathcal{W}_{k,p}
depends only on the variable
z^{d}
, i.e.,
\mathcal{W}_{k,p}(z)=\mathcal{W}_{k,p}(z^{d})
for
z=(z^{\prime},z^{d})\in\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{d}
.

(b)

\mathcal{W}_{k,p}
is independent of
p
and depends only on
k
, i.e.,
\mathcal{W}_{k,p}(z^{d})=\theta_{k}(z^{d})
for
z^{d}\in[0,\infty)
, where
\theta_{k}
is the unique solution to (
3.62
)–(
3.64
).

Proof.

Following
Proposition

2.9
, we replace
f
with
f_{0}
and apply the moving plane arguments to obtain (a).
By (a), (
3.61
), and (
3.64
),
\mathcal{W}_{k,p}=\mathcal{W}_{k,p}(z^{d})
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{k,p}^{\prime\prime}+f_{0}^{\prime}(u_{k})\mathcal{W}_{k,p}=f_{0}^{\prime}(u_{k})\quad\text{in}~(0,\infty),

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{k,p}-\gamma_{k}\mathcal{W}_{k,p}^{\prime}(0)=0,

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\mathcal{W}_{k,p}(t)=1.

By the uniqueness of the solution to (
3.62
)–(
3.64
), it follows that
\mathcal{W}_{k,p}\equiv\theta_{k}
, which gives (b).
This completes the proof of
Proposition

3.11
.
∎

We may use
Proposition

3.11
to prove (
3.67
), (
3.68
), and
\mathcal{W}_{k,p}(z)=\theta_{k}(z^{d})
for
z=(z^{\prime},z^{d})\in\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{d}
.
Let
T>0
,
k\in\{0,1,\dots,K\}
, and
p\in\partial\Omega_{k}
.
Then, under the assumption (
3.41
), we can use (
2.1
), (
3.58
), (
3.67
)–(
3.68
), and
\nabla\delta_{k}(p-t\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p})=-\nu_{p}
to get

\varphi_{k,\epsilon}(p-t\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p})=\theta_{k}(t)+o_{\epsilon}(1),\qquad\nabla\varphi_{k,\epsilon}(p-t\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p})=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}[\theta_{k}^{\prime}(t)\nu_{p}+o_{\epsilon}(1)]

for
0\leq t\leq T
as
\epsilon\to 0^{+}
.
Together with (
3.54
) and (
3.58
), we arrive at

\displaystyle\phi_{\epsilon}(p-t\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p})=u_{k}(t)+(\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*})\theta_{k}(t)+(\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*})o_{\epsilon}(1),

(3.69)

\displaystyle\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}(p-t\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p})=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\{[u_{k}^{\prime}(t)+(\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*})\theta_{k}^{\prime}(t)]\nu_{p}+(\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*})o_{\epsilon}(1)\}

(3.70)

for
0\leq t\leq T
as
\epsilon\to 0^{+}
.

By (
3.53
) and (
3.69
), we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} f_{\epsilon}(\phi_{\epsilon}(p-t\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p}))&=f_{0}(\phi_{\epsilon}(p-t\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p}))-(\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*})f_{0}^{\prime}(\phi_{\epsilon}(p-t\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p}))+(\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*})o_{\epsilon}(1)\\
&=f_{0}(u_{k}(t))+(\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*})f_{0}^{\prime}(u_{k}(t))[\theta_{k}(t)-1]+(\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*})o_{\epsilon}(1)\end{aligned}

(3.71)

for
0\leq t\leq T
as
\epsilon\to 0^{+}
.
Combining (
3.70
)–(
3.71
), we then follow an argument similar to that in
Section

2.3
to obtain

Proposition 3.12
.

Suppose that (
3.41
) holds true.
Then we have

(a)

\int_{\overline{\Omega}_{k,T,\epsilon}}\!f_{\epsilon}(\phi_{\epsilon}(x))\,\mathrm{d}x=\sqrt{\epsilon}|\partial\Omega_{k}|(u_{k}^{\prime}(0)-u_{k}^{\prime}(T))+\sqrt{\epsilon}(\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*})|\partial\Omega_{k}|(\theta_{k}^{\prime}(0)-\theta_{k}^{\prime}(T))+\sqrt{\epsilon}(\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*})o_{\epsilon}(1)
,

(b)

\int_{\overline{\Omega}_{k,T,\epsilon,\beta}}\!f_{\epsilon}(\phi_{\epsilon}(x))\,\mathrm{d}x=\sqrt{\epsilon}|\partial\Omega_{k}|u_{k}^{\prime}(T)+\sqrt{\epsilon}(\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*})|\partial\Omega_{k}|\theta_{k}^{\prime}(T)+\sqrt{\epsilon}(\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*})\epsilon o_{\epsilon}(1)
,

(c)

\left|\int_{\overline{\Omega}_{\epsilon,\beta}}\!f_{\epsilon}(\phi_{\epsilon}(x))\,\mathrm{d}x\right|\leq\sqrt{\epsilon}M^{\prime}\exp\left(-M\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2}\right)

for
0<\epsilon<\epsilon^{*}
and
0<\beta<1/2
, where
|\partial\Omega_{k}|
is the surface area of
\partial\Omega_{k}
.

Proof.

Following
Corollary

1
, we begin with (a).
Integrating (
3.71
) over
\overline{\Omega}_{k,T,\epsilon}
and applying the coarea formula (cf.
[
18
,
37
]
) with (
2.75
), we get

\displaystyle\int_{\overline{\Omega}_{k,T,\epsilon}}\!f_{\epsilon}(\phi_{\epsilon}(x))\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\epsilon}\int_{0}^{T}\!\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\!f_{0}(u_{k}(t))\,\mathrm{d}S_{p}\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\quad+\sqrt{\epsilon}(\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*})\int_{0}^{T}\!\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\!f_{0}^{\prime}(u_{k}(t))[\theta_{k}(t)-1]\,\mathrm{d}S_{p}\,\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\epsilon}(\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*})o_{\epsilon}(1).

Together with (
1.19
) and (
3.62
), we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\overline{\Omega}_{k,T,\epsilon}}\!f_{\epsilon}(\phi_{\epsilon}(x))\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\epsilon}|\partial\Omega_{k}|\int_{0}^{T}\!(-u_{k}^{\prime\prime}(t))\,\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\epsilon}(\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*})|\partial\Omega_{k}|\int_{0}^{T}\!(-\theta_{k}^{\prime\prime}(t))\,\mathrm{d}t+\sqrt{\epsilon}(\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*})o_{\epsilon}(1)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\epsilon}|\partial\Omega_{k}|(u_{k}^{\prime}(0)-u_{k}^{\prime}(T))+\sqrt{\epsilon}(\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*})|\partial\Omega_{k}|(\theta_{k}^{\prime}(0)-\theta_{k}^{\prime}(T))+\sqrt{\epsilon}(\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*})o_{\epsilon}(1),

which gives (a).

We now prove (b).
As in (
2.76
), we integrate (
1.2
) over
\overline{\Omega}_{k,T,\epsilon,\beta}
and then apply the divergence theorem to obtain

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \int_{\overline{\Omega}_{k,T,\epsilon,\beta}}\!f_{\epsilon}(\phi_{\epsilon}(x))\,\mathrm{d}x&=-\epsilon\int_{\overline{\Omega}_{k,T,\epsilon,\beta}}\!\Delta\phi_{\epsilon}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\\
&=-\epsilon\int_{\partial\Omega_{k,T,\epsilon,\beta}}\partial_{\nu_{x}}\phi_{\epsilon}(x)\,\mathrm{d}S_{x}\\
&=-\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\!(\epsilon\partial_{\nu_{p}}\phi_{\epsilon}(p-T\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p}))\mathcal{J}(T,p)\,\mathrm{d}S_{p}\\
&\quad+\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\!(\epsilon\partial_{\nu_{p}}\phi_{\epsilon}(p-\epsilon^{\beta}\nu_{p}))\mathcal{J}(\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2},p)\,\mathrm{d}S_{p},\end{aligned}

(3.72)

where
\nu_{x}
is the unit outer normal at
x\in\partial\Omega_{k,T,\epsilon,\beta}
with respect to
\Omega_{k,T,\epsilon,\beta}
, and
\mathcal{J}(T,p)
and
\mathcal{J}(\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2},p)
are given in (
2.77
).
Then by (
3.41
) and (
3.70
), we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\quad-\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\!(\epsilon\partial_{\nu_{p}}\phi_{\epsilon}(p-T\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p}))\mathcal{J}(T,p)\,\mathrm{d}S_{p}\\
&=-\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\!(\epsilon\partial_{\nu_{p}}\phi_{\epsilon}(p-T\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p}))[1-T\sqrt{\epsilon}(d-1)H(p)+\sqrt{\epsilon}o_{\epsilon}(1)]\,\mathrm{d}S_{p}\\
&=\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\![\sqrt{\epsilon}u_{k}^{\prime}(T)+\sqrt{\epsilon}(\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*})\theta_{k}^{\prime}(T)][1-T\sqrt{\epsilon}(d-1)H(p)+\sqrt{\epsilon}o_{\epsilon}(1)]\,\mathrm{d}S_{p}\\
&=\sqrt{\epsilon}|\partial\Omega_{k}|u_{k}^{\prime}(T)+\sqrt{\epsilon}(\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*})|\partial\Omega_{k}|\theta_{k}^{\prime}(T)+\sqrt{\epsilon}(\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*})o_{\epsilon}(1).\end{aligned}

(3.73)

On the other hand, by (
3.37
) in
Proposition

3.7
, we have

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\!(\epsilon\partial_{\nu_{p}}\phi_{\epsilon}(p-\epsilon^{\beta}\nu_{p}))\mathcal{J}(\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2},p)\,\mathrm{d}S_{p}\right|\leq\sqrt{\epsilon}|\partial\Omega_{k}|M^{\prime}\exp\left(-M\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2}\right).

(3.74)

Combining (
3.72
)–(
3.74
) with
0<\beta<1/2
, we get (b).
Following the argument for (b), one may use (
3.74
) to prove (c).
Therefore, we complete the proof of
Proposition

3.12
.
∎

We are now in a position to derive a contradiction from (
3.41
).
By (
1.2
)–(
1.3
), we observe that

\int_{\Omega}\!f_{\epsilon}(\phi_{\epsilon}(x))\,\mathrm{d}x=\sum_{i=1}^{I}\frac{m_{i}z_{i}\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{\epsilon}(x))}{\int_{\Omega}\!\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{\epsilon}(y))\,\mathrm{d}y}\,\mathrm{d}x=\sum_{i=1}^{I}m_{i}z_{i}=0,

which implies

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{K}\!\int_{\overline{\Omega}_{k,T,\epsilon}\cup\overline{\Omega}_{k,T,\epsilon,\beta}}\!f_{\epsilon}(\phi_{\epsilon}(x))\,\mathrm{d}x=\int_{\overline{\Omega}_{\epsilon,\beta}}\!f_{\epsilon}(\phi_{\epsilon}(x))\,\mathrm{d}x.

(3.75)

Along with (
3.27
), (
3.41
), and
Proposition

3.12
, we have

\sqrt{\epsilon}(\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*})\sum_{k=0}^{K}|\partial\Omega_{k}|\theta_{k}^{\prime}(0)=\sqrt{\epsilon}(\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*})o_{\epsilon}(1),

which gives

\sum_{k=0}^{K}|\partial\Omega_{k}|\theta_{k}^{\prime}(0)=0.

However, since
\theta_{k}^{\prime}(0)>0
for all
k=0,1,\dots,K
(see (
3.66
) in
Remark

5
), we get a contradiction, which shows (
3.41
) cannot hold true.
Consequently, we establish the uniform boundedness of
|\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*}|/\sqrt{\epsilon}
.

Remark 6
.

Due to the uniform boundedness of
|\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*}|/\sqrt{\epsilon}
, there exists a sequence
\{\epsilon_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
with
\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\epsilon_{n}=0
and a constant
Q\in\mathbb{R}
such that
(\phi_{\epsilon_{n}}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*})/\sqrt{\epsilon_{n}}\to Q
as
n\to\infty
.
In
Section

3.5
, we will prove that
Q
is uniquely determined by (
3.114
), which implies

\displaystyle\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}=\phi_{0}^{*}+\sqrt{\epsilon}(Q+o_{\epsilon}(1))\quad\text{as}~\epsilon\to 0^{+}.

(3.76)

3.4
Second-order asymptotic expansion of
\phi_{\epsilon}
in
\Omega_{k,\epsilon}

In this section, we will use uniform boundedness of
|\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*}|/\sqrt{\epsilon}
(cf. (
3.76
)) to derive the second-order asymptotic expansions of
\phi_{\epsilon}
and
\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}
.
As in
Section

3.3
, we first study the second-order asymptotic expansions of
f_{\epsilon}
and
A_{i,\epsilon}
.
A direct computation of
f_{\epsilon}
shows

\displaystyle f_{1,\epsilon}(\phi):=\frac{f_{\epsilon}(\phi)-f_{0}(\phi)}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}=-\sum_{i=1}^{I}\frac{m_{i}z_{i}B_{i,\epsilon}}{|\Omega|[|\Omega|\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{0}^{*})+\sqrt{\epsilon}B_{i,\epsilon}]}\exp(-z_{i}(\phi-\phi_{0}^{*}))\quad\text{for}~\phi\in\mathbb{R},

(3.77)

where

\displaystyle B_{i,\epsilon}=\frac{A_{i,\epsilon}-|\Omega|\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{0}^{*})}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}=\int_{\Omega}\!\frac{\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{\epsilon}(y))-\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{0}^{*})}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\,\mathrm{d}y

(3.78)

for
i=1,\dots,I
and
\epsilon>0
.
Note that
B_{i,\epsilon}
can be expressed by

\displaystyle B_{i,\epsilon}=J_{i,1}+\sum_{k=0}^{K}J_{i,k,2}\quad\text{for}~i=1,\dots,I,

(3.79)

where

\displaystyle J_{i,1}=\int_{\overline{\Omega}_{\epsilon,\beta}}\!\frac{\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{\epsilon}(y))-\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{0}^{*})}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\,\mathrm{d}y,

(3.80)

\displaystyle J_{i,k,2}=\int_{\Omega_{k,\epsilon,\beta}}\!\frac{\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{\epsilon}(y))-\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{0}^{*})}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\,\mathrm{d}y\quad\text{for}~k=0,1,\dots,K.

(3.81)

Here
\overline{\Omega}_{\epsilon,\beta}=\{x\in\Omega\,:\,\delta(x)\geq\epsilon^{\beta}\}
and
\Omega_{k,\epsilon,\beta}=\{x\in\Omega\,:\delta_{k}(x)<\epsilon^{\beta}\}
for
0<\beta<1/2
and
k=0,1,\dots,K
. Clearly,
