\Omega_{k,\epsilon,\beta}
are disjoint for sufficiently small
\epsilon>0
.
Using (
3.36
), (
3.78
), and (
3.76
), we obtain the following result, similar to Lemma 3.2 in
Section

3.3
:

Lemma 3.13
.

B_{i,\epsilon}=\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{0}^{*})[-z_{i}Q|\Omega|-(\hat{m}_{i}/m_{i})|\Omega|]+o_{\epsilon}(1)
for
i=1,\dots,I
, where
u_{k}
is the unique solution to (
1.19
)–(
1.21
), and
\hat{m}_{i}=m_{i}|\Omega|^{-1}\sum_{k=0}^{K}|\partial\Omega_{k}|\int_{0}^{\infty}\![\exp(-z_{i}(u_{k}(s)-\phi_{0}^{*}))-1]\,\mathrm{d}s
for
i=1,\dots,I
.

Remark 7
.

By (
3.78
) and
Lemma

3.13
, we obtain

A_{i,\epsilon}=|\Omega|\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{0}^{*})+\sqrt{\epsilon}\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{0}^{*})\left(-z_{i}Q|\Omega|-\frac{\hat{m}_{i}}{m_{i}}|\Omega|+o_{\epsilon}(1)\right)\quad\text{for}~i=1,\dots,I.

This allows us to compute the expansion of
c_{i,\epsilon}^{\mathrm{b}}=m_{i}/A_{i,\epsilon}
:

\displaystyle c_{i,\epsilon}^{\mathrm{b}}

\displaystyle=\frac{m_{i}}{A_{i,\epsilon}}=\frac{m_{i}}{|\Omega|\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{0}^{*})}\left(1+\sqrt{\epsilon}\left[-z_{i}Q-\frac{\hat{m}_{i}}{m_{i}}+o_{\epsilon}(1)\right]\right)^{-1}

\displaystyle=\frac{m_{i}}{|\Omega|\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{0}^{*})}\left(1+\sqrt{\epsilon}\left[z_{i}Q+\frac{\hat{m}_{i}}{m_{i}}+o_{\epsilon}(1)\right]\right)

\displaystyle=c_{i}^{\mathrm{b}}+\sqrt{\epsilon}\left(\frac{m_{i}z_{i}Q\exp(z_{i}\phi_{0}^{*})+\hat{m}_{i}\exp(z_{i}\phi_{0}^{*})}{|\Omega|}+o_{\epsilon}(1)\right)\quad\text{for}~i=1,\dots,I,

where
c_{i}^{\mathrm{b}}=m_{i}|\Omega|^{-1}\exp(z_{i}\phi_{0}^{*})
for
i=1,\dots,I
.

Proof of
Lemma

3.13
.

To prove
Lemma

3.13
, we analyze
J_{i,1}
and
J_{i,k,2}
as in
Section

3.3
.
First, we compute the limit of
J_{i,1}
using (
3.36
) and (
3.76
).

Claim 3
.

J_{i,1}=-z_{i}Q\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{0}^{*})|\Omega|+o_{\epsilon}(1)
for
i=1,\dots,I
.

Henceforth
\epsilon^{*}>0
is a sufficiently small constant, and
M,M^{\prime}>0
are generic constants independent of
\epsilon>0
.

Proof of
3
.

Fix
i\in\{1,\dots,I\}
.
We first consider the case of
z_{i}>0
.
Then by (
3.36
), we may follow the approach of (
3.47
)–(
3.48
) in
Lemma

3.8
(with
\sqrt{\epsilon}
scaling instead of
\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*}
) and apply (
3.76
) and (
3.80
) to obtain

\displaystyle\quad\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{0}^{*})\left|\Omega-\bigcup_{k=0}^{K}\Omega_{k,\epsilon,\beta}\right|\frac{\exp(-z_{i}Q\sqrt{\epsilon})\exp[-z_{i}M^{\prime}\exp(-M\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2})+\sqrt{\epsilon}o_{\epsilon}(1)]-1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}

\displaystyle\leq J_{i,1}\leq\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{0}^{*})\left|\Omega-\bigcup_{k=0}^{K}\Omega_{k,\epsilon,\beta}\right|\frac{\exp(-z_{i}Q\sqrt{\epsilon})\exp[z_{i}M^{\prime}\exp(-M\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2})+\sqrt{\epsilon}o_{\epsilon}(1)]-1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}.

Along with the fact that

\displaystyle\quad\lim_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}\frac{\exp(-z_{i}Q\sqrt{\epsilon})\exp[\pm z_{i}M^{\prime}\exp(-M\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2})+\sqrt{\epsilon}o_{\epsilon}(1)]-1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}

\displaystyle=\lim_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}\frac{\exp[-z_{i}Q\sqrt{\epsilon}\pm z_{i}M^{\prime}\exp(-M\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2})+\sqrt{\epsilon}o_{\epsilon}(1)]-1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}=-z_{i}Q,

we obtain

\displaystyle J_{i,1}=-z_{i}Q\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{0}^{*})|\Omega|+o_{\epsilon}(1)\quad\text{for}~i=1,\dots,I.

For the case of
z_{i}<0
, we can follow a similar argument and complete the proof of
3
.
∎

To obtain the boundedness of
J_{i,k,2}
(defined in (
3.81
)), we employ (
3.36
), (
3.76
), the principal coordinate system (cf.
[
24
]
), and the coarea formula (cf.
[
18
,
37
]
), adapting techniques analogous to those in
Section

3.3
.
Below are the details.

Claim 4
.

There exists
M>0
independent of
\epsilon
such that
|J_{i,k,2}|\leq M
for
i=1,\dots,I
and
k=0,1,\dots,K
and
0<\epsilon<\epsilon^{*}
, where
\epsilon^{*}>0
is sufficiently small.

Proof of
4
.

Similar to the analysis in
Section

3.3
, we consider
y\in\overline{\Omega}_{k,\epsilon,\beta}=\{x\in\overline{\Omega}:\delta_{k}(x)\leq\epsilon^{\beta}\}
. For sufficiently small
\epsilon>0
, the point
y
can be expressed as
y=p-s\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p}
, where
p\in\partial\Omega_{k}
,
\nu_{p}
is the unit outer normal, and
0\leq s\leq\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2}
.
Then we apply the corea formula and (
2.75
) to get

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} J_{i,k,2}&=\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\!\int_{0}^{\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2}}\![\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{\epsilon}(p-s\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p}))-\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{0}^{*})]\{1-s\sqrt{\epsilon}[(d-1)H(p)+o_{\epsilon}(1)]\}\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}S_{p}\\
&:=J_{i,k,3}-J_{i,k,4}\quad\text{for}~i=1,\dots,I~\text{and}~k=0,1,\dots,K,\end{aligned}

(3.82)

where

\displaystyle J_{i,k,3}=\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\!\int_{0}^{\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2}}\!(\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{\epsilon}(p-s\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p}))-\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{0}^{*}))\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}S_{p},

(3.83)

\displaystyle J_{i,k,4}=\sqrt{\epsilon}\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\!\int_{0}^{\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2}}\!s[\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{\epsilon}(p-s\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p}))-\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{0}^{*})][(d-1)H(p)+o_{\epsilon}(1)]\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}S_{p}.

(3.84)

By
Propositions

3.1
and
3.3
,
|\phi_{\epsilon}(x)-\phi_{0}^{*}|
is uniformly bounded for
x\in\overline{\Omega}
and
\epsilon>0
, which implies that there exists
M>1
such that
|\exp(-z_{i}(\phi_{\epsilon}(x)-\phi_{0}^{*}))-1|\leq M|\phi_{\epsilon}(x)-\phi_{0}^{*}|
for
x\in\overline{\Omega}
and
0<\epsilon<\epsilon^{*}
.
Then by (
3.36
), we have

\displaystyle\left|\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{\epsilon}(x))-\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{0}^{*})\right|\leq M^{\prime}\exp\left(-M\delta(x)/\sqrt{\epsilon}\right)+M^{\prime}|\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*}|

(3.85)

for
x\in\overline{\Omega}
and
0<\epsilon<\epsilon^{*}
.
Using (
3.76
) and (
3.85
) with
x=p-s\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p}
,
J_{i,k,3}
can be estimated by

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} |J_{i,k,3}|&=\left|\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\!\int_{0}^{\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2}}\!\left[\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{\epsilon}(p-s\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p}))-\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{0}^{*})\right]\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}S_{p}\right|\\
&\leq M^{\prime}\int_{0}^{\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2}}\![\exp(-Ms)+|\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*}|]\,\mathrm{d}s\\
&=M^{\prime}(1-\exp(-M\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2}))+M^{\prime}\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2}|\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*}|\leq M\quad\text{for}~k=0,1,\dots,K.\end{aligned}

(3.86)

On the other hand, due to the smoothness of
\partial\Omega_{k}
, there exists a positive constant
M
independent of
\epsilon
such that
|(d-1)H(p)+o_{\epsilon}(1)|\leq M
for
0<\epsilon<\epsilon^{*}
.
Using (
3.76
) and (
3.85
) with
x=p-s\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p}
,
J_{i,k,4}
can be estimated by

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} |J_{i,k,4}|&=\sqrt{\epsilon}\left|\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\!\int_{0}^{\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2}}\!s[\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{\epsilon}(p-s\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p}))-\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{0}^{*})][(d-1)H(p)+o_{\epsilon}(1)]\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}S_{p}\right|\\
&\leq M^{\prime}\sqrt{\epsilon}\int_{0}^{\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2}}\!s\left[\exp(-Ms)+|\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*}|\right]\,\mathrm{d}s=\sqrt{\epsilon}\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}(1)\quad\text{for}~k=0,1,\dots,K.\end{aligned}

(3.87)

Combining (
3.82
)–(
3.87
), we arrive at
|J_{i,k,2}|\leq|J_{i,k,3}|+|J_{i,k,4}|\leq M
for
0<\epsilon<\epsilon^{*}
, which completes the proof of
4
.
∎

To complete the proof of
Lemma

3.13
, we define

\displaystyle\varphi_{k,\epsilon}(x)=\frac{\phi_{\epsilon}(x)-u_{k}(\delta_{k}(x)/\sqrt{\epsilon})}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\quad\text{for}~x\in\overline{\Omega}_{k,\epsilon,\beta}~\text{and}~k=0,1,\dots,K,

(3.88)

where
u_{k}
is the unique solution to (
1.19
)–(
1.21
),
\delta_{k}(x)=\operatorname{dist}(x,\partial\Omega_{k})
and
\overline{\Omega}_{k,\epsilon,\beta}=\{x\in\overline{\Omega}:\delta_{k}(x)\leq\epsilon^{\beta}\}
for
k=0,1,\dots,K
.
Note that (
3.88
) has the same form as (
2.54
).
Following the approach of
Proposition

2.7
in
Section

3.3
, we claim

Claim 5
.

There exists a constant
M>0
independent of
\epsilon
such that
\displaystyle\max_{\overline{\Omega}_{k,\epsilon,\beta}}|\varphi_{k,\epsilon}|\leq M
for
k=0,1,\dots,K
and
0<\epsilon<\epsilon^{*}
.

Proof of
5
.

Following the method of
Section

2.2
, we derive the partial differential equation for
\varphi_{k,\epsilon}
in
\Omega_{k,\epsilon,\beta}
.
As in (
2.55
), we use (
1.2
), (
1.19
), (
2.58
) and (
3.88
) to get

\displaystyle\epsilon\Delta\varphi_{k,\epsilon}(x)

\displaystyle=-\frac{f_{\epsilon}(\phi_{\epsilon}(x))-f_{0}(u_{k}(\delta_{k}(x)/\sqrt{\epsilon}))}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}+[(d-1)H(p_{x})+\epsilon^{\beta}\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}(1)]u_{k}^{\prime}(\delta_{k}(x)/\sqrt{\epsilon})

\displaystyle=-\frac{f_{\epsilon}(\phi_{\epsilon}(x))-f_{\epsilon}(u_{k}(\delta_{k}(x)/\sqrt{\epsilon}))}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}-\frac{f_{\epsilon}(u_{k}(\delta_{k}(x)/\sqrt{\epsilon}))-f_{0}(u_{k}(\delta_{k}(x)/\sqrt{\epsilon}))}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}

\displaystyle\quad+[(d-1)H(p_{x})+\epsilon^{\beta}\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}(1)]u_{k}^{\prime}(\delta_{k}(x)/\sqrt{\epsilon})

\displaystyle=-c_{\epsilon}(x)\varphi_{k,\epsilon}+g_{\epsilon}(x)\quad\text{for}~x\in\Omega_{k,\epsilon,\beta},

where

\displaystyle c_{\epsilon}(x)=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{f_{\epsilon}(\phi_{\epsilon}(x))-f_{\epsilon}(u_{k}(\delta_{k}(x)/\sqrt{\epsilon}))}{\phi_{\epsilon}(x)-u_{k}(\delta_{k}(x)/\sqrt{\epsilon})}&\text{if}~\phi_{\epsilon}(x)\neq u_{k}(\delta_{k}(x)/\sqrt{\epsilon});\\
f_{\epsilon}^{\prime}(\phi_{\epsilon}(x))&\text{if}~\phi_{\epsilon}(x)=u_{k}(\delta_{k}(x)/\sqrt{\epsilon}),\end{cases}

(3.89)

\displaystyle g_{\epsilon}(x)=(d-1)H(p_{x})u_{k}^{\prime}(\delta_{k}(x)/\sqrt{\epsilon})-f_{1,\epsilon}(u_{k}(\delta_{k}(x)/\sqrt{\epsilon}))+\epsilon^{\beta}\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}(1).

(3.90)

Here we have used the definition of
f_{1,\epsilon}
(cf. (
3.77
)) and the fact that
u_{k}^{\prime}
is bounded on
[0,\infty)
(cf.
Proposition

B.1
).
Note that functions
c_{\epsilon}
and
g_{\epsilon}
are different from (
2.56
)–(
2.57
) because
f_{1,\epsilon}
is a nonzero function.
Along with (
1.4
), (
1.20
), (
2.3
) and (
3.20
), we obtain

\displaystyle\epsilon\Delta\varphi_{k,\epsilon}+c_{\epsilon}(x)\varphi_{k,\epsilon}

\displaystyle=g_{\epsilon}(x)\quad

\displaystyle\text{for}~x\in\Omega_{k,\epsilon},

(3.91)

\displaystyle\varphi_{k,\epsilon}+\gamma_{k}\sqrt{\epsilon}\partial_{\nu}\varphi_{k,\epsilon}

\displaystyle=0\quad

\displaystyle\text{on}~\partial\Omega_{k}\quad\text{for}~k=0,1,\dots,K.

(3.92)

Note that
c_{\epsilon}(x)<0
for
x\in\Omega_{k,\epsilon}
because
f_{\epsilon}^{\prime}(\phi)\leq-C_{3}^{2}
for
\phi\in\mathbb{R}
and
\epsilon>0
(cf (
3.7
)).
By (
3.77
), (
3.79
),
3
and
4
,
f_{1,\epsilon}
is uniformly bounded on the interval
[\underline{\phi_{bd}},\overline{\phi_{bd}}]
with respect to
\epsilon
and then
g_{\epsilon}
is uniformly bounded with respect to
\epsilon
.
By (
3.36
), (
3.76
) and (
3.23
) with
Proposition

3.5
, we find the following inequality:

\displaystyle|\varphi_{k,\epsilon}(x)|\leq\frac{|\phi_{\epsilon}(x)-\phi_{0}^{*}|+|u_{k}(\delta_{k}(x)/\sqrt{\epsilon})-\phi_{0}^{*}|}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\leq\frac{M^{\prime}}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\exp\left(-M\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2}\right)+\frac{|\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}-\phi_{0}^{*}|}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\leq 2|Q|+\frac{1}{2}

for
\delta_{k}(x)=\epsilon^{1/8}
as
\epsilon
sufficiently small, which has the same form as (
2.60
) in
Proposition

2.7
.
Hence as in
Proposition

2.7
, we conclude that
\varphi_{k,\epsilon}
is uniformly bounded in
\overline{\Omega}_{k,\epsilon}
, i.e., there exists a positive constant
M
independent of
\epsilon
such that
\displaystyle\max_{\overline{\Omega}_{k,\epsilon}}|\varphi_{k,\epsilon}|\leq M
for
k=0,1,\dots,K
and sufficiently small
\epsilon>0
.
Therefore, this ends the proof of
5
.
∎

By (
3.88
) and
5
, we have

\displaystyle\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{\epsilon}(p-s\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p}))=\exp(-z_{i}u_{k}(s))+\sqrt{\epsilon}\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}(1)\quad\text{for}~0\leq s\leq\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2}.

(3.93)

Thus, by (
3.83
) and (
3.93
), we get

\displaystyle J_{i,k,3}

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\!\int_{0}^{\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2}}\![\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{\epsilon}(p-s\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p}))-\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{0}^{*})]\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}S_{p}

\displaystyle=\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\!\int_{0}^{\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2}}\![\exp(-z_{i}u_{k}(s))-\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{0}^{*})+\sqrt{\epsilon}\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}(1)]\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}S_{p}

\displaystyle=\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{0}^{*})\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\!\int_{0}^{\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2}}\![\exp(-z_{i}(u_{k}(s)-\phi_{0}^{*}))-1]\,\mathrm{d}s\,\mathrm{d}S_{p}+\epsilon^{\beta}\mathcal{O}_{\epsilon}(1)

\displaystyle=\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{0}^{*})|\partial\Omega_{k}|\int_{0}^{\infty}\![\exp(-z_{i}(u_{k}(s)-\phi_{0}^{*}))-1]\,\mathrm{d}s+o_{\epsilon}(1)\quad\text{for}~i=1,\dots,I,

which implies

\displaystyle J_{i,k,3}=\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{0}^{*})|\partial\Omega_{k}|\int_{0}^{\infty}\![\exp(-z_{i}(u_{k}(s)-\phi_{0}^{*}))-1]\,\mathrm{d}s+o_{\epsilon}(1)\quad\text{for}~i=1,\dots,I.

(3.94)

Here we have used the fact that
\int_{0}^{\infty}\![\exp(-z_{i}(u_{k}(s)-\phi_{0}^{*}))-1]\,\mathrm{d}s
is convergent because of (
3.23
) and
Proposition

3.5
.
Therefore, we can use (
3.79
), (
3.82
)–(
3.84
), (
3.87
), (
3.94
) and
3
to get

\displaystyle B_{i,\epsilon}

\displaystyle=J_{i,1}+\sum_{k=0}^{K}J_{i,k,2}

\displaystyle=(-z_{i}Q\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{0}^{*})+o_{\epsilon}(1))+\sum_{k=0}^{K}(J_{i,k,3}+J_{i,k,4})

\displaystyle=\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{0}^{*})\left[-z_{i}Q|\Omega|+\sum_{k=0}^{K}|\partial\Omega_{k}|\int_{0}^{\infty}\![\exp(-z_{i}(u_{k}(s)-\phi_{0}^{*}))-1]\,\mathrm{d}s\right]+o_{\epsilon}(1)\quad\text{for}~i=1,\dots,I,

and complete the proof of
Lemma

3.13
.
∎

Applying
Lemma

3.13
to (
3.77
), we obtain

\displaystyle f_{1,\epsilon}(\phi)

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i=1}^{I}\frac{m_{i}z_{i}B_{i,\epsilon}}{|\Omega|[|\Omega|\exp(-z_{i}\phi_{0}^{*})+\sqrt{\epsilon}B_{i,\epsilon}]}\exp(-z_{i}(\phi-\phi_{0}^{*}))

\displaystyle=-\sum_{i=1}^{I}\frac{m_{i}z_{i}}{|\Omega|^{2}}\left[-z_{i}Q|\Omega|+\sum_{k=0}^{K}|\partial\Omega_{k}|\int_{0}^{\infty}\![\exp(-z_{i}(u_{k}(s)-\phi_{0}^{*}))-1]\,\mathrm{d}s\right]\exp(-z_{i}(\phi-\phi_{0}^{*}))+o_{\epsilon}(1)

\displaystyle:=f_{1}(\phi)+o_{\epsilon}(1),

which implies

\displaystyle f_{1,\epsilon}(\phi)=f_{1}(\phi)+o_{\epsilon}(1)\quad\text{for}~\phi\in\mathbb{R}.

(3.95)

Note that
f_{1}
is given in (
3.4
).
Hence the nonlinear term
f_{\epsilon}
of the CCPB equation (
3.1
) can be expressed as

\displaystyle f_{\epsilon}(\phi)=f_{0}(\phi)+\sqrt{\epsilon}(f_{1}(\phi)+o_{\epsilon}(1))\quad\text{for}~\phi\in\mathbb{R},

(3.96)

where
f_{0}(\phi)=|\Omega|^{-1}\sum_{i=1}^{I}m_{i}z_{i}\exp(-z_{i}(\phi-\phi_{0}^{*}))
(defined in (
3.15
)).
Consequently, the CCPB equation (
3.1
) can be rewritten as

\displaystyle-\epsilon\Delta\phi_{\epsilon}=f_{0}(\phi_{\epsilon})+\sqrt{\epsilon}(f_{1}(\phi_{\epsilon})+o_{\epsilon}(1))\quad\text{in}~\Omega.

(3.97)

Moreover, by (
3.88
)–(
3.92
) and (
3.96
)–(
3.97
), we have

\displaystyle\epsilon\Delta\varphi_{k,\epsilon}+c_{\epsilon}(x)\varphi_{k,\epsilon}

\displaystyle=g_{\epsilon}(x)\quad

\displaystyle\text{for}~x\in\Omega_{k,\epsilon,\beta},

(3.98)

\displaystyle\varphi_{k,\epsilon}+\gamma_{k}\sqrt{\epsilon}\partial_{\nu}\varphi_{k,\epsilon}

\displaystyle=0\quad

\displaystyle\text{on}~\partial\Omega_{k}\quad\text{for}~k=0,1,\dots,K,

(3.99)

where

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} c_{\epsilon}(x)&=\frac{f_{0}(\phi_{\epsilon}(x))-f_{0}(u_{k}(\delta_{k}(x)/\sqrt{\epsilon}))}{\phi_{\epsilon}(x)-u_{k}(\delta_{k}(x)/\sqrt{\epsilon})}\\
&\quad+\sqrt{\epsilon}\frac{f_{1}(\phi_{\epsilon}(x))-f_{1}(u_{k}(\delta_{k}(x)/\sqrt{\epsilon}))+o_{\epsilon}(1)}{\phi_{\epsilon}(x)-u_{k}(\delta_{k}(x)/\sqrt{\epsilon})}\quad\text{if}~\phi_{\epsilon}(x)\neq u_{k}(\delta_{k}(x)/\sqrt{\epsilon}),\end{aligned}

(3.100)

\displaystyle c_{\epsilon}(x)=f_{0}^{\prime}(\phi_{\epsilon}(x))+\sqrt{\epsilon}(f_{1}^{\prime}(\phi_{\epsilon}(x))+o_{\epsilon}(1))\quad\text{if}~\phi_{\epsilon}(x)=u_{k}(\delta_{k}(x)/\sqrt{\epsilon}),

(3.101)

and

\displaystyle g_{\epsilon}(x)=(d-1)H(p)u_{k}^{\prime}(\delta_{k}(x)/\sqrt{\epsilon})-f_{1}(u_{k}(\delta_{k}(x)/\sqrt{\epsilon}))+o_{\epsilon}(1).

(3.102)

Here the nonzero function
f_{1}
, defined in (
3.4
), distinguishes (
3.100
)–(
3.102
) from (
2.56
)–(
2.57
).

To get the asymptotic expansion of
\varphi_{k,\epsilon}
in
\Omega_{k,\epsilon,\beta}
, we apply the methods of
Lemma

2.8
and
Proposition

2.9
(cf.
Section

2.2
) to (

)–(
3.99
) and define

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{k,p,\epsilon}(z)=\varphi_{k,\epsilon}(\Psi_{p}(\sqrt{\epsilon}z))\quad\text{for}~z\in\overline{B}_{\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2}}^{+}~\text{and}~0<\epsilon<\epsilon^{*}

(3.103)

(cf. (
2.62
)) where
\Psi_{p}
is given in (
2.1
).
Analogously to (

)–(

),
\mathcal{W}_{k,p,\epsilon}
satisfies

\displaystyle\sum_{i,j=1}^{d}a_{ij}(z)\frac{\partial^{2}\mathcal{W}_{k,p,\epsilon}}{\partial z^{i}z^{j}}+\sum_{j=1}^{d}b_{j}(z)\frac{\partial\mathcal{W}_{k,p,\epsilon}}{\partial z^{j}}+c_{\epsilon}(z)\mathcal{W}_{k,p,\epsilon}

\displaystyle=g_{\epsilon}(z)\quad

\displaystyle\text{in}~B_{\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2}}^{+},

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{k,p,\epsilon}-\gamma_{k}\partial_{z^{d}}\mathcal{W}_{k,p,\epsilon}

\displaystyle=0\quad

\displaystyle\text{on}~\overline{B}_{\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2}}^{+}\cap\partial\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d},

for
0<\epsilon<\epsilon^{*}
, where
a_{ij}
and

are given in (

)–(

), and

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} c_{\epsilon}(z)&=\frac{f_{0}(u_{k}(z^{d})+\sqrt{\epsilon}\mathcal{W}_{k,p,\epsilon}(z))-f_{0}(u_{k}(z^{d}))}{\sqrt{\epsilon}\mathcal{W}_{k,p,\epsilon}(z)}\\
&\quad+\frac{f_{1}(u_{k}(z^{d})+\sqrt{\epsilon}\mathcal{W}_{k,p,\epsilon}(z))-f_{1}(u_{k}(z^{d}))+o_{\epsilon}(1)}{\mathcal{W}_{k,p,\epsilon}(z)}\quad\text{if}~\mathcal{W}_{k,p,\epsilon}(z)\neq 0,\end{aligned}

\displaystyle c_{\epsilon}(z)=f_{0}^{\prime}(u_{k}(z^{d}))+\sqrt{\epsilon}(f_{1}^{\prime}(u_{k}(z^{d}))+o_{\epsilon}(1))\quad\text{if}~\mathcal{W}_{k,p,\epsilon}(z)=0

and

\displaystyle g_{\epsilon}(z)=(d-1)H(p)u_{k}^{\prime}(z^{d})-f_{1}(u_{k}(z^{d}))+o_{\epsilon}(1).

As in
Lemma

2.8
, we use the uniform boundedness of
\varphi_{k,\epsilon}
(cf.
5
) to prove

Lemma 3.14
.

For any sequence
\{\epsilon_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
of positive numbers with
\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\epsilon_{n}=0
,
\alpha\in(0,1)
, and
p\in\partial\Omega_{k}
(
k\in\{0,1,\dots,K\}
), there exists a subsequence
\{\epsilon_{nn}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
such that
\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\|\mathcal{W}_{k,p,\epsilon_{nn}}-\mathcal{W}_{k,p}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{2,\alpha}(\overline{B}_{m}^{+})}=0
for
m\in\mathbb{N}
, where
\mathcal{W}_{k,p}\in\mathcal{C}^{2,\alpha}_{\text{loc}}(\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{d})
satisfies

\displaystyle\Delta\mathcal{W}_{k,p}+f_{0}^{\prime}(u_{k})\mathcal{W}_{k,p}

\displaystyle=(d-1)H(p)u_{k}^{\prime}-f_{1}(u_{k})\quad

\displaystyle\text{in}~\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d},

(3.104)

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{k,p}-\gamma_{k}\partial_{z^{d}}\mathcal{W}_{k,p}

\displaystyle=0\quad

\displaystyle\text{on}~\partial\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}.

(3.105)

Moreover, there exists
M>0
such that
\mathcal{W}_{k,p}
satisfies the following estimate

\displaystyle|\mathcal{W}_{k,p}(z)-(d-1)H(p)v_{k}(z^{d})-w_{k}(z^{d})|\leq M\exp(-C_{3}z^{d}/8)

(3.106)

for
z=(z^{\prime},z^{d})\in\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{d}
and
z^{d}\geq 2(d-1)/C_{3}
, where
v_{k}
and
w_{k}
are solutions to (
1.22
)–(
1.24
) and (
1.25
)–(
1.27
), respectively.
In addition, the constant
C_{3}
is given in (
3.7
).

From
Lemma

3.14
, the solution
\mathcal{W}_{k,p}
to (
3.104
)–(
3.105
) may, a priori, depend on the sequence
\{\epsilon_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
.
To establish the indepedence from the sequence, we employ the moving plane arguments, as in
Proposition

2.9
to prove that
\mathcal{W}_{k,p}(z)=\mathcal{W}_{k,p}(z^{d})=(d-1)H(p)v_{k}(z^{d})+w_{k}(z^{d})
for
z=(z^{\prime},z^{d})\in\mathbb{R}_{+}^{d}
, where
v_{k}
and
w_{k}
are the unique solutions to (
1.22
)–(
1.24
) and (
1.25
)–(
1.27
). Here
v_{k}
and
w_{k}
are independent of the choice of sequence
\{\epsilon_{n}\}_{n=1}^{\infty}
and point
p\in\partial\Omega_{k}
.
Consequently, we can improve the results of
Lemma

3.14
as

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}\|\mathcal{W}_{k,p,\epsilon}-(d-1)H(p)v_{k}-w_{k}\|_{\mathcal{C}^{2,\alpha}(\overline{B}_{m}^{+})}=0\quad\text{for}~m\in\mathbb{N}~\text{and}~\alpha\in(0,1),

(3.107)

and

\displaystyle\lim_{\epsilon\to 0^{+}}\mathcal{W}_{k,p,\epsilon}(z)=(d-1)H(p)v_{k}(z^{d})+w_{k}(z^{d})\quad\text{for}~z=(z^{\prime},z^{d})\in\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{d},

(3.108)

where
\mathcal{W}_{k,p,\epsilon}
is defined in (
3.103
).
The details are stated as follows.

Proposition 3.15
.

For
p\in\partial\Omega_{k}
and
k\in\{0,1,\dots,K\}
, the solution
\mathcal{W}_{k,p}
to (
3.104
)–(
3.105
) satisfies

(a)

\mathcal{W}_{k,p}
depends only on the variable
z^{d}
, i.e.,
\mathcal{W}_{k,p}(z)=\mathcal{W}_{k,p}(z^{d})
for
z=(z^{\prime},z^{d})\in\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{d}
.

(b)

\mathcal{W}_{k,p}(z^{d})=(d-1)H(p)v_{k}(z^{d})+w_{k}(z^{d})
for
z^{d}\in[0,\infty)
, where
v_{k}
and
w_{k}
are the unique solutions to (
1.22
)–(
1.24
) and (
1.25
)–(
1.27
), respectively.

Proof.

Following
Proposition

2.9
, we replace
f
with
f_{0}
and apply the moving plane arguments to obtain (a).
By (a), (
1.24
), (
1.27
), and (
3.106
),
\mathcal{W}_{k,p}=\mathcal{W}_{k,p}(z^{d})
satisfies

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{k,p}^{\prime\prime}+f_{0}^{\prime}(u_{k})\mathcal{W}_{k,p}=(d-1)H(p)u_{k}^{\prime}-f_{1}(u_{k})\quad\text{in}~(0,\infty),

\displaystyle\mathcal{W}_{k,p}(0)-\gamma_{k}\mathcal{W}_{k,p}^{\prime}(0)=0,

\displaystyle\lim_{z^{d}\to\infty}\mathcal{W}_{k,p}(z^{d})=Q.

By the uniqueness of solutions to (
1.22
)–(
1.24
) and (
1.25
)–(
1.27
) (cf. (
C.4
) and (
D.9
)), we can conclude that
\mathcal{W}_{k,p}\equiv(d-1)H(p)v_{k}+w_{k}
, which gives (b).
This completes the proof of
Proposition

3.15
.
∎

We may use
Proposition

3.15
to prove (
3.107
), (
3.108
), and
\mathcal{W}_{k,p}(z)=(d-1)H(p)v_{k}(z^{d})+w_{k}(z^{d})
for
z=(z^{\prime},z^{d})\in\overline{\mathbb{R}}_{+}^{d}
.
Let
T>0
,
k\in\{0,1,\dots,K\}
, and
p\in\partial\Omega_{k}
.
Analogously to (
2.72
)–(
2.73
), we use (
2.1
), (
3.103
), (
3.107
)–(
3.108
), and
\nabla\delta_{k}(p-t\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p})=-\nu_{p}
to get

\displaystyle\varphi_{k,\epsilon}(p-t\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p})=(d-1)H(p)v_{k}(t)+w_{k}(t)+o_{\epsilon}(1),

(3.109)

\displaystyle\nabla\varphi_{k,p}(p-t\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p})=-\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\{[(d-1)H(p)v_{k}^{\prime}(t)+w_{k}^{\prime}(t)]\nu_{p}+o_{\epsilon}(1)]

(3.110)

for
0\leq t\leq T
as
\epsilon\to 0^{+}
.
Combining (
3.88
), (
3.103
) and (
3.109
)–(
3.110
), we arrive at

\displaystyle\phi_{\epsilon}(p-t\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p})=u_{k}(t)+\sqrt{\epsilon}\left((d-1)H(p)v_{k}(t)+w_{k}(t)+o_{\epsilon}(1)\right),

\displaystyle\nabla\phi_{\epsilon}(p-t\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p})=-\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}u_{k}^{\prime}(t)+(d-1)H(p)v_{k}^{\prime}(t)+w_{k}^{\prime}(t)\right)\nu_{p}+o_{\epsilon}(1)

for
0\leq t\leq T
as
\epsilon\to 0^{+}
, which implies (
1.17
)–(
1.18
).
Therefore, we complete the proof of

2
(a).

3.5
Proof of
Corollary

2

We present the proof of
Corollary

2
, following the structure of
Corollary

1
. We first prove (a). Integrating (
1.35
) over
\overline{\Omega}_{k,T,\epsilon}
and applying the coarea formula (cf.
[
18
,
37
]
) with (
2.75
), we get

\displaystyle\int_{\overline{\Omega}_{k,T,\epsilon}}\!f_{\epsilon}(\phi_{\epsilon}(x))\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\epsilon}\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\!\int_{0}^{T}\!f_{0}(u_{k}(t))\,\mathrm{d}t\,\mathrm{d}S_{p}

\displaystyle\quad+\epsilon\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\!\int_{0}^{T}\!\{(d-1)H(p)[f_{0}^{\prime}(u_{k}(t))v_{k}(t)-tf_{0}(u_{k}(t))]+f_{0}^{\prime}(u_{k}(t))w_{k}(t)+f_{1}(u_{k}(t))\}\,\mathrm{d}t\,\mathrm{d}S_{p}

\displaystyle\quad+\epsilon o_{\epsilon}(1).

Together with (
1.19
), (
1.22
), and (
1.25
), we obtain

\displaystyle\int_{\overline{\Omega}_{k,T,\epsilon}}\!f_{\epsilon}(\phi_{\epsilon}(x))\,\mathrm{d}x

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\epsilon}|\partial\Omega_{k}|\int_{0}^{T}\!(-u_{k}^{\prime\prime}(t))\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\quad+\epsilon\left[(d-1)\left(\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\!H(p)\,\mathrm{d}S_{p}\right)\left(\int_{0}^{T}\![u_{k}^{\prime}(t)-v_{k}^{\prime\prime}(t)+tu_{k}^{\prime\prime}(t)]\,\mathrm{d}t\right)+|\partial\Omega_{k}|\int_{0}^{T}\!(-w_{k}^{\prime\prime}(t))\,\mathrm{d}t\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\epsilon o_{\epsilon}(1)

\displaystyle=\sqrt{\epsilon}|\partial\Omega_{k}|(u_{k}^{\prime}(0)-u_{k}^{\prime}(T))

\displaystyle\quad+\epsilon\left[(d-1)\left(\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\!H(p)\,\mathrm{d}S_{p}\right)(Tu_{k}^{\prime}(T)+v_{k}^{\prime}(0)-v_{k}^{\prime}(T))+|\partial\Omega_{k}|(w_{k}^{\prime}(0)-w_{k}^{\prime}(T))\right]

\displaystyle\quad+\epsilon o_{\epsilon}(1),

which gives (a).

We now prove (b) as follows.
As in (
2.76
), we integrate (
1.2
) over
\overline{\Omega}_{k,T,\epsilon,\beta}
and apply the divergence theorem to obtain

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \int_{\overline{\Omega}_{k,T,\epsilon,\beta}}\!f_{\epsilon}(\phi_{\epsilon}(x))\,\mathrm{d}x&=-\epsilon\int_{\overline{\Omega}_{k,T,\epsilon,\beta}}\!\Delta\phi_{\epsilon}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\\
&=-\epsilon\int_{\partial\Omega_{k,T,\epsilon,\beta}}\partial_{\nu_{x}}\phi_{\epsilon}(x)\,\mathrm{d}S_{x}\\
&=-\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\!(\epsilon\partial_{\nu_{p}}\phi_{\epsilon}(p-T\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p}))\mathcal{J}(T,p)\,\mathrm{d}S_{p}\\
&\quad+\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\!(\epsilon\partial_{\nu_{p}}\phi_{\epsilon}(p-\epsilon^{\beta}\nu_{p}))\mathcal{J}(\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2},p)\,\mathrm{d}S_{p},\end{aligned}

(3.111)

where
\nu_{x}
is the unit outer normal at
x\in\partial\Omega_{k,T,\epsilon,\beta}
with respect to
\Omega_{k,T,\epsilon,\beta}
, and
\mathcal{J}(T,p)
and
\mathcal{J}(\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2},p)
are given in (
2.77
).
Then by (
1.18
), we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} &\quad-\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\!(\epsilon\partial_{\nu_{p}}\phi_{\epsilon}(p-T\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p}))\mathcal{J}(T,p)\,\mathrm{d}S_{p}\\
&=-\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\!(\epsilon\partial_{\nu_{p}}\phi_{\epsilon}(p-T\sqrt{\epsilon}\nu_{p}))[1-T\sqrt{\epsilon}(d-1)H(p)+\sqrt{\epsilon}o_{\epsilon}(1)]\,\mathrm{d}S_{p}\\
&=\sqrt{\epsilon}\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\{u_{k}^{\prime}(T)+\sqrt{\epsilon}(d-1)H(p)v_{k}^{\prime}(T)+w_{k}^{\prime}(T)+o_{\epsilon}(1)]\}[1-T\sqrt{\epsilon}(d-1)H(p)+\sqrt{\epsilon}o_{\epsilon}(1)]\,\mathrm{d}S_{p}\\
&=\sqrt{\epsilon}|\partial\Omega_{k}|u_{k}^{\prime}(T)+\epsilon\left[(d-1)\left(\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\!H(p)\,\mathrm{d}S_{p}\right)(-Tu_{k}^{\prime}(T)+v_{k}^{\prime}(T))+|\partial\Omega_{k}|w_{k}^{\prime}(T)\right]+\epsilon o_{\epsilon}(1).\end{aligned}

(3.112)

On the other hand, by
Theorem

2
(b,ii), we have

\displaystyle\left|\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\!(\epsilon\partial_{\nu_{p}}\phi_{\epsilon}(p-\epsilon^{\beta}\nu_{p}))\mathcal{J}(\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2},p)\,\mathrm{d}S_{p}\right|\leq\sqrt{\epsilon}|\partial\Omega_{k}|M^{\prime}\exp\left(-M\epsilon^{(2\beta-1)/2}\right).

(3.113)

Combining (
3.111
)–(
3.113
) with
0<\beta<1/2
, we get (b).
Following the argument as in (b), one may use (
3.113
) to prove (c).
Therefore, we complete the proof of
Corollary

2
.

As aforementioned in
Remark

6
, to complete the proof of (
3.76
), it is necessary to show that
Q
is uniquely determined by

\displaystyle Q=\frac{\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{K}\frac{|\partial\Omega_{k}|\hat{F}_{1}(u_{k}(0))+(d-1)\left(\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\!H(p)\,\mathrm{d}S_{p}\right)\left(\int_{0}^{\infty}\!u_{k}^{\prime 2}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s\right)}{u_{k}^{\prime}(0)+\gamma_{k}f_{0}(u_{k}(0))}}{\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{K}\frac{|\partial\Omega_{k}|f_{0}(u_{k}(0))}{u_{k}^{\prime}(0)+\gamma_{k}f_{0}(u_{k}(0))}}.

(3.114)

Using (
3.27
), (
3.75
), and
Corollary

2
, we find

\epsilon\left[(d-1)\sum_{k=0}^{K}\left(\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\!H(p)\,\mathrm{d}S_{p}\right)v_{k}^{\prime}(0)+\sum_{k=0}^{K}|\partial\Omega_{k}|w_{k}^{\prime}(0)\right]=\epsilon o_{\epsilon}(1),

which implies

(d-1)\sum_{k=0}^{K}\left(\int_{\partial\Omega_{k}}\!H(p)\,\mathrm{d}S_{p}\right)v_{k}^{\prime}(0)+\sum_{k=0}^{K}|\partial\Omega_{k}|w_{k}^{\prime}(0)=0.

(3.115)

By (
1.23
), (
1.26
), (
C.5
) (in
Appendix

C
), and (
D.10
) (in
Appendix

D
), a straightforward calculation gives (
3.114
).
Therefore, we complete the proof of (
3.76
) in
Remark

6
.

Final Remark

When the Robin boundary condition (
1.4
) is replaced by the Dirichlet boundary condition
\phi_{\epsilon}=\phi_{bd,k}
on
\partial\Omega_{k}
for
k=0,1,\dots,K
(i.e.,
\gamma_{k}=0
for
k=0,1,\dots,K
), the conclusions of
Theorems

1
and
2
hold true, particularly the asymptotic expansions (
1.5
)–(
1.6
) and (
1.17
)–(
1.18
), remain valid.
In (
1.5
)–(
1.6
) and (
1.17
)–(
1.18
), the solutions
u_{k}
,
v_{k}
and
w_{k}
to ODEs satisfy the Dirichlet boundary conditions
u_{k}(0)=\phi_{bd,k}
and
v_{k}(0)=w_{k}(0)=0
, which can be directly regarded as the Robin boundary conditions (
1.8
), (
1.11
), (
1.20
), (
1.23
) and (
1.26
) with
\gamma_{k}=0
.
By applying the maximum principle, the
L^{q}
-theory and Schauder estimate, the proofs of
Theorems

1
and
2
remain valid without other modifications for the Dirichlet boundary condition except
Proposition

3.6
.
When
\gamma_{k}=0
for all
k=0,1,\dots,K
, equations (
3.26
)–(
3.27
) are replaced by

\sum_{k=0}^{K}|\partial\Omega_{k}|u_{k}^{\prime}(0)=\sum_{k=0}^{K}|\partial\Omega_{k}|\operatorname{sgn}(\phi_{0}^{*}-\phi_{bd,k})\sqrt{\frac{2}{|\Omega|}{\sum_{i=1}^{I}m_{i}[\exp(-z_{i}(\phi_{bd,k}-\phi_{0}^{*}))-1]}}=0,

which determines the value of
\phi_{0}^{*}
(given in (
3.14
)).
The properties of ODEs remains true whenever
\gamma_{k}\geq 0
(see
Appendices

B
,
C
and
D
).

Conclusion

In this work, we present a rigorous analysis of boundary layer solutions to Poisson–Boltzmann (PB) type equations in general smooth domains.
We extend the classical boundary layer theory to multiply connected domains via the moving plane arguments, and address the challenge posed by the nonlocal nonlinearity in charge-conserving PB equations using novel integral estimates.
In the near-boundary region (where the distance to the boundary is of order
\sqrt{\epsilon}\mathcal{O}(1)
), we construct asymptotic expansions based on solutions to associated ordinary differential equations, capturing the effect of mean curvature through second-order approximations.
In the far-field region, we establish exponential decay estimates, which reveal the localized nature of boundary layer phenomena.
Our analysis quantifies how domain geometry, particularly mean curvature, influences electrostatic characteristics such as electric potential, electric field, total ionic charge and total ionic charge density.
These results highlight the explicit dependence of electrostatic screening on geometric features and provide analytical tools for applications in electrochemistry (e.g., porous electrodes), biophysics (e.g. ion channels), and microfluidics.

Appendix A
Exponential-type estimate of radial solution

Here we study the radial solutions
\Phi_{\epsilon}=\Phi_{\epsilon}(r)
to equations
(
1.1
)
and (
3.1
) in the ball
B_{R}(0)
with the Dirichlet boundary condition, respectively.
Equation (
1.1
) with the Dirichlet boundary condition can be denoted as

\displaystyle-\epsilon(r^{d-1}\Phi_{\epsilon}^{\prime}(r))^{\prime}=r^{d-1}f(\Phi_{\epsilon}(r))\quad\text{for}~r\in(0,R),

(A.1)

\displaystyle\Phi_{\epsilon}^{\prime}(0)=0,

(A.2)

\displaystyle\Phi_{\epsilon}(R)=\Phi_{bd},

(A.3)

where
f\in\mathcal{C}^{1}(\mathbb{R})
is a strictly decreasing function satisfying (A1)–(A2), and
\Phi_{bd}
is a constant independent of
\epsilon
.
Note that if
\Phi_{bd}=\phi^{*}
(
\phi^{*}
is the unique zero of
f
), then by the uniqueness of (
A.1
)–(
A.3
),
\Phi_{\epsilon}\equiv\phi^{*}
is a trivial solution.
Hence we assume
\Phi_{bd}\neq\phi^{*}
. By (A1)–(A2), it is easy to prove

Proposition A.1
.

Assume that
f
satisfies (A1)–(A2).
Let
\Phi_{\epsilon}
be the solution to (
A.1
)–(
A.3
) for
\epsilon>0
.

(a)

If
\Phi_{bd}>\phi^{*}
, then
\Phi_{\epsilon}(0)>\phi^{*}
and
\Phi_{\epsilon}
is strictly increasing;

(b)

if
\Phi_{bd}<\phi^{*}
, then
\Phi_{\epsilon}(0)<\phi^{*}
and
\Phi_{\epsilon}
is strictly decreasing.

Proposition A.2
.

Assume that
f
satisfies (A1)–(A2).
Let
\Phi_{\epsilon}
be the solution to (
A.1
)–(
A.3
) for
\epsilon>0
. Then

\displaystyle|\Phi_{\epsilon}(r)-\phi^{*}|\leq 2|\Phi_{bd}-\phi^{*}|\exp\left(-\frac{m_{f}(R-r)}{8\sqrt{\epsilon}}\right)

(A.4)

for
r\in[0,R]
and
0<\epsilon<(m_{f}R)^{2}/[8(d-1)^{2}]
, where
m_{f}=m_{f}([\min\{\phi^{*},\Phi_{bd}\},\max\{\phi^{*},\Phi_{bd}\}])
is given in (A1).

Proof.

Let
\overline{\Phi}_{\epsilon}:=\Phi_{\epsilon}-\phi^{*}
on
[0,R]
for
\epsilon>0
.
Then from (
A.1
)–(
A.3
),
\overline{\Phi}_{\epsilon}
satisfies

\displaystyle-\epsilon(r^{d-1}\overline{\Phi}_{\epsilon}^{\prime}(r))^{\prime}=r^{d-1}f(\phi^{*}+\overline{\Phi}_{\epsilon}(r))\quad\text{for}~r\in(0,R),

(A.5)

\displaystyle\overline{\Phi}_{\epsilon}^{\prime}(0)=0,

(A.6)

\displaystyle\overline{\Phi}_{\epsilon}(R)=\Phi_{bd}-\phi^{*}.

(A.7)

By (A1)–(A2), we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} f(\phi^{*}+\overline{\Phi}_{\epsilon}(r))\overline{\Phi}_{\epsilon}(r)&=\left(\int_{0}^{1}\!\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}s}f(\phi^{*}+s\overline{\Phi}_{\epsilon}(r))\,\mathrm{d}s\right)\overline{\Phi}_{\epsilon}(r)\\
&=\left(\int_{0}^{1}\!f^{\prime}(\phi^{*}+s\overline{\Phi}_{\epsilon}(r))\,\mathrm{d}s\right)\overline{\Phi}_{\epsilon}^{2}(r)\leq-m_{f}^{2}\overline{\Phi}_{\epsilon}^{2}(r)\end{aligned}

(A.8)

for
r\in(0,R)
.
Here we have used the fact that
\overline{\Phi}_{\epsilon}=\Phi_{\epsilon}-\phi^{*}
is uniformly bounded (cf.
Proposition

2.1
also works for the Dirichlet boundary condition (
A.3
)).
By (
A.5
) and (
A.8
), we obtain

\displaystyle\epsilon(\overline{\Phi}_{\epsilon}^{2}(r))^{\prime\prime}

\displaystyle=2\epsilon\overline{\Phi}_{\epsilon}^{\prime 2}(r)+2\epsilon\overline{\Phi}_{\epsilon}^{\prime\prime}(r)\overline{\Phi}_{\epsilon}(r)

(A.9)

\displaystyle=2\epsilon\overline{\Phi}_{\epsilon}^{\prime 2}(r)-\frac{2\epsilon(d-1)}{r}\overline{\Phi}_{\epsilon}^{\prime}(r)\overline{\Phi}_{\epsilon}(r)-2f(\phi^{*}+\overline{\Phi}_{\epsilon}(r))\overline{\Phi}_{\epsilon}(r)

\displaystyle\geq 2\epsilon\overline{\Phi}_{\epsilon}^{\prime 2}(r)-\frac{2\epsilon(d-1)}{r}\overline{\Phi}_{\epsilon}^{\prime}(r)\overline{\Phi}_{\epsilon}(r)+2m_{f}^{2}\overline{\Phi}_{\epsilon}^{2}(r)\quad\text{for}~r\in(0,R).

On the other hand, by Young’s inequality, we have

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \frac{2\epsilon(d-1)}{r}\overline{\Phi}_{\epsilon}^{\prime}(r)\overline{\Phi}_{\epsilon}(r)&\leq 2\epsilon\overline{\Phi}_{\epsilon}^{\prime 2}(r)+\frac{\epsilon(d-1)^{2}}{2r^{2}}\overline{\Phi}_{\epsilon}^{2}(r)\\
&\leq 2\epsilon\overline{\Phi}_{\epsilon}^{\prime 2}(r)+\frac{8\epsilon(d-1)^{2}}{R^{2}}\overline{\Phi}_{\epsilon}^{2}(r)\quad\text{for}~r\in[R/4,R).\end{aligned}

(A.10)

Combining (
A.9
) and (
A.10
), we get

\displaystyle\epsilon(\overline{\Phi}_{\epsilon}^{2}(r))^{\prime\prime}\geq\left(2m_{f}^{2}-\frac{8\epsilon(d-1)^{2}}{R^{2}}\right)\overline{\Phi}_{\epsilon}^{2}(r)\geq m_{f}^{2}\overline{\Phi}_{\epsilon}^{2}(r)\quad\text{for}~r\in[R/4,R).

(A.11)

Here we have used that
0<\epsilon<(m_{f}R)^{2}/[8(d-1)^{2}]
.
Applying the comparison theorem to (
A.11
), it yields

\displaystyle\overline{\Phi}_{\epsilon}^{2}(r)\leq\max\{\overline{\Phi}_{\epsilon}^{2}(R/4),\overline{\Phi}_{\epsilon}^{2}(R)\}\left[\exp\left(-\frac{m_{f}(R-r)}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\right)+\exp\left(-\frac{m_{f}(r-R/4)}{\sqrt{\epsilon}}\right)\right]

(A.12)

for
r\in[R/4,R]
.
By
Proposition

A.1
,
(\overline{\Phi}_{\epsilon}^{2}(r))^{\prime}=2\overline{\Phi}_{\epsilon}^{\prime}(r)\overline{\Phi}_{\epsilon}(r)>0
on
(0,R)
, and
\overline{\Phi}^{2}
is strictly increasing on
[0,R]
.
Consequently, we have
\overline{\Phi}_{\epsilon}^{2}(R/4)<\overline{\Phi}_{\epsilon}^{2}(R)=(\Phi_{bd}-\phi^{*})^{2}
.
From (
A.12
), we get

|\overline{\Phi}_{\epsilon}(r)|\leq|\Phi_{bd}-\phi^{*}|\left[\exp\left(-\frac{m_{f}(R-r)}{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}\right)+\exp\left(-\frac{m_{f}R}{8\sqrt{\epsilon}}\right)\right]\quad\text{for}~r\in[3R/4,R],

which implies

\displaystyle|\overline{\Phi}_{\epsilon}(r)|\leq 2|\Phi_{bd}-\phi^{*}|\exp\left(-\frac{m_{f}(R-r)}{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}\right)\quad\text{for}~r\in[3R/4,R].

(A.13)

Since
\overline{\Phi}_{\epsilon}^{2}
is strictly increasing on
[0,R]
,
|\overline{\Phi}|
is also strictly increasing on
[0,R]
.
Then by (
A.13
), we have

\displaystyle|\overline{\Phi}_{\epsilon}(r)|\leq|\overline{\Phi}_{\epsilon}(3R/4)|\leq 2|\Phi_{bd}-\phi^{*}|\exp\left(-\frac{m_{f}R}{8\sqrt{\epsilon}}\right)\leq 2|\Phi_{bd}-\phi^{*}|\exp\left(-\frac{m_{f}(R-r)}{8\sqrt{\epsilon}}\right)

(A.14)

for
r\in[0,3R/4]
.
Combining (
A.13
) and (
A.14
), we arrive at (
A.4
) and complete the proof of
Proposition

A.2
.
∎

Now we consider the radial solution
\Phi_{\epsilon}=\Phi_{\epsilon}(r)
to equation (
3.1
) with the Dirichlet boundary condition, which satisfies

\displaystyle-\epsilon(r^{d-1}\Phi_{\epsilon}^{\prime}(r))^{\prime}=r^{d-1}f_{\epsilon}(\Phi_{\epsilon}(r))\quad\text{for}~r\in(0,R),

(A.15)

\displaystyle\Phi_{\epsilon}^{\prime}(0)=0,

(A.16)

\displaystyle\Phi_{\epsilon}(R)=\Phi_{bd},

(A.17)

where
f_{\epsilon}
has a unique zero
\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}\in\mathbb{R}
and satisfies
f_{\epsilon}^{\prime}(\phi)\leq-M^{2}<0
for
\phi\in\mathbb{R}
. Note that
f_{\epsilon}
and its zero
\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}
may depend on
\epsilon
but
M
is independent of
\epsilon
.

Analogous to
Propositions

A.1
and
A.2
(with
\phi^{*}
replaced by
\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}
), we can prove

Proposition A.3
.

Assume that
f_{\epsilon}
has a unique zero
\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}\in\mathbb{R}
and satisfies
f_{\epsilon}^{\prime}(\phi)\leq-M^{2}<0
for
\phi\in\mathbb{R}
and
\epsilon>0
, where
M
is independent of
\epsilon
.
Let
\Phi_{\epsilon}
be the solution to (
A.15
)–(
A.17
) for
\epsilon>0
.

(a)

If
\Phi_{bd}>\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}
for
\epsilon>0
, then
\Phi_{\epsilon}(0)>\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}
and
\Phi_{\epsilon}
is strictly increasing for
\epsilon>0
;

(b)

if
\Phi_{bd}<\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}
for
\epsilon>0
, then
\Phi_{\epsilon}(0)<\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}
and
\Phi_{\epsilon}
is strictly decreasing for
\epsilon>0
.

Proposition A.4
.

Assume that
f_{\epsilon}
has a unique zero
\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}\in\mathbb{R}
and satisfies
f_{\epsilon}^{\prime}(\phi)\leq-M^{2}<0
for
\phi\in\mathbb{R}
and
\epsilon>0
, where
M
is independent of
\epsilon
.
Let
\Phi_{\epsilon}
be the solution to (
A.15
)–(
A.17
) for
\epsilon>0
. Then

\displaystyle|\phi_{\epsilon}(r)-\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}|\leq 2|\Phi_{bd}-\phi_{\epsilon}^{*}|\exp\left(-\frac{M(R-r)}{8\sqrt{\epsilon}}\right)

(A.18)

for
r\in[0,R]
and
0<\epsilon<(MR)^{2}/[8(d-1)^{2}]
.

Appendix B
Properties of the solution to (
1.7
)–(
1.9
) and (
1.19
)–(
1.21
)

In this appendix, we establish the existence, uniqueness, qualitative properties, and asymptotic behaviors of solutions to (
1.7
)–(
1.9
) and (
1.19
)–(
1.21
).
For the sake of simplicity, equations (
1.7
)–(
1.9
) and (
1.19
)–(
1.21
) can be represented as follows.

\displaystyle U^{\prime\prime}+f(U)=0\quad\text{in}~(0,\infty),

(B.1)

\displaystyle U(0)-\gamma\,U^{\prime}(0)=\Phi_{bd},

(B.2)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}U(t)=\phi^{*},

(B.3)

where
\gamma\geq 0
and
\Phi_{bd}\neq\phi^{*}
are fixed constants. (When
\Phi_{bd}=\phi^{*}
, the solution
U\equiv\phi^{*}
is trivial.)
Here the function
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
satisfies assumptions (A1) and (A2).
It is clear that the existence and uniqueness of solution to equations (
1.7
)–(
1.9
) and (
1.19
)–(
1.21
) for
k=0,1,\dots,K
follow from that of solution to equations (
B.1
)–(
B.3
).
We may approach (
B.1
)–(
B.3
) by the following initial-value problem

\displaystyle\tilde{U}^{\prime\prime}+f(\tilde{U})=0\quad\text{in}~(0,\infty),

(B.4)

\displaystyle\tilde{U}(0)=U_{0},

(B.5)

\displaystyle\tilde{U}^{\prime}(0)=U_{0}^{\prime},

(B.6)

where
U_{0}
(between
\phi^{*}
and
\Phi_{bd}
) and
U_{0}^{\prime}
are constants to be determined such that
\tilde{U}\equiv U
on
[0,\infty)
.
The existence and uniqueness of (
B.4
)–(
B.6
) follow from the standard ODE theory.
From (
B.2
) and (
B.5
)–(
B.6
),
U_{0}
and
U_{0}^{\prime}
must satisfy

\displaystyle U_{0}-\gamma U_{0}^{\prime}=\Phi_{bd}.

(B.7)

Multiplying (
B.4
) by
\tilde{U}^{\prime}
and then integrating it over
[0,t]
, we can use (
B.5
)–(
B.6
) to get

\displaystyle\tilde{U}^{\prime 2}(t)+2F(\tilde{U}(t))=U_{0}^{\prime 2}+2F(U_{0})\quad\text{for}~t\geq 0,

(B.8)

where
F(\phi)=\int_{\phi^{*}}^{\phi}\!f(s)\,\mathrm{d}s
for
\phi\in\mathbb{R}
.
Note that
F^{\prime}(\phi^{*})=f(\phi^{*})=0
and
F^{\prime\prime}(\phi)=f^{\prime}(\phi)<0
for
\phi\in\mathbb{R}
(by (A1)–(A2)) so

\displaystyle 0=F(\phi^{*})>F(\phi)\quad\text{for}~\phi\neq\phi^{*}.

(B.9)

Suppose that
\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\tilde{U}^{\prime}(t)=0
.
Then we use (
B.3
) and (
B.7
)–(
B.8
) to find that
U_{0}
(between
\phi^{*}
and
\Phi_{bd}
) must satisfy

\displaystyle\Phi_{bd}-U_{0}=\operatorname{sgn}(\Phi_{bd}-\phi^{*})\gamma\sqrt{-2F(U_{0})}.

(B.10)

To show that there exists a unique
U_{0}
, between
\phi^{*}
and
\Phi_{bd}
, satisfying (
B.10
), we define a function
g:\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
by

g(s)=\Phi_{bd}-s-\operatorname{sgn}(\Phi_{bd}-\phi^{*})\gamma\sqrt{-2F(s)}\quad\text{for}~s\in\mathbb{R},

which is well-defined because of (
B.9
).
Since
g(\phi^{*})g(\Phi_{bd})<0
and
g^{\prime}(s)<0
for
s
between
\phi^{*}
and
\Phi_{bd}
, function
g
has a unique zero
U_{0}
between
\phi^{*}
and
\Phi_{bd}
, which means that equation (
B.10
) has a unique solution.
Here one may use the facts that (i) if
\Phi_{bd}>\phi^{*}
, then
f(s)<0
for
s\in(\phi^{*},\Phi_{bd}]
and (ii) if
\Phi_{bd}<\phi^{*}
, then
f(s)>0
for
s\in[\Phi_{bd},\phi^{*})
due to (A1)–(A2).
Therefore, the initial-value problem (
B.4
)–(
B.6
) can be reformulated as

\displaystyle\tilde{U}^{\prime\prime}+f(\tilde{U})=0\quad\text{in}~(0,\infty),

(B.11)

\displaystyle\tilde{U}(0)=U_{0},

(B.12)

\displaystyle\tilde{U}^{\prime}(0)=\operatorname{sgn}(\phi^{*}-\Phi_{bd})\sqrt{-2F(U_{0})},

(B.13)

where
U_{0}
(between
\phi^{*}
and
\Phi_{bd}
) is uniquely determined by (
B.10
).

Figure 4
:
We sketch the numerical profile for the solution
U
to (
B.1
)–(
B.3
) for the case (a)
\Phi_{bd}=1
and the case (b)
\Phi_{bd}=-1
, which is consistent with
Proposition

B.1
.
Here
f(\phi)=\exp(\phi)-\exp(-\phi)
and
\gamma=0.1
.

To prove
\tilde{U}\equiv U
on
[0,\infty)
, we need the following proposition.

Proposition B.1
.

Let
\tilde{U}
be the solution to (
B.11
)–(
B.13
).

(a)

If
\Phi_{bd}>\phi^{*}
, then
\Phi_{bd}\geq\tilde{U}(t)>\phi^{*}
and
\tilde{U}^{\prime}(t)<0
for
t\geq 0
;

(b)

if
\Phi_{bd}<\phi^{*}
, then
\Phi_{bd}\leq\tilde{U}(t)<\phi^{*}
and
\tilde{U}^{\prime}(t)>0
for
t\geq 0
.

Proof.

We firstly claim that
\tilde{U}^{\prime}(t)\neq 0
for
t\geq 0
.
Suppose by contradiction that there exists
t_{1}\in(0,\infty)
such that
\tilde{U}^{\prime}(t_{1})=0
.
Multiplying (
B.11
) by
\tilde{U}
and then integrating it over
[0,t]
, we obtain

\displaystyle\tilde{U}^{\prime 2}(t)=\tilde{U}^{\prime 2}(0)+2(F(\tilde{U}(0))-F(\tilde{U}(t)))=-2F(\tilde{U}(t))\quad\text{for}~t\geq 0.

(B.14)

Here we have used (
B.12
)–(
B.13
).
By (
B.14
) and
\tilde{U}^{\prime}(t_{1})=0
, we get
\tilde{U}(t_{1})=\phi^{*}
, which implies
\tilde{U}\equiv\phi^{*}
on
[t_{1},\infty)
.
Due to the unique continuation,
U\equiv\phi^{*}
on
[0,\infty)
, which contradicts to (
B.12
).
Hence
\tilde{U}^{\prime}(t)\neq 0
for
t\geq 0
.
By (
B.9
) and (
B.14
), it is clear that
\tilde{U}(t)\neq\phi^{*}
for
t\geq 0
because
\tilde{U}^{\prime}(t)\neq 0
for
t\geq 0
.

Now we prove (a) and (b).
For the case of (a), we assume that
\Phi_{bd}>\phi^{*}
.
From (
B.10
) and (
B.12
)–(
B.13
), we have
\Phi_{bd}\geq\tilde{U}(0)>\phi^{*}
and
\tilde{U}^{\prime}(0)<0
.
Since
\tilde{U}(t)\neq\phi^{*}
and
\tilde{U}^{\prime}(t)\neq 0
for
t\geq 0
, which implies
\Phi_{bd}\geq\tilde{U}(t)>\phi^{*}
and
\tilde{U}^{\prime}(t)<0
for
t\geq 0
.
Thus, the proof of (a) is complete.
The proof of (b) is similar to (a).
Therefore, we complete the proof of
Proposition

B.1
.∎

Now we are in the position to show that
\tilde{U}
solves equations (
B.1
)–(
B.3
) and the uniqueness of (
B.1
)–(
B.3
), which implies
\tilde{U}\equiv U
in
[0,\infty)
.
By (
B.10
) and (
B.12
)–(
B.13
), we have

\tilde{U}(0)-\gamma\tilde{U}^{\prime}(0)=U_{0}\gamma\operatorname{sgn}(\phi^{*}-\Phi_{bd})\sqrt{-2F(U_{0})}=\Phi_{bd},

which means
\tilde{U}
satisfies (
B.2
).
By
Proposition

B.1
,
\tilde{U}
is bounded and strictly monotonic on
[0,\infty)
, which implies that there exists a constant
C
such that

\lim_{t\to\infty}\tilde{U}(t)=C.

Suppose by contradiction that
C\neq\phi^{*}
.
By (
B.9
) and (
B.12
)–(
B.14
), we get

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\tilde{U}^{\prime 2}(t)=\tilde{U}^{\prime 2}(0)+2(F(U_{0})-F(C))=-2F(C)>0.

(B.15)

Since
\tilde{U}^{\prime}(t)\neq 0
for
t\geq 0
, we can use (
B.15
) to get
\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}|\tilde{U}(t)|=\infty
, which leads to a contradiction.
Thus,
\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\tilde{U}(t)=\phi^{*}
, i.e.,
\tilde{U}
satisfies (
B.3
).
Hence
\tilde{U}
solves equations (
B.1
)–(
B.3
).
To prove
\tilde{U}\equiv U
on
[0,\infty)
, it suffices to show the uniqueness of solution to (
B.1
)–(
B.3
).
Suppose that there exist two solutions
U_{1}
and
U_{2}
to equations (
B.1
)–(
B.3
).
Let
\overline{U}=U_{1}-U_{2}
.
Then
\overline{U}
satisfies

\displaystyle\overline{U}^{\prime\prime}+c(t)\overline{U}=0\quad\text{in}~(0,\infty),

(B.16)

\displaystyle\overline{U}(0)-\gamma\,\overline{U}^{\prime}(0)=0,

(B.17)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\overline{U}(t)=0,

(B.18)

where

c(t)=\begin{cases}\displaystyle\frac{f(U_{1}(t))-f(U_{2}(t))}{U_{1}(t)-U_{2}(t)}&\text{if}~U_{1}(t)\neq U_{2}(t);\\
f^{\prime}(U_{1}(t))&\text{if}~U_{1}(t)=U_{2}(t).\end{cases}

Note that
c(t)<0
for
t\geq 0
by (A1).
Thus, by the standard maximum principle,
\overline{U}\equiv 0
on
[0,\infty)
, and we obtain the uniqueness of solution to (
B.1
)–(
B.3
).
Therefore,
\tilde{U}\equiv U
on
[0,\infty)
and hence
U
satisfies
Proposition

B.1
and (
B.14
).

The following result will be used in
Appendix

C
to prove the existence of solution to (
1.10
)–(
1.12
) and (
1.22
)–(
1.24
).

Proposition B.2
.

Let
U
be the solution to (
B.1
)–(
B.3
). Then we have

(a)

|U^{\prime}(t)|\leq|U^{\prime}(0)|\exp(-m_{f}t)
for
t\geq 0
, where
m_{f}=m_{f}([\min\{\phi^{*},\Phi_{bd}\},\max\{\phi^{*},\Phi_{bd}\}])
.

(b)

\int_{0}^{\infty}\!U^{\prime 2}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t=\operatorname{sgn}(\phi^{*}-\Phi_{bd})\int_{U_{0}}^{\phi^{*}}\!\sqrt{-2F(s)}\,\mathrm{d}s<\infty
.

(c)

\int_{0}^{t}\!(U^{\prime}(s))^{-2}\,\mathrm{d}s\geq t^{2}(\int_{0}^{\infty}\!U^{\prime 2}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s)^{-1}
for
t\geq 0
.

(d)

\lim_{t\to\infty}[f(U(t))/U^{\prime}(t)]=-\sqrt{-f^{\prime}(\phi^{*})}
.

(e)

\int_{0}^{\infty}\!(U^{\prime}(s_{1}))^{-2}\int_{s_{1}}^{\infty}\!U^{\prime 2}(s_{2})\,\mathrm{d}s_{2}\,\mathrm{d}s_{1}=\infty
.

The proof of
Proposition

B.2
is easy by
Proposition

B.1
and calculus so we omit it here.

Appendix C
Properties of the solution to (
1.10
)–(
1.12
) and (
1.22
)–(
1.24
)

In this appendix, we establish the existence, uniqueness, and qualitative properties for solutions to (
1.10
)–(
1.12
) and (
1.22
)–(
1.24
).
For the sake of simplicity, equations (
1.10
)–(
1.12
) and (
1.22
)–(
1.24
) can be represented as follows.

\displaystyle V^{\prime\prime}+f^{\prime}(U)V=U^{\prime}\quad\text{in}~(0,\infty),

(C.1)

\displaystyle V(0)-\gamma\,V^{\prime}(0)=0,

(C.2)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}V(t)=0,

(C.3)

where
U
is the solution to equations (
B.1
)–(
B.3
),
f\in\mathcal{C}^{\infty}(\mathbb{R})
and
\gamma\geq 0
are as given in (
B.1
)–(
B.2
).
By standard ODE theory, the solution
V
to equations (
C.1
)–(
C.3
) can be represented as

\displaystyle V(t)=\frac{V_{0}}{U^{\prime}(0)}U^{\prime}(t)-U^{\prime}(t)\int_{0}^{t}\frac{1}{U^{\prime 2}(s_{1})}\int_{s_{1}}^{\infty}\!U^{\prime 2}(s_{2})\,\mathrm{d}s_{2}\,\mathrm{d}s_{1}\quad\text{for}~t\geq 0,

(C.4)

where
V_{0}
is given by

\displaystyle V_{0}=-\frac{\gamma}{U^{\prime}(0)+\gamma f(U_{0})}\int_{0}^{\infty}\!U^{\prime 2}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t.

(C.5)

Note that
V_{0}
is well-defined due to
Proposition

B.2
(b) and
U^{\prime}(0)+\gamma f(U_{0})\neq 0
.
Clearly, (
C.4
) with (
C.5
) satisfies (
C.1
)–(
C.2
).
It suffices to verify that (
C.4
) satisfies (
C.3
).
By (
B.1
), (
B.3
), (A1)–(A2),
Proposition

B.2
and L’Hôpital’s rule, we obtain
\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}V(t)=\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}(U^{\prime}(t)/f^{\prime}(U(t)))=0
.
Thus, (
C.4
) defines a solution to (
C.1
)–(
C.3
).

Now we prove the uniqueness of the solution to (
C.1
)–(
C.3
).
Suppose that there exist two solutions
V
and
\tilde{V}
to (
C.1
)–(
C.3
).
Let
\overline{V}=V-\tilde{V}
.
Then
\overline{V}
satisfies

\displaystyle\overline{V}^{\prime\prime}+f^{\prime}(U)\overline{V}=0\quad\text{in}~(0,\infty),

(C.6)

\displaystyle\overline{V}(0)-\gamma\overline{V}^{\prime}(0)=0,

(C.7)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\overline{V}(t)=0.

(C.8)

Hence by (A1) and the standard maximum principle,
\overline{V}\equiv 0
on
[0,\infty)
, and the solution to (
C.1
)–(
C.3
) must be given by (
C.4
).

Proposition C.1
.

Let
V
be the unique solution to (
C.1
)–(
C.3
).

(a)

If
\Phi_{bd}>\phi^{*}
, then
V
is nonnegative on
[0,\infty)
, and there exists a unique
t^{*}\in(0,\infty)
such that
V
is strictly increasing on
[0,t^{*})
and strictly decreasing on
(t^{*},\infty)
. Moreover, if
\gamma>0
, then
V
is positive on
[0,\infty)
.

(b)

If
\Phi_{bd}<\phi^{*}
, then
V
is nonpositive on
[0,\infty)
, and there exists a unique
t^{*}\in(0,\infty)
such that
V
is strictly decreasing on
[0,t^{*})
and strictly increasing on
(t^{*},\infty)
. Moreover, if
\gamma>0
, then
V
is negative on
[0,\infty)
.

Proof.

We first note that

Claim 6
.

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}V^{\prime}(t)=0
.

Proof of
6
.

By (
B.1
) and (
C.4
), we have

V^{\prime}(t)=-\frac{V_{0}}{U^{\prime}(0)}f(U(t))+f(U(t))\int_{0}^{t}\!\frac{1}{U^{\prime 2}(s_{1})}\int_{s_{1}}^{\infty}\!U^{\prime 2}(s_{2})\,\mathrm{d}s_{2}\,\mathrm{d}s_{1}-\frac{1}{U^{\prime}(t)}\int_{t}^{\infty}\!U^{\prime 2}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s.

By (A2), (
B.1
) and
Proposition

B.2
(d), we apply the L’Hôpital’s rule to obtain

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}V^{\prime}(t)

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\to\infty}\left[\frac{\displaystyle\int_{0}^{t}\!\frac{1}{U^{\prime 2}(s_{1})}\int_{s_{1}}^{\infty}\!U^{\prime 2}(s_{2})\,\mathrm{d}s_{2}\,\mathrm{d}s_{1}}{[f(U(t)]^{-1}}-\frac{\displaystyle\int_{t}^{\infty}\!U^{\prime 2}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s}{U^{\prime}(t)}\right]=\lim_{t\to\infty}\left[\frac{\displaystyle\frac{1}{U^{\prime 2}(t)}\int_{t}^{\infty}\!U^{\prime 2}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s}{[f(U(t))]^{-2}f^{\prime}(U(t))U^{\prime}(t)}+\frac{U^{\prime 2}(t)}{U^{\prime\prime}(t)}\right]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{f^{\prime}(\phi^{*})}\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{\displaystyle[f(U(t))]^{2}\int_{t}^{\infty}\!U^{\prime 2}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s}{U^{\prime 3}(t)}-\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{U^{\prime 2}(t)}{f(U(t))}=-\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{\displaystyle\int_{t}^{\infty}\!U^{\prime 2}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s}{U^{\prime}(t)}=\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{U^{\prime 2}(t)}{U^{\prime\prime}(t)}

\displaystyle=-\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{U^{\prime 2}(t)}{f(U(t))}=0,

which completes the proof of
6
.
∎

We now assume
\Phi_{bd}>\phi^{*}
and prove (a).
By
Proposition

B.1
(a), we have
U^{\prime}(0)<0
and
f(U_{0})<0
. Along with (
C.5
), we get
V(0)=V_{0}>0
, and
V^{\prime}(0)=-(U^{\prime}(0)+\gamma f(U_{0}))^{-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}\!U^{\prime 2}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t>0
.
Suppose by contradiction that there exists
t_{0}\in(0,\infty)
such that
V(t_{0})\leq 0
.
Since
\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}V(t)=0
, we may assume that
V
attains its minimum value at
t_{0}\in(0,\infty)
, which implies
V^{\prime\prime}(t_{0})\geq 0
.
Then by (
C.1
), we have
U^{\prime}(t_{0})=V^{\prime\prime}(t_{0})+f^{\prime}(U(t_{0}))V(t_{0})\geq 0
, which contradicts Proposition
B.1
(a).
This shows that
V
is positive on
[0,\infty)
.
Since
V^{\prime}(0)>0
and
\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}V(t)=0
, there exists
t^{*}\in(0,\infty)
such that
V
attains its maximum value at
t^{*}
and
V^{\prime}(t^{*})=0
.
Suppose by contradiction that there exists another maximum point
t_{1}
such that
V^{\prime}(t_{1})=0
.
Without loss of generality, we may assume that
t_{1}<t^{*}
, and there exists
t_{2}\in(t_{1},t^{*})
such that
V
attains its local minimum value at
t_{2}
with
V^{\prime}(t_{2})=0
and
V^{\prime}(t)\geq 0
on
(t_{2},t^{*})
.
Integrating (
C.1
) over
[t,\infty)
and using (a), we obtain

\displaystyle V^{\prime}(t)=\frac{f(U(t))V(t)+\int_{t}^{\infty}\!U^{\prime 2}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s}{-U^{\prime}(t)}\quad\text{for}~t\geq 0.

(C.9)

Clearly,
V^{\prime}(t_{2})=V^{\prime}(t^{*})=0
.
Let
G(t)=f(U(t))V(t)+\int_{t}^{\infty}\!U^{\prime 2}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s
for
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Then
G(t_{2})=G(t^{*})=0
.
Differentiating
G
gives

G^{\prime}(t)=f^{\prime}(U(t))U^{\prime}(t)V(t)+f(U(t))V^{\prime}(t)-U^{\prime 2}(t)\quad\text{for}~t\in\mathbb{R}.

By Proposition
B.1
(a) and (A1)–(A2), we find that
U^{\prime}(t)<0
,
f(U(t))<0
and
f^{\prime}(U(t))<0
.
Since
V(t)>0
and
V^{\prime}(t)>0
on
(t_{2},t^{*})
, we get
G^{\prime}(t)<0
on
(t_{2},t^{*})
, which contradcits with
g(t_{2})=g(t^{*})=0
.
Therefore, there exists a unique
t^{*}\in(0,\infty)
such that
V
attains its maximum value.
The proof of (a) is complete.

Next we assume
\Phi_{bd}<\phi^{*}
and prove (b).
By
Proposition

B.1
(b), we have
U^{\prime}(0)>0
and
f(U_{0})>0
. Along with (
C.5
), we get
V(0)=V_{0}<0
, and
V^{\prime}(0)=-(U^{\prime}(0)+\gamma f(U_{0}))^{-1}\int_{0}^{\infty}\!U^{\prime 2}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t<0
.
Suppose by contradiction that there exists
t_{0}\in(0,\infty)
such that
V(t_{0})\geq 0
.
Since
\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}V(t)=0
, we may assume that
V
attains its maximum value at
t_{0}\in(0,\infty)
, which implies
V^{\prime\prime}(t_{0})\leq 0
.
Then by (
C.1
), we have
U^{\prime}(t_{0})=V^{\prime\prime}(t_{0})+f^{\prime}(U(t_{0}))V(t_{0})\leq 0
, which contradicts to
Proposition

B.1
(b).
This shows that
V
is negative on
[0,\infty)
.
Since
V^{\prime}(0)<0
and
\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}V(t)=0
, there exists
t^{*}\in(0,\infty)
such that
V
attains its minimum value at
t^{*}
and
V^{\prime}(t^{*})=0
.
Suppose by contradiction that there exists another minimum point
t_{1}
such that
V^{\prime}(t_{1})=0
.
Then by a similar fashion of (b), we can use (
C.9
) to get a contradiction because
U^{\prime}(t)>0
,
f(U(t))>0
and
f^{\prime}(U(t))<0
(cf.
Proposition

B.1
(b) and (A1)–(A2)).
Hence there exists a unique
t^{*}\in(0,\infty)
such that
V
attains its minimum value, which implis (b).
Therefore, we complete the proof of
Proposition

C.1
.
∎

Figure 5
:
We sketch the numerical profile for the solution
V
to (
C.1
)–(
C.3
) for the case (a)
\Phi_{bd}=1
and the case (b)
\Phi_{bd}=-1
, which is consistent with
Proposition

C.1
.
Here
f(\phi)=\exp(\phi)-\exp(-\phi)
and
\gamma=0.1
.

Proposition C.2
.

Let
U
and
V
be the unique solutions to (
B.1
)–(
B.3
) and (
C.1
)–(
C.3
), respectively.

If
\Phi_{bd}\neq\phi^{*}
, then function
g:=f(U)V+U^{\prime}V^{\prime}
is negative on
[0,\infty)
.

Proof.

By (
B.1
), (
C.1
) and
Proposition

B.1
, we have

g^{\prime}(t)=f^{\prime}(U(t))U^{\prime}(t)V(t)+f(U(t))V^{\prime}(t)+U^{\prime\prime}(t)V^{\prime}(t)+U^{\prime}(t)V^{\prime\prime}(t)=U^{\prime 2}(t)>0

for
t\geq 0
and
\Phi_{bd}\neq\phi^{*}
, which implies
g
is strictly increasing on
[0,\infty)
. By (A2), (
B.3
), (
C.3
) and
6
, it is clear that
\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}g(t)=0
. Hence
g(t)<0
for
t\geq 0
, and the proof of
Proposition

C.2
is complete.
∎

Appendix D
Properties of the solution to (
1.25
)–(
1.27
)

In this appendix, we establish the existence, uniqueness, and qualitative properties for solutions to (
1.25
)–(
1.27
).

\displaystyle w_{k}^{\prime\prime}+f_{0}^{\prime}(u_{k})w_{k}=-f_{1}(u_{k})\quad\text{in}~(0,\infty),

(D.1)

\displaystyle w_{k}(0)-\gamma_{k}w_{k}^{\prime}(0)=0,

(D.2)

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}w_{k}(t)=Q,

(D.3)

where
u_{k}
are the solutions to equations (
1.19
)–(
1.21
), and
\gamma_{k}\geq 0
are given fixed constants for
k=0,1,\dots,K
.
Here
f_{0}
and
f_{1}
are smooth functions defined by

\displaystyle f_{0}(\phi)=\frac{1}{|\Omega|}\sum_{i=1}^{I}m_{i}z_{i}\exp(-z_{i}(\phi-\phi_{0}^{*}))\quad\text{and}\quad f_{1}(\phi)=-Qf_{0}^{\prime}(\phi)+\hat{f}_{1}(\phi)\quad\text{for}~\phi\in\mathbb{R},

(D.4)

where
Q\in\mathbb{R}
(defined in (
1.30
)),
m_{i}>0
and

\displaystyle\hat{f}_{1}(\phi)=\frac{1}{|\Omega|}\sum_{i=1}^{I}\hat{m}_{i}z_{i}\exp(-z_{i}(\phi-\phi_{0}^{*}))\quad\text{for}~\phi\in\mathbb{R},

(D.5)

\displaystyle\hat{m}_{i}=\frac{m_{i}}{|\Omega|}\sum_{k=0}^{K}|\partial\Omega_{k}|\int_{0}^{\infty}\![1-\exp(-z_{i}(u_{k}(s)-\phi_{0}^{*}))]\,\mathrm{d}s\quad\text{for}~i=1,\dots,I,

(D.6)

In addition,
\phi_{0}^{*}
and
u_{k}(0)
satisfy (cf. (
3.26
)–(
3.27
))

\displaystyle\phi_{bd,k}-u_{k}(0)=\operatorname{sgn}(\phi_{bd,k}-\phi_{0}^{*})\gamma_{k}\sqrt{\frac{2}{|\Omega|}\sum_{i=1}^{I}m_{i}[\exp(-z_{i}(u_{k}(0)-\phi_{0}^{*}))-1]}\quad\text{for}~k=0,1,\dots,K,

(D.7)

\displaystyle\sum_{k=0}^{K}|\partial\Omega_{k}|u^{\prime}(0)=\sum_{k=0}^{K}|\partial\Omega_{k}|\frac{u_{k}(0)-\phi_{bd,k}}{\gamma_{k}}=0.

(D.8)

By standard ODE theory, the solution
w_{k}
to equations (
D.1
)–(
D.3
) can be represented as

\displaystyle w_{k}(t)=\frac{w_{k}(0)}{u_{k}^{\prime}(0)}u_{k}^{\prime}(t)+u_{k}^{\prime}(t)\int_{0}^{t}\!\frac{Qf_{0}(u_{k}(s))-\hat{F}_{1}(u_{k}(s))}{u_{k}^{\prime 2}(s)}\,\mathrm{d}s\quad\text{for}~t\geq 0,

(D.9)

where
w_{k}(0)
and
\hat{F}_{1}
are given by

\displaystyle w_{k}(0)=\frac{\gamma_{k}(Qf_{0}(u_{k}(0))-\hat{F}_{1}(u_{k}(0)))}{u_{k}^{\prime}(0)+\gamma_{k}f_{0}(u_{k}(0))},

(D.10)

\displaystyle\hat{F}_{1}(\phi)=\int_{\phi_{0}^{*}}^{\phi}\!\hat{f}_{1}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s=\frac{1}{|\Omega|}\sum_{i=1}^{I}\hat{m}_{i}[1-\exp(-z_{i}(\phi-\phi_{0}^{*}))]\quad\text{for}~\phi\in\mathbb{R}.

(D.11)

Note that (
D.10
) is well-defined because
u_{k}^{\prime}(0)+\gamma_{k}f_{0}(u_{k}(0))\neq 0
.
Clearly, (
D.9
) with (
D.10
)–(
D.11
) satisfies (
D.1
)–(
D.2
).
To verify that (
D.9
) satisfies (
D.3
), we need the following claim.

Claim 7
.

Let
u_{k}
be the solution to (
1.19
)–(
1.21
),
\hat{f}_{1}
and
\hat{F}_{1}
defined in (
D.5
) and (
D.11
), respectively, and
\phi_{0}^{*}
be the unique zero of
f_{0}
.
Then we have

(a)

\hat{f}_{1}(\phi_{0}^{*})=\dfrac{1}{|\Omega|}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{I}\hat{m}_{i}z_{i}=0
;

(b)

\displaystyle\left|\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\frac{Qf_{0}(u_{k}(s))-\hat{F}_{1}(u_{k}(s))}{u_{k}^{\prime 2}(s)}\,\mathrm{d}s\right|=\infty
if
Q\neq 0
.

Proof of
7
.

By (
D.4
) and
\sum_{i=1}^{I}m_{i}z_{i}=0
(cf. (
1.3
)), we have

\displaystyle\hat{f}_{1}(\phi_{0}^{*})

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\Omega|}\sum_{i=1}^{I}\hat{m}_{i}z_{i}=\frac{1}{|\Omega|}\sum_{i=1}^{I}m_{i}z_{i}\sum_{k=0}^{K}|\partial\Omega_{k}|\int_{0}^{\infty}\![1-\exp(-z_{i}(u_{k}(s)-\phi_{0}^{*}))]\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{|\Omega|}\sum_{k=0}^{K}|\partial\Omega_{k}|\int_{0}^{\infty}\!\left(\sum_{i=1}^{I}m_{i}z_{i}-\sum_{i=1}^{I}m_{i}z_{i}\exp(-z_{i}(u_{k}(s)-\phi_{0}^{*}))\right)\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{|\Omega|}\sum_{k=0}^{K}|\partial\Omega_{k}|\int_{0}^{\infty}\!f_{0}(u_{k}(s))\,\mathrm{d}s.

Along with (
1.19
)–(
1.20
), (
D.8
) and
Proposition

B.2
(a), we get

\hat{f}_{1}(\phi_{0}^{*})=\frac{1}{|\Omega|}\sum_{k=0}^{K}|\partial\Omega_{k}|\int_{0}^{\infty}\!u_{k}^{\prime\prime}(s)\,\mathrm{d}s=\frac{1}{|\Omega|}\sum_{k=0}^{K}|\partial\Omega_{k}|(-u_{k}^{\prime}(0))=\frac{1}{|\Omega|}\sum_{k=0}^{K}|\partial\Omega_{k}|\frac{\phi_{bd,k}-u_{k}(0)}{\gamma_{k}}=0.

which shows (a).
To prove (b), we use (
1.19
) and L’Hôpital’s rule to get

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{Qf_{0}(u_{k}(t))-\hat{F}_{1}(u_{k}(t))}{u_{k}^{\prime 2}(t)}

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{Qf_{0}^{\prime}(u_{k}(t))u_{k}^{\prime}(t)-\hat{f}_{1}(u_{k}(t))u_{k}^{\prime}(t)}{2u_{k}^{\prime}(t)u_{k}^{\prime\prime}(t)}

\displaystyle=\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{-Qf_{0}^{\prime}(u_{k}(t))+\hat{f}_{1}(u_{k}(t))}{2f_{0}(u_{k}(t))}.

By (
1.21
)–(
1.28
), (
3.17
),
Proposition

B.1
, we have
f_{0}(u_{k}(t))\neq 0
for
t\geq 0
and
\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}f_{0}(u_{k}(t))=0
.
By (
1.21
), (
3.17
), (a) and the condition
Q\neq 0
, we get

\lim_{t\to\infty}(-Qf_{0}^{\prime}(u_{k}(t))+\hat{f}_{1}(u_{k}(t)))=-Qf_{0}^{\prime}(\phi_{0}^{*})\neq 0.

Hence
\left|\int_{0}^{\infty}\!(u_{k}^{\prime}(s))^{-2}[Qf_{0}(u_{k}(s))-\hat{F}_{1}(u_{k}(s))]\,\mathrm{d}s\right|=\infty
, which gives (b) and completes the proof of
7
.
∎

Now we show that (
D.9
) satisfies (
D.3
) and obtain the existence of (
D.1
)–(
D.3
). For the case that
Q\neq 0
, we can use (
B.1
),
7
and L’Hôpital’s rule to get

\displaystyle\begin{aligned} \lim_{t\to\infty}w_{k}(t)&=\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{\displaystyle\frac{w_{k}(0)}{u_{k}^{\prime}(0)}+\int_{0}^{t}\!\frac{Qf_{0}(u_{k}(s))-\hat{F}_{1}(u_{k}(s))}{u_{k}^{\prime 2}(s)}\,\mathrm{d}s}{\displaystyle\frac{1}{u_{k}^{\prime}(t)}}\\
&=\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{\displaystyle\frac{Qf_{0}(u_{k}(t))-\hat{F}_{1}(u_{k}(t))}{u_{k}^{\prime 2}(t)}}{\displaystyle-\frac{u_{k}^{\prime\prime}(t)}{u_{k}^{\prime 2}(t)}}\\
&=\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{Qf_{0}(u_{k}(t))-\hat{F}_{1}(u_{k}(t))}{f_{0}(u_{k}(t))}=Q+\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{-\hat{f}_{1}(u_{k}(t))u_{k}^{\prime}(t)}{f_{0}^{\prime}(u_{k}(t))u_{k}^{\prime}(t)}\\
&=Q-\lim_{t\to\infty}\frac{\hat{f}_{1}(u_{k}(t))}{f_{0}^{\prime}(u_{k}(t))}=Q-\frac{\hat{f}_{1}(\phi_{0}^{*})}{f_{0}^{\prime}(\phi_{0}^{*})}=Q,\end{aligned}

(D.12)

which gives (
D.3
) for
Q\neq 0
.
This shows the existence of a solution (
D.1
)–(
D.3
) for
Q\neq 0
.
For the case of
Q=0
, we define
w_{k}^{0}
and
w_{k}^{\pm}
by (
D.9
) for
Q=0
and
Q=\pm 1
, respectively.
Then due to the linearity of (
D.9
) (with respect to
Q
),
w_{k}^{0}=(w_{k}^{+}+w_{k}^{-})/2
.
By (
D.12
) with
Q=\pm 1
, we find that
\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}w_{k}^{0}(t)=\lim\limits_{t\to\infty}(w_{k}^{+}(t)+w_{k}^{-}(t))/2=(1+(-1))/2=0
, which implies that
w_{k}^{0}
satisfies (
D.3
) for
Q=0
.
Therefore, we get the existence of the solution to (
D.1
)–(
D.3
) for
Q\in\mathbb{R}
.

It remains to prove the uniqueness of the solution to (
D.1
)–(
D.3
).
Suppose that there exist two solutions
w_{k}
and
\tilde{w}_{k}
of (
D.1
)–(
D.3
).
Let
\overline{w}_{k}=w_{k}-\tilde{w}_{k}
.
Then
\overline{w}_{k}
satisfies

\displaystyle\overline{w}_{k}^{\prime\prime}+f_{0}^{\prime}(u_{k})\overline{w}_{k}=0\quad\text{in}~(0,\infty),

\displaystyle\overline{w}_{k}(0)-\gamma_{k}\overline{w}_{k}^{\prime}(0)=0,

\displaystyle\lim_{t\to\infty}\overline{w}_{k}(t)=0.

By the standard maximum principle, it is easy to obtain
\overline{w}_{k}\equiv 0
on
[0,\infty)
, which gives the uniqueness of solution to (
D.1
)–(
D.3
).
